# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

<term>Theosophy</term> <note>Derived from the Greek words *theos* (god) and *sophia* (wisdom). It refers to a philosophical system—popularized in the late 19th century—that seeks to uncover the underlying "Divine Wisdom" or universal truths found across all religions and sciences.</note>

<term>Jesuit Seminary</term> <note>The Society of Jesus (Jesuits) is a religious order of the Catholic Church. A seminary is a school for training priests. The presence of this stamp suggests that this work on occult philosophy was, perhaps surprisingly, kept in a major Catholic library collection.</note>

# PREFACE.

The purpose of this book is exactly expressed in its title, "THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY," and needs only a few words of explanation. It is not a complete or exhaustive textbook of <term>Theosophy</term> <note>Theosophy: Literally "divine wisdom"; a philosophical system seeking to uncover the underlying spiritual truths of the universe.</note>, but only a key to unlock the door that leads to deeper study. It traces the broad outlines of the <term>Wisdom Religion</term> <note>Wisdom Religion: The idea that a single universal truth or "ancient wisdom" underlies all the world's religions and philosophies.</note> and explains its fundamental principles; it addresses, at the same time, the various objections raised by the average Western inquirer <note>original: "enquirer"</note>, and attempts to present unfamiliar concepts in a form as simple and in language as clear as possible. That it should succeed in making Theosophy understandable <note>original: "intelligible"</note> without mental effort on the part of the reader would be too much to expect; but it is hoped that the obscurity still remaining is in the thought, not the language, and is due to depth rather than confusion. To those who are mentally lazy or slow to understand <note>original: "obtuse"</note>, Theosophy must remain a riddle; for in the mental world, as in the spiritual world, each person must progress by their own efforts. The writer cannot do the reader's thinking for them, nor would the latter be any better off if such vicarious <note>vicarious: experienced through the actions or thoughts of another person rather than directly</note> thought were possible. The need for such an explanation <note>original: "exposition"</note> as the present has long been felt among those interested in the <term>Theosophical Society</term> <note>Theosophical Society: An organization founded in 1875 to advance the study of ancient truths and promote the universal brotherhood of humanity.</note> and its work, and it is hoped that it...

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces the book as an introductory guide to the fundamental principles of Theosophy, noting that while the language is clear, the reader must apply their own mental effort to understand the deep concepts presented.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Wisdom Religion, Theosophical Society, spiritual study, mental effort, introductory guide</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Preface, with the author reflecting on the conflict between Spiritualism and Theosophy and offering acknowledgments to those who helped with the book's development.</meta>

# PREFACE.

This book will provide information, as free as possible from technical jargon <note>original: "technicalities"</note>, to the many people whose interest has been sparked but who, so far, are merely puzzled and not yet convinced.

Great care has been taken to separate what is true from what is false in <term>Spiritualist</term> <note>Spiritualism: A movement popular in the 19th century based on the belief that the living can communicate with the spirits of the dead, often through mediums.</note> teachings regarding life after death <note>original: "post-mortem life"</note>, and to show the true nature of Spiritualist phenomena. Earlier explanations of this kind have brought a great deal of anger upon the author. Like so many others, Spiritualists often prefer to believe what is pleasant rather than what is true; they become very angry with anyone who destroys a comforting delusion. For the past year, <term>Theosophy</term> has been the target of every "poisoned arrow" from the Spiritualist camp, as if those who possess a partial truth feel more hostility toward those who possess the whole truth than toward those who have no spiritual knowledge at all.

The author owes very hearty thanks to the many Theosophists who have sent suggestions and questions, or have otherwise provided help during the writing of this book. This work will be all the more useful because of their assistance, and that will be their greatest reward.

->H. P. B.<-
<note>H. P. B.: Helena Petrovna Blavatsky (1831–1891), the primary founder of the Theosophical Society.</note>

<summary>In the conclusion of the Preface, Blavatsky explains her intent to clarify the differences between Theosophy and Spiritualism and thanks her fellow Theosophists for their contributions to the text.</summary>
<keywords>Spiritualism, post-mortem life, phenomena, Theosophy, H. P. Blavatsky, Spiritualist vs Theosophist conflict</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the main text, transitioning from the introductory Preface to the first chapter, which defines the fundamental terms and origins of Theosophy.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

---

## I.

### THEOSOPHY AND THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

#### *THE MEANING OF THE NAME.*

ENQUIRER. Theosophy and its doctrines are often referred to as a newly invented religion. Is it a religion?

THEOSOPHIST. It is not. Theosophy is Divine Knowledge or Science.

ENQ. What is the real meaning of the term?

THEO. It means “Divine Wisdom,” <note>original Greek: *Theosophia*</note> or the Wisdom of the gods, similar to how “theogony” means the genealogy or origin of the gods <note>original Greek: *theogonia*</note>. In Greek, the word for “god” <note>original Greek: *Theos*</note> refers to a divine being, and certainly not to “God” in the sense we use the word today. Therefore, it does not mean “Wisdom of God,” as some have translated it, but rather the Divine Wisdom such as that possessed by the gods. The term is many thousands of years old.

ENQ. What is the origin of the name?

THEO. It comes to us from the <term>Alexandrian philosophers</term> <note>A group of thinkers in Alexandria, Egypt, around the 2nd to 3rd century AD, who sought to reconcile different systems of philosophy and religion.</note>, who were called “lovers of truth” or <term>Philaletheians</term> <note>From the Greek words *phil* (loving) and *aletheia* (truth).</note>. The name Theosophy dates from

<summary>In this opening dialogue, the author clarifies that Theosophy is not a new religion but an ancient "Divine Wisdom" whose name originates from the Alexandrian philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Theosophical Society, Philaletheians, Alexandrian philosophers, Divine Wisdom, etymology, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical origins of Theosophy, specifically focusing on the 3rd-century Alexandrian school and the core principles of the Eclectic Theosophical system.</meta>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

the third century of our era, and began with Ammonius Saccas and his disciples,\* who started the Eclectic Theosophical system.

**ENQUIRER.** What was the goal of this system?

**THEOSOPHIST.** First of all, its purpose was to instill certain great moral truths into its disciples and all those who were “lovers of the truth.” This led to the motto adopted by the Theosophical Society: “There is no religion higher than truth.”\† The primary goal of the Founders

---

\* Also called Analogeticists <note>Those who interpret by analogy.</note>. As explained by Professor Alexander Wilder (a Fellow of the Theosophical Society) in his *Eclectic Philosophy*, they were called this because of their practice of interpreting all sacred legends, narratives, myths, and mysteries using a rule or principle of analogy and correspondence. In this way, events described as occurring in the external world were seen as representing the processes and experiences of the human soul. They were also called Neo-Platonists. Although Theosophy, or the Eclectic Theosophical system, is generally dated to the third century, if Diogenes Laertius is to be believed, its origin is much earlier. He attributed the system to an Egyptian priest named Pot-Amun, who lived during the early years of the Ptolemaic dynasty. The same author tells us that the name is Coptic and means "one consecrated to Amun, the God of Wisdom." Theosophy is the equivalent of *Brahma-Vidya* <note>original Sanskrit: *Brahm-Vidya*</note>, or divine knowledge.

† Eclectic Theosophy was divided into three main categories: (1) Belief in one absolute, incomprehensible, and supreme Deity, or infinite essence, which is the root of all nature and everything that exists, both visible and invisible. (2) Belief in the eternal, immortal nature of humanity because, being a radiation of the Universal Soul, it shares the same essence. (3) *Theurgy*, meaning “divine work” or “producing the work of gods” <note>original Greek: *theoï* (gods) and *ergeïn* (to work)</note>. This term is very old, but because it belongs to the vocabulary of the <term>MYSTERIES</term> <note>Sacred rites and teachings of ancient civilizations reserved for initiated members.</note>, it was not commonly used. It was a mystical belief—verified in practice by initiated adepts and priests—that by making oneself as pure as non-physical beings (that is, by returning to one’s original purity of nature), a person could prompt the gods to reveal Divine mysteries and even cause them to become visible at times, whether through internal vision or external appearance. It was the transcendental aspect of what is now called Spiritualism; but having been abused and misunderstood by the public, it had come to be

<summary>This page explains the historical roots of the Eclectic Theosophical system under Ammonius Saccas and defines its three core pillars: belief in a supreme deity, the immortality of the soul, and the practice of theurgy.</summary>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

of the Eclectic Theosophical School was one of the three objects of its modern successor, the Theosophical Society: namely, to reconcile all religions, sects, and nations under a common system of ethics based on eternal truths <note>original: "verities"</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** What evidence can you provide to show that this is not an impossible dream?

---

...which was regarded by some as necromancy <note>the practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead</note> and was generally forbidden. A distorted <note>original: "travestied"</note> practice of the theurgy <note>divine work or magic performed with the help of beneficent spirits</note> of Iamblichus still lingers in the ceremonial magic of some modern Kabalists. Modern Theosophy avoids and rejects both these types of magic and "necromancy" as being very dangerous. True divine theurgy requires an almost superhuman purity and holiness of life; otherwise, it degrades into mediumship or black magic. The immediate disciples of Ammonius Saccas—who was called *Theodidaktos* <note>original Greek: "god-taught"</note>—such as Plotinus and his follower Porphyry, rejected theurgy at first. However, they were finally reconciled to it through Iamblichus, who wrote a work on the subject titled *On the Mysteries* <note>original Latin: "De Mysteriis"</note> under the name of his own master, a famous Egyptian priest called Abammon. 

Ammonius Saccas was the son of Christian parents. Having been repelled by dogmatic, spiritualistic Christianity since his childhood, he became a Neo-Platonist. Like Jakob Böhme and other great seers and mystics, it is said that divine wisdom was revealed to him in dreams and visions—hence his name, *Theodidaktos*. He was determined to reconcile every system of religion and, by demonstrating their identical origin, to establish one universal creed based on ethics. His life was so blameless and pure, and his learning so profound and vast, that several Fathers of the Church were his secret disciples. Clement of Alexandria <note>original: "Clemens Alexandrinus"</note> speaks very highly of him. 

Plotinus, who was like the "St. John" to Ammonius, was also a man universally respected and esteemed, possessing the most profound learning and integrity. At thirty-nine years of age, he accompanied the Roman Emperor Gordian III and his army to the East to receive instruction from the sages of Bactria <note>an ancient region in Central Asia, modern-day Afghanistan/Uzbekistan</note> and India. He later established a School of Philosophy in Rome. Porphyry, his disciple, whose real name was Malek (a Jew whose name was adapted into Greek), collected all the writings of his master. Porphyry was himself a distinguished author and provided an allegorical interpretation of parts of Homer’s writings. 

The system of meditation the Philaletheians <note>original Greek: "lovers of truth"</note> resorted to was "ecstasy," a system similar to Indian Yoga practice. Most of what is known about the Eclectic School is due to Origen, Longinus, and Plotinus, the direct disciples of Ammonius. (See <note>original Latin: *vide*</note> *Eclectic Philosophy* by Alexander Wilder.)

<term>THEOSOPHIST.</term> Their comparative study and analysis. The "Wisdom-religion" was a single, unified system in ancient times. The consistency of primitive religious philosophy is proven to us by the identical doctrines taught to the Initiates during the <term>MYSTERIES</term> <note>secret religious rites and teachings of the ancient world, accessible only to those formally initiated</note>, an institution that was once found all over the world. "All the old forms of worship indicate the existence of a single Theosophy that existed before them. The key that is meant to open one must open all; otherwise, it cannot be the right key." (from *Eclectic Philosophy*)

### THE POLICY OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

<term>ENQUIRER.</term> In the days of Ammonius, there were several ancient great religions, and there were numerous sects in Egypt and Palestine alone. How could he reconcile them?

<term>THEOSOPHIST.</term> By doing exactly what we are trying to do again now. The Neo-Platonists were a large group and belonged to various religious philosophies*; our Theosophists do the same. In those days, the Jewish scholar Aristobulus affirmed that the ethics of Aristotle represented—

---

* It was under the reign of Ptolemy II Philadelphus <note>King of Ptolemaic Egypt, 283–246 BCE</note> that Judaism established itself in Alexandria, and immediately the Greek <note>original: "Hellenic"</note> teachers became the dangerous rivals of the College of Rabbis of Babylon. As the author of *Eclectic Philosophy* very correctly <note>original: "pertinently"</note> remarks: "The Buddhist, Vedantic, and Magian <note>relating to the Magi, or ancient Persian Zoroastrianism</note> systems were explained alongside the philosophies of Greece at that period. It was not surprising that thoughtful men thought that the 'war of words' should stop, and considered it possible to extract one harmonious system from these various teachings. . . . Panænus, Athenagoras, and Clement were thoroughly educated in Platonic philosophy and understood its essential unity with the Eastern <note>original: "Oriental"</note> systems."

It is the same in our own day. We can show the lineage of every Christian religion, as well as every sect, even the smallest. These sects are like minor twigs or shoots growing on larger branches, but both shoots and branches spring from the same trunk—the WISDOM-RELIGION. To prove this was the goal of Ammonius, who tried to persuade Gentiles and Christians, Jews and pagans <note>original: "Idolators"</note>, to set aside their arguments and conflicts. He wanted them to remember only that they all possessed the same truth under different outward forms <note>original: "vestments"</note> and were all the children of a common mother.* This is also the goal of Theosophy.

**ENQUIRER.** What evidence or authorities do you have for saying this about the ancient Theosophists of Alexandria?

***

* Mosheim <note>Johann Lorenz von Mosheim (1693–1755), a prominent German church historian</note> says of Ammonius: "Believing that not only the philosophers of Greece, but also those of the various non-Greek <note>original: "barbarian"</note> nations, were in perfect agreement regarding every essential point, he made it his work to explain the numerous tenets of all these various sects. He aimed to show they had all originated from one and the same source, and were all directed toward one and the same goal." If the author writing about Ammonius in the *Edinburgh Encyclopedia* knows what he is talking about, then he is describing modern Theosophists, their beliefs, and their work. Speaking of the *God-taught* <note>original: "Theodidaktos"</note>, he says: "He adopted the doctrines received in Egypt—the inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> teachings were those of India—concerning the Universe and the Deity, viewed as forming one great whole; concerning the eternity of the world... and established a system of moral discipline. This allowed the general public to live according to the laws of their country and the impulses <note>original: "dictates"</note> of nature, but required the wise to elevate their minds through meditation <note>original: "contemplation"</note>."

**THEO.** An almost countless number of well-known writers agree. Mosheim <note>Johann Lorenz von Mosheim (1693–1755), a famous German historian of Christianity</note>, one of them, says that:

> “Ammonius taught that the religion of the common people <note>original: "multitude"</note> went hand-in-hand with philosophy. Along with philosophy, religion had shared the fate of being gradually corrupted and obscured by mere human inventions <note>original: "conceits"</note>, superstitions, and lies. He believed it should, therefore, be brought back to its original purity by stripping away this waste <note>original: "dross"</note> and explaining it according to philosophical principles. His view was that Christ’s whole purpose was to reinstate and restore the wisdom of the ancients to its original integrity, to limit the universally prevailing power of superstition, and to partly correct and partly eliminate the various errors that had found their way into different popular religions.”

This, again, is precisely what modern Theosophists say. However, while the great Philaletheian <note>meaning "lover of truth," a title given to Ammonius Saccas</note> was supported and helped in his approach by two Church Fathers, Clement and Athenagoras, and by all the learned Rabbis of the Synagogue, the Academy, and the Groves <note>referring to the famous schools of philosophy in Athens</note>, we receive no recognition. Although he taught a doctrine common to all, we, his followers on the same path, are instead abused and persecuted. This shows that people 1,500 years ago were more tolerant than they are in this "enlightened" century.

**ENQ.** Was he encouraged and supported by the Church because, despite his heresies, Ammonius taught Christianity and was a Christian?

**THEO.** Not at all. He was born a Christian, but never accepted Church Christianity. As the same writer said of him:

> “He only had to present <note>original: "propound"</note> his teachings according to the ancient pillars of Hermes <note>legendary Egyptian stone pillars inscribed with ancient wisdom</note>, which Plato and Pythagoras knew before him and used to build their philosophy. Finding the same truths in the prologue of the Gospel of Saint John, he quite rightly supposed that the

### THE WISDOM-RELIGION IS ESOTERIC IN ALL AGES.

**ENQUIRER:** Since Ammonius never committed anything to writing, how can one feel sure that these were his teachings?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Neither did Buddha, Pythagoras, Confucius, Orpheus, Socrates, or even Jesus leave behind any writings. Yet most of these are historical figures, and their teachings have all survived. The disciples of Ammonius (among whom were Origen and Herennius) wrote treatises and explained his ethics. Certainly, these records are as historical, if not more so, than the Apostolic writings. Moreover, his pupils—Origen, Plotinus, and Longinus (the counselor to the famous Queen Zenobia)—all left voluminous records of the Philaletheian <note>meaning "lover of truth"</note> system—at least as far as their public profession of faith was known, for the school was divided into <term>exoteric</term> (public) and <term>esoteric</term> (hidden or secret) teachings.

**ENQUIRER:** How have these secret <note>original: "esoteric"</note> tenets reached our day, since you maintain that what is properly called the WISDOM-RELIGION was secret?

**THEOSOPHIST:** The WISDOM-RELIGION was always one and the same, and being the final word of possible human knowledge, it was therefore carefully preserved. It preceded the Alexandrian Theosophists by long ages...

sophists, reached the modern age, and will survive every other religion and philosophy.

**ENQUIRER:** Where and by whom was it preserved?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Among Initiates <note>those admitted into secret religious knowledge</note> of every country; among profound seekers after truth—their disciples—and in those parts of the world where such topics have always been most valued and pursued: in India, Central Asia, and Persia.

**ENQUIRER:** Can you give me some proofs of its secret nature <note>original: "esotericism"</note>?

**THEOSOPHIST:** The best proof you can have is the fact that every ancient religious—or rather, philosophical—cult consisted of an **esoteric** (secret) teaching and an **exoteric** (outward, public) worship. Furthermore, it is a well-known fact that the **MYSTERIES** of the ancients in every nation included both the "greater" (secret) and "lesser" (public) **MYSTERIES**—for example, in the celebrated ceremonies called the *Eleusinia* <note>ancient Greek festivals and rites held for the goddesses Demeter and Persephone</note> in Greece. From the high priests <term>Hierophants</term> of Samothrace and Egypt, and the initiated Brahmins <note>the highest priestly caste in Hindu society</note> of ancient India, down to the later Hebrew Rabbis, all kept their true, authentic <note>original Latin: *bonâ fide*</note> beliefs secret for fear of them being dishonored. The Jewish Rabbis called their public <note>original: "secular"</note> religious series the *Mercavah* <note>Hebrew for "Chariot"; refers to the external structure of the teaching</note> (the exterior body), "the vehicle," or the covering which contains the hidden soul—that is, their highest secret knowledge. No ancient nation ever shared its real philosophical secrets with the masses through its priests; instead, they gave the public only the outer shells <note>original: "husks"</note>. Northern Buddhism has its "greater" and "lesser" vehicles, known as the *Mahayana* <note>Sanskrit for "Great Vehicle"</note>, the secret school, and the *Hinayana* <note>Sanskrit for "Lesser Vehicle"</note>, the public school. You cannot blame them for such secrecy; for surely you would not think of feeding your flock of sheep—

Pythagoras called his *Knowledge* <note>original: *Gnosis*</note> "the knowledge of things that are," <note>original Greek: *ἡ γνῶσις τῶν ὄντων* (hē gnōsis tōn ontōn)</note> and preserved that knowledge for his pledged disciples only: for those who could digest such mental food and feel satisfied; and he pledged them to silence and secrecy. Secret alphabets and ciphers are the development of the old Egyptian <term>hieratic</term> <note>a cursive writing system used by ancient Egyptian priests</note> writings, the secret of which was, in the days of old, in the possession only of the <term>Hierogrammatists</term> <note>sacred scribes or initiated Egyptian priests</note>. Ammonius Saccas <note>a Greek philosopher often considered the founder of Neoplatonism</note>, as his biographers tell us, bound his pupils by oath not to divulge *his higher doctrines* except to those who had already been instructed in preliminary knowledge, and who were also bound by a pledge. Finally, do we not find the same even in early Christianity, among the Gnostics, and even in the teachings of Christ? Did he not speak to the crowds in parables which had a two-fold meaning, and explain his reasons only to his disciples? "To you," he says, "it is given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven; but unto those who are on the outside, all these things are done in parables" (Mark 4:11). "The Essenes <note>a Jewish sect known for their asceticism and communal living</note> of Judea and Carmel made similar distinctions, dividing their adherents into neophytes <note>beginners</note>, brethren, and the perfect, or those initiated" (from *The Eclectic Philosophy*). Examples might be brought from every country to this effect.

Is this so?

Had Noah Webster <note>original: "Webster"; referring to the famous lexicographer</note> said “by metaphysical and alchemical processes,” the definition would be approximately correct; as it is, it is absurd. Ancient Theosophists claimed, and so do the modern, that the infinite cannot be known by the finite—that is, sensed by the finite Self—but that the divine essence could be communicated to the higher Spiritual Self in a state of ecstasy. This condition can hardly be attained, like hypnotism, by “physical and chemical means.”

ENQUIRER: What is your explanation of it?

THEOSOPHIST: Real ecstasy was defined by Plotinus <note>a major Neoplatonist philosopher of the 3rd century</note> as “the liberation of the mind from its finite consciousness, becoming one and identified with the infinite.” This is the highest condition, says Professor Alexander Wilder <note>original: "Prof. Wilder"; a 19th-century scholar and physician known for his interest in Neoplatonism</note>, but not one of permanent duration, and it is reached only by the very, *very* few. It is, indeed, identical with that state which is known in India as *Samadhi* <term>Samadhi</term> <note>the highest state of mental concentration and spiritual union in Yoga</note>. The latter is practiced by the Yogis, who facilitate it physically by the greatest abstinence in food and drink, and mentally by an incessant endeavor to purify and elevate the mind. Meditation is silent and unuttered prayer, or, as Plato expressed it, “the ardent turning of the soul toward the divine; not to ask for any particular good (as in the common meaning of prayer), but for good itself—for the universal Supreme Good” of which we are a part on earth, and out of the essence of which we have all emerged. Therefore, adds Plato, “remain silent in the presence of the *divine ones*, until they remove the clouds from your eyes and enable you to see by the light which issues from

**ENQUIRER:** Theosophy, then, is not a newly invented scheme, as some people believe?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Only ignorant people would refer to it that way. In its teachings and ethics, it is as old as the world—even if the name itself is not. It is also the broadest and most universal <note>original: "catholic"; here meaning all-embracing or universal rather than referring to the Roman Catholic Church</note> system among them all.

**ENQUIRER:** How does it happen, then, that Theosophy has remained so unknown to the nations of the Western Hemisphere? Why should it have been a closed book <note>original: "sealed book"</note> to races that are admittedly the most cultured and advanced?

**THEOSOPHIST:** We believe there were nations just as cultured in ancient days, and certainly more spiritually “advanced” than we are. But there are several reasons for this willing ignorance. One of them was given by St. Paul to the cultured Athenians—a loss, over long centuries, of real spiritual insight, and even interest, owing to their too great devotion to physical things <note>original: "things of sense"</note> and their...

<margin>]</margin>
This is what the scholarly author of “The Eclectic Philosophy,” Prof. Alexander Wilder <note>original: "Prof. A. Beyond our everyday world of limits, all is one single day or state—the past and future are comprised within the present.” . . . Death is the last *ecstasy* <term>ecstasis</term> <note>original: "ecstasis"; a state of being "beside oneself" or in a trance where the soul leaves the body</note> on earth. Then the soul is freed from the constraint of the body, and its nobler part is united to a higher nature and becomes a sharer in the wisdom and foreknowledge of the higher beings.” Real Theosophy is, for the mystics, that state which Apollonius of Tyana <note>a famous Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker of the 1st century AD</note> was made to describe this way: “I can see the present and the future as in a clear mirror. The sage need not wait for the vapors of the earth and the corruption of the air to foresee events. . . . The *gods* <note>original: "theoi"</note> see the future; common men the present; sages that which is about to take place.” “The Theosophy of the Sages” he speaks of is well expressed in the assertion, “The Kingdom of God is within us.”

<meta>This page continues the explanation of why Theosophy remained hidden from the Western world, moving from the loss of spiritual insight to the intentional secrecy maintained by its guardians.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

...long slavery to the literal interpretation <note>original: "dead letter"</note> of dogma and ritualism. But the strongest reason for this ignorance lies in the fact that real Theosophy has always been kept secret.

**ENQUIRER:** You have brought forward proof that such secrecy has existed; but what was the real cause for it?

**THEOSOPHIST:** The causes for it were: *Firstly*, the perversity of average human nature and its selfishness, which always tends toward fulfilling personal desires at the expense <note>original: "detriment"</note> of neighbors and kin. Such people could never be entrusted with divine secrets. *Secondly*, their inability to be trusted to keep sacred and divine knowledge from being corrupted <note>original: "desecration"</note>. It is this latter reason that led to the perversion of the most sublime truths and symbols, and to the gradual transformation of spiritual things into human-like <note>original: "anthropomorphic"</note>, concrete, and physical <note>original: "gross"</note> imagery—in other words, to the diminishing <note>original: "dwarfing"</note> of the concept of God and to the practice of idolatry.

### THEOSOPHY IS NOT BUDDHISM.

**ENQUIRER:** You are often spoken of as "Esoteric Buddhists." Are you all, then, followers of Gautama Buddha?

**THEOSOPHIST:** No more than all musicians are followers of Wagner. Some of us are Buddhists by religion; yet there are far more Hindus and Brahmins than Buddhists among us, and more Christian-born Europeans and Americans than converted Buddhists. The mistake has arisen from a misunderstanding of the real meaning of the title of Mr. Sinnett’s <note>Alfred Percy Sinnett (1840–1921), an English author and Theosophist</note> excellent work, *Esoteric Buddhism*. That last word ought to have been spelled *with one "d" instead of two; if it had been, "Budhism" would have meant what it actually signifies...*

<summary>The author explains that Theosophy was kept secret to prevent the selfish misuse or corruption of divine knowledge into idolatry, and clarifies that Theosophy is a universal philosophy rather than a branch of the Buddhist religion.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

[The title of Sinnett's book] was intended to mean simply "Wisdom-ism" (from *Bodha* or *bodhi*, meaning "intelligence" or "wisdom") instead of *Buddhism*, which refers to the religious philosophy of Gautama Buddha. Theosophy, as already said, is the WISDOM-RELIGION.

<term>ENQUIRER:</term> What is the difference between Buddhism, the religion founded by the Prince of Kapilavastu <note>The birthplace of Gautama Buddha</note>, and *Budhism*, the "Wisdom-ism" which you say is synonymous with Theosophy?

<term>THEOSOPHIST:</term> It is exactly the same difference that exists between the secret teachings of Christ—which are called "the mysteries of the Kingdom of Heaven"—and the later rituals and dogmatic theology of the various churches and sects. *Buddha* means "the Enlightened One" through *Bodha* <note>understanding or wisdom</note>. This wisdom was fully integrated into the secret <note>original: "esoteric"</note> teachings that Gautama shared only with his chosen <term>Arhats</term> <note>Advanced disciples or saints who have reached a high state of spiritual realization</note>.

<term>ENQUIRER:</term> But some scholars of Eastern cultures <note>original: "Orientalists"</note> deny that Buddha ever taught any secret <note>original: "esoteric"</note> doctrine at all?

<term>THEOSOPHIST:</term> They might as well deny that Nature has any hidden secrets from scientists. Later on, I will prove this by referencing Buddha’s conversation with his disciple Ananda. His secret teachings were simply the <term>Gupta Vidya</term> <note>original: "Gupta Vidya" – Sanskrit for secret knowledge</note> of the ancient Brahmins; the key to this knowledge has been completely lost by their modern successors, with very few exceptions. This <term>Vidya</term> <note>knowledge</note> has become what is now known as the *inner* teachings of the <term>Mahayana</term> <note>original: "Mahayana" – "The Great Vehicle," one of the primary branches of Buddhism common in North Asia</note> school of Northern Buddhism. Those who deny this are merely ignorant people pretending to be experts in Eastern studies. I advise you to read the Reverend Joseph Edkins’ *Chinese Buddhism*—especially the chapters on the public <note>original: "exoteric"</note> and secret schools.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

...and teachings—and then compare the testimony of the whole ancient world upon the subject.

<term>ENQUIRER:</term> But are not the ethics of Theosophy identical with those taught by Buddha?

<term>THEOSOPHIST:</term> Certainly, because these ethics are the soul of the <term>Wisdom-Religion</term> <note>The underlying universal truth that Theosophists believe precedes all formal religions</note>, and were once the common property of the initiates of all nations. But Buddha was the first to embody these lofty ethics in his public teachings, and to make them the foundation and the very essence of his public system. It is here that the immense difference lies between *exoteric* <note>publicly taught; intended for the general population</note> *Buddhism* and every other religion. For while in other religions ritualism and dogma hold the first and most important place, in Buddhism it is the ethics which have always been the most insisted upon. This accounts for the resemblance, amounting almost to identity, between the ethics of Theosophy and those of the religion of Buddha.

<term>ENQUIRER:</term> Are there any great points of difference?

<term>THEOSOPHIST:</term> One great distinction between Theosophy and *exoteric Buddhism* is that the latter—represented by the Southern Church <note>Referring to the Theravada tradition found in Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia</note>—entirely denies (a) the existence of any Deity, and (b) any conscious life after death <note>original: "post-mortem"</note>, or even any *self-conscious* surviving individuality in man. Such at least is the teaching of the Siamese <note>Thai</note> sect, now considered as the *purest* form of exoteric Buddhism. And it is so, if we refer only to Buddha's public teachings; the reason for such silence <note>original: "reticence"</note> on his part I will give further on. But the schools of the Northern Buddhist Church <note>Referring to the Mahayana tradition found in Tibet, China, and Japan</note>, established in those countries to which his initiated <gloss>Arhats</gloss> <note>original: "Arinhats"; advanced disciples who have attained spiritual enlightenment</note> retired after the Master's death, teach all that is now...

<summary>The author explains that while Theosophy and Buddhism share identical ethics, public (exoteric) Buddhism differs from Theosophy by denying a personal deity and a conscious individual afterlife, though these concepts are preserved in the "Northern" secret traditions.</summary>

# II.

## EXOTERIC AND ESOTERIC THEOSOPHY.
<note>Exoteric refers to knowledge intended for the general public, while Esoteric refers to secret or inner knowledge intended only for a small group of initiates.</note>

### *WHAT THE MODERN THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY IS NOT.*

**ENQUIRER:** Your doctrines, then, are not a revival of <term>Buddhism</term>, nor are they entirely copied from <term>Neoplatonic Theosophy</term>? <note>A school of mystical philosophy that arose in the 3rd century AD, blending the teachings of Plato with Oriental mysticism.</note>

**THEOSOPHIST:** They are not. But I cannot give you a better answer to these questions than by quoting from a paper on “Theosophy” read by Dr. J. D. Buck, F.T.S. <note>Dr. Jirah Dewey Buck (1838–1916) was a prominent American physician and Theosophist. F.T.S. stands for "Fellow of the Theosophical Society."</note>, before the last <term>Theosophical Convention</term> in Chicago, America (April 1889). No living Theosophist has better expressed and understood the real essence of <term>Theosophy</term> than our honored friend Dr. Buck:

> “The <term>Theosophical Society</term> was organized for the purpose of spreading <term>Theosophical</term> doctrines and for the promotion of the <term>Theosophic</term> life. The present <term>Theosophical Society</term> is not the first of its kind. I have a volume entitled *<term>Theosophical Transactions of the Philadelphian Society</term>*, published in London in 1697 <note>The Philadelphian Society was an English group of Christian mystics influenced by the writings of Jakob Böhme.</note>; and another with the following title: *Introduction to <term>Theosophy</term>, or the <term>Science of the Mystery of Christ</term>*. That is, a science of God, Nature, and Created Beings <note>original: "Creature"</note>, embracing the philosophy of all the working powers of life, both magical and spiritual. It forms a practical guide to the most sublime purity, holiness <note>original: "sanctity"</note>, and evangelical perfection; it also leads to the attainment of divine vision and the..."

<summary>The author clarifies that modern Theosophy is not merely a revival of Buddhism or Neoplatonism, citing Dr. J. D. Buck to show that "Theosophical" societies and literature existed in England as early as the 17th century.</summary>

“In the following year (1856) another volume was issued, in a large book format <term>royal octavo</term> <note>A standard book size, approximately 6 by 10 inches.</note>, of 600 pages, printed in very small type <term>diamond type</term> <note>A very small size of printing type, about 4.5 points.</note>, titled ‘Theosophical Miscellanies.’ Of the last-named work, only 500 copies were issued for free <note>original: "gratuitous"</note> distribution to libraries and universities. These earlier movements, of which there were many, originated within the Church, among persons of great piety, earnestness, and unblemished character. All of these writings were in an orthodox form, using Christian expressions, and, like the writings of the distinguished <note>original: "eminent"</note> Churchman William Law <note>William Law (1686–1761) was an English priest whose mystical writings influenced the early Methodist movement.</note>, would only be recognized by the ordinary reader for their great earnestness and piety. These were one and all nothing but attempts to derive and explain the deeper meanings and original significance <note>original: "import"</note> of the Christian Scriptures, and to illustrate and unfold the Theosophic life. 

These works were soon forgotten and are now generally unknown. They sought to reform the clergy and revive genuine piety, but they were never welcomed. That one word, ‘Heresy,’ was sufficient to bury them in the limbo of all such Utopias. At the time of the Reformation, John Reuchlin <note>Johann Reuchlin (1455–1522) was a German humanist and scholar of Greek and Hebrew who attempted to integrate Kabbalah into Christian thought.</note> made a similar attempt with the same result, even though he was the intimate and trusted friend of Martin Luther. Orthodoxy never desired to be informed or enlightened. These reformers were told—just as the Apostle Paul was told by the Roman governor Festus <note>A reference to Acts 26:24, where Festus tells Paul, "Much learning doth make thee mad."</note>—that too much learning had made them mad, and that it would be dangerous to go any further. Coming to the core of the matter, these writings were Theosophical in the strictest sense, and relate solely to humanity's knowledge of its own nature and the higher life of the soul. The current Theosophical movement has sometimes been described as an attempt to convert the Christian world <note>original: "Christendom"</note> to Buddhism; this simply shows that the word "heresy" has lost its ability to terrify and has given up its power.

In every age, individuals have understood Theosophical doctrines more or less clearly and woven <note>original: "wrought"</note> them into the fabric of their lives. These doctrines do not belong to any single religion, and they are not confined to any specific society or time. They are the birthright of every human soul. A sense of "orthodoxy" <note>In this context, a personal standard of correct belief or "right thinking."</note> must be developed by each individual according to their own nature, needs, and varying experiences. This may explain why those who have imagined Theosophy to be a new religion have searched in vain for its creed and its ritual. Its only creed is Loyalty to Truth, and its ritual is "to honor every truth by use."

> "How little the masses of humanity understand this principle of Universal Brotherhood, and how seldom its great importance is recognized, can be seen in the various opinions and false interpretations regarding the Theosophical Society. This Society was organized on this one principle—the essential Brotherhood of Humanity—as briefly outlined and described here. It has been attacked as both Buddhistic and anti-Christian, as if it could be both at once, when in fact both Buddhism and Christianity (as described by their inspired founders) make brotherhood the single essential requirement of both doctrine and life.

> Theosophy has also been regarded as something new under the sun, or at best as old mysticism masquerading under a new name. While it is true that many societies founded to support the principles of altruism, or essential brotherhood, have used various names, it is also true that many have also been called Theosophical, having the same principles and aims as the current society of that name. For all these societies, the core doctrine has been the same, and everything else has been secondary <note>original: "incidental"</note>. However, this does not change the fact that many people are drawn to these secondary details while overlooking or ignoring the essentials."

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

No better or more explicit answer—provided by a man who is one of our most esteemed and earnest Theosophists—could be given to your questions.

**ENQUIRER:** Which system do you prefer or follow, in that case, besides Buddhist <note>original: "Buddhistic"</note> ethics?

**THEOSOPHIST:** None, and all. We hold to no religion, just as we hold to no philosophy in particular: we select <note>original: "cull"</note> the good we find in each. But here, again, it must be stated that, like all other ancient systems, Theosophy is divided into <term>Exoteric</term> (public) and *<term>Esoteric</term>* (inner) Sections.

**ENQUIRER:** What is the difference?

<margin>* An "attached member" means someone who has joined a specific branch of the Theosophical Society <note>original: "T.S."</note>. An "unattached" member is someone who belongs to the Society as a whole and has received their diploma from the Headquarters (Adyar, Madras) but is not connected with a specific branch or lodge.</margin>

**THEOSOPHIST:** The members of the Theosophical Society at large are free to follow whatever religion or philosophy they like, or none if they prefer, provided they are in sympathy with, and ready to carry out, one or more of the three objects of the Association. The Society is a philanthropic and scientific body for spreading the idea of brotherhood through practical action instead of just theory. The Fellows may be Christians or Muslims <note>original: "Mussulmen"</note>, Jews or Parsis <note>original: "Parsees," the Zoroastrian community of India</note>, Buddhists or Brahmins <note>Members of the highest Hindu caste, traditionally priests and scholars</note>, Spiritualists or Materialists; it does not matter. However, every member must be a philanthropist, a scholar, a researcher of ancient <term>Aryan</term> <note>In this 19th-century context, "Aryan" refers to ancient Indo-Iranian or Sanskrit languages and cultures.</note> and other old literature, or a student of the soul <note>original: "psychic student"</note>. In short, they must help, if they can, in carrying out at least one of the program’s goals. Otherwise, they have no reason to become a "Fellow." Such is the majority of the public <term>Exoteric</term> Society, which is composed of "attached" and "unattached" members.* These may, or may not, become

<summary>This page defines the Theosophical Society as a non-religious, philanthropic, and scientific organization divided into public (exoteric) and private (esoteric) sections. It emphasizes that membership is open to individuals of all faiths, provided they support the cause of universal brotherhood and ancient research.</summary>

The proverb “Handsome is as handsome does” could be rephrased in this case as: “A Theosophist is someone who lives by Theosophy.”

### THEOSOPHISTS AND MEMBERS OF THE “T.S.” <note>T.S. stands for the Theosophical Society</note>

**ENQUIRER:** I understand that this applies to general <note>original: "lay"</note> members. But what about those who pursue the inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> study of Theosophy; are they the true Theosophists?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not necessarily—not until they have proven themselves to be so. They have joined the inner group and pledged to follow the rules of the hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> organization as strictly as they can. This is a difficult task, as the most important rule of all is the complete surrender of one's personality—that is <note>original: "i.e."</note>, a committed member must become a total altruist. They must never think of themselves, but instead forget their own vanity and pride in order to focus on the well-being of their fellow humans, as well as their brothers within the inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> circle. If the inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> teachings are to be of any benefit, they must live a life of moderation in all things, self-sacrifice, and strict morality, doing their duty to everyone. The few true Theosophists in the Society are found among these members. This does not mean that there are no Theosophists outside of the Society and its inner circle; for there...

**ENQUIRER:** Then what is the point of joining the so-called Theosophical Society in that case? What is the motivation?

**THEOSOPHIST:** There is none, except for the advantage of receiving inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> instructions—the authentic doctrines of the "Wisdom-Religion"—and, if the actual plan is followed, gaining a great deal of help from mutual assistance and shared understanding. Unity provides strength and harmony, and well-coordinated, collective efforts produce wonders. This has been the secret of all organizations and communities since humanity began.

**ENQUIRER:** But why couldn't a person with a stable mind and a focused purpose—someone, let's say, with tireless energy and persistence—become a student of hidden knowledge <note>original: "Occultist"</note> or even a master <note>original: "Adept"</note> if they work alone?

**THEOSOPHIST:** They might; but the odds against success are ten thousand to one. One reason among many is that no modern books on the study of hidden forces <note>original: "Occultism"</note> or divine magic <note>original: "Theurgy"; from the Greek *theourgia*</note> explain the secrets of alchemy or medieval Theosophy in straightforward language. Everything is written in symbols or parables; and since the key to understanding these has been lost in the West for centuries, how can anyone learn the true meaning of what they are reading and studying? This is where the greatest danger lies—one that leads to the unintended practice of ***black magic*** or to becoming a helpless psychic channel <note>original: "mediumship"</note>. Anyone who does not have an enlightened teacher <note>original: "Initiate"</note> as a master should leave this dangerous study alone. Look around you and observe. While two-thirds of civilized society mock the very idea that there is any truth in ***Theosophy***, ***Occultism***, ***Spiritualism***, or the ***Kabbalah*** <note>original: "Kabala"; a traditional system of Jewish mysticism</note>, the other third is made up of the most diverse and conflicting...

Every person who puts these theories into practice is rapidly drifting, through ignorance, into **black magic**. Those who escape from it are fortunate, as they have neither a test nor a standard by which they can distinguish between the true and the false.

...share the knowledge they received with others who did not have the same opportunity. A small portion of the true sciences is better than a mass of learning that is not understood or properly processed. An ounce of gold is worth more than a ton of dust.

**Enquirer:** But how can someone tell if that ounce is real gold or just a fake?

**Theosophist:** You know a tree by its fruit, and a system by its results. Our critics should try to prove that any person studying the hidden sciences <note>original: "Occultism"</note> on their own throughout the ages has ever become a saintly master <note>original: "adept"</note> like Ammonius Saccas <note>The 3rd-century founder of Neoplatonism</note>, or even a Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Ammonius Saccas</note>, or a miracle-worker <note>original: "Theurgist"</note> like Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the Egyptian mysteries</note>. If they can show us that, then we will admit we are wrong. 

But until then, Theosophists prefer to follow the proven natural law of the tradition of the Sacred Science. There are mystics who have made great discoveries in chemistry and the physical sciences that almost touch upon alchemy and the hidden sciences. Others, through sheer genius, have rediscovered parts—or even all—of the lost alphabets of the "Mystery language" and are therefore able to read Hebrew scrolls correctly. Still others are seers who have caught wonderful glimpses of the hidden secrets of Nature. 

But all of these people are *specialists*. One is a theoretical inventor; another is a student of Hebrew—specifically <note>original: "i.e."</note> a sectarian follower of the **Kabbalah** <note>original: "Kabalist"</note>; a third is a modern version of Swedenborg <note>Emanuel Swedenborg, a 17th-century Swedish scientist and mystic who claimed to visit heaven and hell</note> who rejects everything outside his own specific science or religion. Not one of them can claim to have produced a benefit for the whole world, or even for their own nation, or even for themselves. With

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limitations of modern independent study compared to ancient traditions and the role of specialized knowledge in healing.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

...with the exception of a few healers—of that class which the Royal College of Physicians or Surgeons would call quacks—none have helped humanity with their science, nor even helped a significant number of people within their own community. Where are the ancient Chaldeans <note>original: "Chaldees"; an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their knowledge of astronomy and magic</note> who performed marvelous cures, “not by magic charms but by medicinal herbs <note>original: "simples"</note>”? Where is an Apollonius of Tyana <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note>, who healed the sick and raised the dead in any climate or circumstance? We know of some specialists of the former class in Europe, but none of the latter—except in Asia, where the secret of the Yogi—how “to live in death”—is still preserved.

**ENQUIRER:** Is the goal of Theosophy to produce such expert healers <note>original: "adepts"</note>?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Its aims are several; but the most important of all are those likely to lead to the relief of human suffering in any and every form, both moral and physical. And we believe the former to be far more important than the latter. Theosophy must teach <note>original: "inculcate"</note> ethics; it must purify the soul if it intends to relieve the physical body, whose illnesses—except in cases of accidents—are all hereditary. One can never reach the true goal of helping suffering humanity by studying the hidden sciences <note>original: "Occultism"</note> for selfish ends, or for the gratification of personal ambition, pride, or vanity. Nor does a person become a student of these hidden truths <note>original: "Occultist"</note> by studying only a single branch of the inner philosophy <note>original: "esoteric philosophy"</note>; they must study, if not master, them all.

**ENQUIRER:** Is help for reaching this most important goal given only to those who study the inner sciences?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not at all. Every general member <note>original: "lay member"</note> is entitled to general instruction if they want it; but few are willing to become what are called “working members,” and most prefer to remain the

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

...drones of Theosophy <note>People who are members of the Society but do not actively contribute to its work or study</note>. Let it be understood that private research is encouraged in the Theosophical Society <note>T.S.</note>, provided it does not cross the boundary that separates the exoteric <note>original: "exoteric"; public or external knowledge</note> from the esoteric <note>original: "esoteric"; secret or inner knowledge</note>—the "blind" or accidental magic from conscious magic.

### THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THEOSOPHY AND OCCULTISM.

**ENQUIRER:** You speak of Theosophy and Occultism <note>The study of hidden spiritual laws and powers</note>; are they identical?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not at all. A person may be a very good Theosophist indeed, whether *inside* or *outside* of the Society, without being an Occultist in any way. But no one can be a true Occultist without being a real Theosophist; otherwise, he is simply a black magician <note>Someone who uses hidden powers for selfish or harmful ends</note>, whether he realizes it or not.

**ENQUIRER:** What do you mean?

**THEOSOPHIST:** I have said already that a true Theosophist must practice the highest moral ideals, must strive to realize his unity with all of humanity, and work tirelessly for others. Now, if an Occultist does not do all this, he must be acting selfishly for his own personal benefit. If he has acquired more practical power than other ordinary people, he immediately becomes a far more dangerous enemy to the world and those around him than the average mortal. This is clear.

**ENQUIRER:** So, is an Occultist simply a person who possesses more power than other people?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Far more—if he is a *practical* and truly learned Occultist, and not just one in name. Occult sciences are *not*, as they are described in encyclopedias, "those imaginary sciences of the..."

<summary>This page clarifies the distinction between Theosophy and Occultism, emphasizing that while Theosophy focuses on altruism and ethics, Occultism involves the study of hidden powers which, without a moral foundation, can lead to dangerous "black magic."</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Occultism, black magic, ethics, altruism, esoteric, exoteric, spiritual power, Theosophical Society</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of Occultism, specifically refuting the idea that these sciences are merely "supposed" powers or relics of the past.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

...Middle Ages that related to the *supposed* action or influence of hidden <note>original: "Occult"</note> qualities or supernatural powers, such as alchemy, magic, necromancy <note>The practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead</note>, and astrology," because they are real, actual, and very dangerous sciences. They teach the secret potency of things in Nature, developing and cultivating the hidden powers "lying dormant <note>original: "latent"</note> in humanity," thereby giving the practitioner tremendous advantages over more ignorant people. Hypnotism, which has now become so common and a subject of serious scientific study, is a good example. *Hypnotic power* was discovered almost by accident, after the way was paved by mesmerism <note>An earlier form of hypnotism named after Franz Mesmer, involving the theory of "animal magnetism"</note>. Now, a skilled hypnotist can do almost anything with it—from forcing a person, without their knowledge, to act like a fool, to making them commit a crime. This is often done as a proxy for the hypnotist and *for the hypnotist's benefit*. Isn't this a terrible power if left in the hands of unethical people? And please remember that this is only one of the minor branches of Occultism.

**ENQUIRER:** But aren't all these hidden sciences—magic and sorcery—considered by the most cultured and learned people to be relics of ancient ignorance and superstition?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Let me remind you that this argument works both ways. The "most cultured and learned" among you also regard Christianity and every other religion as a relic of ignorance and superstition. People are beginning to believe in hypnotism now, at any rate; and some—even among the most cultured—believe in Theosophy and supernatural events <note>original: "phenomena"</note>. But who among them, except preachers and blind fanatics, will admit to a belief in *Biblical miracles*? This is where the difference lies.

<meta>This page continues the distinction between theoretical Theosophy and practical Occultism, transitioning into a critique of the Spiritualist movement’s explanation for supernatural phenomena.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

There are very good and pure Theosophists who may believe in the supernatural, including divine miracles, but no Occultist will do so. This is because an Occultist practices *scientific* Theosophy, based on accurate knowledge of Nature’s secret workings. However, a Theosophist who practices powers considered abnormal, without <note>original: "minus"</note> the light of Occultism, will simply drift toward a dangerous form of mediumship <note>The practice of acting as a channel for communication between the living and the spirits of the dead</note>. This is because, although they hold to Theosophy and its highest conceivable ethical code, they practice it in the dark, based on sincere but *blind* faith. Anyone—whether a Theosophist or a Spiritualist—who attempts to cultivate one of the branches of Occult science—for example <note>original: "e.g."</note>, Hypnotism, Mesmerism <note>An early form of hypnotism involving the theory of "animal magnetism"</note>, or even the secrets of producing physical phenomena, and so on—without knowing the philosophical reasoning <note>original: "rationale"</note> behind those powers, is like a boat without a rudder launched on a stormy ocean.

### *THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THEOSOPHY AND SPIRITUALISM.*

**ENQUIRER:** But do you not believe in Spiritualism?

**THEOSOPHIST:** If by "Spiritualism" you mean the explanation that Spiritualists give for certain abnormal phenomena, then we most certainly *do not*. They maintain that these manifestations are all produced by the "spirits" of deceased mortals—generally their relatives—who return to earth, they say, to communicate with those they loved or to whom they were attached. We deny this completely. We assert that the spirits of the dead cannot return to earth—except in rare and exceptional cases, which I may speak about later. Nor do they communicate with humans except by entirely subjective <note>Existing in the mind; internal or mental rather than physical</note> means. That which...

<summary>The author distinguishes between "blind" Theosophy and "scientific" Occultism, while categorically rejecting the Spiritualist belief that the dead can physically return to communicate with the living.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophy, Occultism, Spiritualism, mediumship, Mesmerism, afterlife communication, subjective phenomena</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while spirits of the dead do not return to earth, certain "phantoms" or remnants may appear, though they are not the true soul of the deceased. It transitions into explaining the Theosophical view of spiritualist phenomena.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

Whatever appears physically <note>original: "objectively"</note> is only the phantom of the former physical man. However, we do believe most decidedly in *psychic* and, so to speak, "spiritual" Spiritualism.

**ENQUIRER:** Do you also reject the phenomena [of séances]?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Certainly not—except in cases of intentional fraud.

**ENQUIRER:** How do you account for them, then?

**THEOSOPHIST:** In many ways. The causes of such manifestations are not nearly as simple as Spiritualists would like to believe. Above all, the driving force <note>original: "deus ex machinâ"; a Latin phrase meaning "a god from a machine," referring to a power that provides a solution to a problem</note> behind so-called "materializations" is usually the astral body or "double" of the medium or of someone present. This astral body is also the producer or active force in manifestations like slate-writing, "Davenport"-style <note>Refers to the Davenport Brothers, famous 19th-century mediums known for their "spirit cabinet" performances</note> manifestations, and so on.

**ENQUIRER:** You say "usually"; then what produces the rest?

**THEOSOPHIST:** That depends on the nature of the manifestations. Sometimes they are astral remains—the "shells" <note>The cast-off psychic remains of a person in the intermediate state after death</note> of former *personalities* that no longer exist in our world <note>original: "Kamalokic 'shells'"</note>; at other times, they are Elementals <note>Nature spirits or non-human entities that inhabit the invisible planes of existence</note>. "Spirit" is a word with many broad meanings. I really do not know what Spiritualists mean by the term; but we understand them to claim that physical phenomena are produced by the reincarnating *Ego*—the *spiritual* and immortal "individuality." We reject this hypothesis entirely. The *conscious individuality* of the deceased cannot materialize, nor can it return from its own mental state in Devachan <note>original: "Devachanic sphere"; a blissful, heavenly state between earthly incarnations</note> to the realm of physical reality <note>original: "plane of terrestrial objectivity"</note>.

**ENQUIRER:** But many of the communications received from the "spirits" show not only intelligence, but a knowledge of facts not known to...

<summary>The author argues that spiritualist phenomena are not caused by the immortal souls of the dead, but rather by the "astral bodies" of the living, psychic "shells" of the deceased, or elemental nature spirits.</summary>
<keywords>spiritualism, materialization, astral body, Kamaloka, Elementals, Ego, Devachan, mediumship, Davenport Brothers</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the Enquirer's observation that information appearing in spiritualist séances is often unknown to the medium or the audience. It transitions into the Theosophical explanation that such intelligence stems from the "inner Self" rather than external spirits.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY<-

...the medium, and sometimes even not consciously present to the mind of the investigator, or any of those who compose the audience.

**THEOSOPHIST:** This does not necessarily prove that the intelligence and knowledge you speak of belong to spirits, or originate from *disembodied* souls. Sleepwalkers <note>original: "Somnambulists"; individuals who perform actions while asleep or in a trance-like state</note> have been known to compose music and poetry and to solve mathematical problems while in their trance state, without having ever learned music or mathematics. Others responded intelligently to questions put to them, and even, in several cases, spoke languages—such as Hebrew and Latin—of which they were entirely ignorant when awake; all this occurred in a state of profound sleep. Will you, then, maintain that this was caused by “spirits”?

**ENQUIRER:** But how would you explain it?

**THEOSOPHIST:** We assert that the divine spark in man, being one and identical in its essence with the Universal Spirit, our “spiritual Self” is practically all-knowing <note>original: "omniscient"</note>, but that it cannot manifest its knowledge owing to the obstacles <note>original: "impediments"</note> of matter. Now, the more these obstacles are removed—in other words, the more the physical body is paralyzed regarding its own independent activity and consciousness, as in deep sleep, a deep trance, or even in illness—the more fully the *inner Self* can manifest on this physical level <note>original: "plane"</note>. This is our explanation of those truly wonderful phenomena of a higher order, in which undeniable intelligence and knowledge are displayed. As for the lower order of manifestations, such as physical phenomena and the clichés <note>original: "platitudes"</note> and trivial talk of the average “spirit,” to explain even the most important of the teachings we hold upon the subject...

<summary>This page argues that the intelligence displayed in trances comes from the "spiritual Self," which is naturally all-knowing but usually blocked by the physical body, rather than from external spirits. It distinguishes between these high-level manifestations of the inner self and the trivial physical phenomena often seen in séances.</summary>

<keywords>Theosophy, somnambulism, divine spark, Universal Spirit, spiritual Self, trance, consciousness, omniscience</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the Theosophist's explanation of psychic phenomena, arguing that while some events involve disembodied souls, many are often misunderstood by Spiritualists.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. ###<-

...would take up more space and time than can be allocated to it at present. We have no desire to interfere with the belief of the Spiritualists any more than with any other belief. The burden of proof <note>original: "*onus probandi*"</note> must fall on those who believe in "spirits." Even now, while still convinced that the higher sort of manifestations occur through disembodied souls, the leaders and the most learned and intelligent among the Spiritualists are the first to confess that not all phenomena are produced by spirits. Eventually, they will come to recognize the whole truth. In the meantime, we have no right or desire to try to convert <note>original: "proselytize"</note> them to our views. This is especially true since, in cases of purely psychic and spiritual manifestations, we also believe in the communication between the spirit of the living person and the spirits of those who have died.*

---

\* We maintain that in such cases, it is not the spirits of the dead who *descend* to earth, but rather the spirits of the living that *ascend* to the level of the pure Spiritual Souls. In reality, there is neither ascending nor descending, but rather a change of state or condition for the medium <note>a person thought to be a bridge between the living and the dead</note>. When the medium’s body becomes paralyzed or "entranced," the spiritual Ego <note>the permanent, immortal part of a person's being</note> is freed from its restraints <note>original: "trammels"</note> and finds itself on the same level <note>original: "plane"</note> of consciousness as the disembodied spirits. 

Therefore, if there is any spiritual attraction between the two, *they can communicate*, as often happens in dreams. The difference between a mediumistic nature and a non-sensitive nature is this: the liberated spirit of a medium has the opportunity and ease of influencing the passive organs of its entranced physical body. It can make those organs act, speak, and write according to its will. The Ego can make the body repeat—like an echo and in human language—the thoughts and ideas of the disembodied entity, as well as its own. 

However, the *non-receptive* or non-sensitive organism of someone who is very "positive" <note>in this context, "positive" refers to someone whose physical or skeptical nature is resistant to psychic impressions</note> cannot be influenced in this way. Consequently, although there is hardly a human being whose Ego does not communicate freely during sleep with those they loved and lost, no memory of it remains. Because of the "positiveness" and lack of receptivity in their physical shell and brain, no recollection—or perhaps only a very dim, dream-like memory—lingers once the person is awake.

<summary>The author explains that "spirit communication" is actually the living person's spiritual Ego reaching the level of the deceased during a trance or sleep, rather than spirits descending to Earth. She notes that while everyone experiences this during sleep, only "sensitive" or mediumistic individuals can bring those memories back to their waking consciousness.</summary>

<keywords>Spiritualism, Ego, mediumship, consciousness, spirit communication, trance, dreams, receptivity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Spiritualism, moving from the mechanics of mediumship to a critique of Spiritualism’s lack of a formal philosophical system compared to Theosophy.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. ###<-

**ENQUIRER:** Does this mean that you reject the philosophy of Spiritualism entirely <note>original: "*in toto*"</note>?

**THEOSOPHIST:** If by "philosophy" you mean their underdeveloped <note>original: "crude"</note> theories, yes, we do. But in truth, they have no philosophy. Their best, most intellectual, and earnest defenders say as much. No one—except perhaps a blind materialist of the "Huxley big toe" school <note>A reference to Thomas Henry Huxley, a famous scientist and proponent of Darwinism, whom Blavatsky often criticized for reducing human consciousness to purely physical or "material" components</note>—will or can deny their fundamental and only unquestionable truth: that phenomena occur through mediums who are controlled by invisible forces and intelligences. However, regarding their philosophy, let me read to you what the capable editor of the journal *Light* says about them. The Spiritualists will find no wiser or more devoted champion than him. This is what "M.A. Oxon" <note>The pseudonym of William Stainton Moses (1839–1892), a prominent clergyman and medium who attempted to bring a more philosophical approach to Spiritualism</note>, one of the very few truly philosophical Spiritualists, writes regarding their lack of organization and blind stubbornness:

> It is worthwhile to look steadily at this point, for it is of vital importance. We possess an experience and a knowledge compared to which all other knowledge is relatively insignificant. The ordinary Spiritualist becomes very angry <note>original: "waxes wroth"</note> if anyone dares to challenge <note>original: "impugn"</note> his confident knowledge of the future and his absolute certainty of the life to come. Where other men have stretched forth feeble hands, groping into the dark future, he walks boldly like someone who has a map and knows his way. Where other men have stopped short with a religious hope <note>original: "pious aspiration"</note> or have been content with a faith inherited from their ancestors, it is his boast that he knows what they only believe. He claims that from his rich store of experience, he can supplement the fading faiths built only upon hope. He is magnificent in his dealings with humanity’s most cherished expectations. "You hope," he seems to say, "for that which I can demonstrate. You have accepted a traditional belief in what I can prove experimentally, according to the strictest scientific method. The old beliefs are fading; come out from them and be separate. They contain as much falsehood as truth. Only by building on a sure foundation..."

<meta>This page continues a long quotation from the journal 'Light' criticizing the lack of organization among Spiritualists, then transitions into a discussion on the distinction between 'personality' and 'individuality' in Theosophical thought.</meta>

->### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. ###<-

...only on the foundation of demonstrated fact can your structure <note>original: "superstructure"</note> be stable. All around you, old faiths are toppling. Avoid the crash and get yourself out.

> "When one comes to deal with this magnificent person in a practical way, what is the result? It is very curious and very disappointing. He is so sure of his ground that he takes no trouble to find out the interpretation which others put upon his facts. The wisdom of the ages has concerned itself with the explanation of what he rightly regards as proven; but he does not give even a passing glance to its researches. He does not even agree altogether with his fellow <note>original: "brother"</note> Spiritualist. It is the story over again of the old Scottish woman <note>original: "Scotch body"</note> who, together with her husband, formed a 'church' <note>original: "kirk"</note>. They believed they held the exclusive keys to Heaven—or, rather, she did, for she was 'not certain about James' <note>original: "na certain aboot Jamie." This is a joke about extreme religious sectarianism where a person eventually trusts no one's salvation but their own.</note>. So the infinitely divided and subdivided and re-subdivided sects of Spiritualists shake their heads, and are 'not certain about' one another. Again, the collective experience of mankind is solid and unvarying on this point: that union is strength, and disunion is a source of weakness and failure. Standing shoulder to shoulder, drilled and disciplined, a mob <note>original: "rabble"</note> becomes an army, where each man is a match for a hundred of the untrained men that may be brought against it. Organization in every department of man's work means success, the saving of time and labor, profit, and development. Want of method, want of plan, haphazard work, inconsistent <note>original: "fitful"</note> energy, and undisciplined effort—these mean bungling failure. The voice of humanity confirms <note>original: "attests"</note> the truth. Does the Spiritualist accept the verdict and act on the conclusion? Truly <note>original: "Verily"</note>, no. He refuses to organize. He is a law unto himself, and a nuisance <note>original: "a thorn in the side"</note> to his neighbors." — *Light*, June 22, 1889.

**ENQUIRER:** I was told that the Theosophical Society was originally founded to destroy <note>original: "crush"</note> Spiritualism and the belief in the survival of human individuality?

**THEOSOPHIST:** You are misinformed. Our beliefs are all founded on that immortal individuality. But then, like so many others, you confuse *personality* with *individuality*. Your Western psychologists do not seem to have established any clear distinction...
