<meta>This page continues the Theosophist's critique of traditional prayer, shifting from the specific example of war to a broader philosophical argument about the psychological and spiritual dangers of petitionary prayer.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

**THEOSOPHIST.** This is explained by the fact that prayer has several other meanings beyond the one given to it by Christians. It refers not only to a plea or petition, but in ancient times, it meant much more—an invocation or an incantation. The <term>mantra</term> <note>A sacred sound, syllable, or phrase in Sanskrit believed to have spiritual and psychological power.</note>, or the rhythmically chanted prayer of the Hindus, has exactly this meaning, as the Brahmins <note>The highest-ranking social class in Hindu society, traditionally responsible for religious rituals and teaching.</note> consider themselves higher than the common <term>devas</term> (gods). A prayer can be an appeal or an incantation for a curse <note>original: "malediction"</note>—such as when two armies pray at the same time for each other’s destruction—just as easily as it can be for a blessing. 

Since the vast majority of people are intensely selfish and pray only for themselves—asking to be given their "daily bread" instead of working for it, and begging God not to lead them "into temptation" but to deliver them (and only those making the petition <note>original: "memorialists"</note>) from evil—the result is that prayer, as it is understood today, is harmful <note>original: "pernicious"</note> in two ways: 

(a) It destroys a person's self-reliance; 
(b) It encourages an even more ferocious selfishness and ego than nature has already given them. 

I repeat: we believe in "communion" and acting in unison with our "Father in secret." In rare moments of ecstatic bliss, we believe in the merging of our higher soul with the universal essence, drawn toward its origin and center. This state is called <term>Samadhi</term> <note>A state of intense concentration achieved through meditation, leading to spiritual union.</note> during life, and <term>Nirvana</term> <note>The ultimate state of spiritual enlightenment and release from the cycle of suffering and rebirth.</note> after death. We refuse to pray to created, finite beings—such as gods, saints, or angels—because we consider that to be idolatry. We cannot pray to the **ABSOLUTE** <note>In Theosophy, the "Absolute" is the ultimate, infinite, and impersonal reality that is the source of all existence; because it is infinite, it cannot be influenced by human requests.</note> for the reasons explained previously. Therefore, we try to replace fruitless and useless prayer with worthy and beneficial actions.

<summary>The Theosophist argues that modern petitionary prayer fosters selfishness and dependency, contrasting it with the ancient use of mantras and the high spiritual states of Samadhi and Nirvana.</summary>

**THEOSOPHIST.** Entirely so. This—when understood esoterically <note>From the "esoteric" or inner, hidden perspective, as opposed to the "exoteric" or literal, public meaning.</note>—is confirmed by both Buddha and Jesus. The one says, "Seek nothing <note>original: "nought"</note> from the helpless gods—do not pray! *But rather act*; for darkness will not become bright. And the other—Jesus—recommends: "Whatever you shall ask in my name (that of <term>Christos</term> <note>original Greek: "Christos." Of course, if this quote is taken literally, it goes against our argument. But if we accept it esoterically, with a full understanding of the meaning of the term "Christos"—which to us represents <term>Atma-Buddhi-Manas</term> <note>A Sanskrit term for the three higher parts of the human soul: Atma (Spirit), Buddhi (Intuition/Universal Soul), and Manas (Mind).</note>, the "SELF"—it comes to this: the only God we must recognize and pray to (or rather, act in harmony with) is that spirit of God for which our body is the temple, and in which that spirit lives <note>original: "dwelleth"</note>.

## ON THE SOURCE OF THE HUMAN SOUL.

**ENQUIRER.** How, then, do you explain man being endowed with a Spirit and Soul? Where do these come from?

**THEOSOPHIST.** From the Universal Soul. They are certainly not bestowed by a personal God. Where does the moisture in a jellyfish come from? From the ocean that surrounds it, in which it lives, breathes, and exists <note>original: "has its being"</note>, and to which it returns when it dissolves.

**ENQUIRER.** So you reject the teaching that the Soul is given, or breathed into man, by God?

**THEOSOPHIST.** We are forced to. The "Soul" spoken of in chapter 2 of Genesis (verse 7) is, as stated there, the "living Soul" or *Nephesh* <note>original: Hebrew for "breath," "living being," or "appetite"</note>—the vital, animal soul—with which God (we say "nature" and unchanging <note>original: "immutable"</note> law) endows man just like every other animal. It is not at all the thinking Soul or mind; least of all is it the immortal Spirit.

**ENQUIRER.** Well, let us put it another way: is it God who endows man with a human rational Soul and immortal Spirit?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Again, we must object to the way you put the question. Since we believe in no personal God, how can we believe that he endows man with anything? But granting, for the sake of argument, a God who takes the risk of creating a new Soul for every newborn baby, all that can be said is that such a God can hardly be regarded as being endowed with any wisdom or foresight <note>original: "prevision"</note>. There are certain other difficulties and the impossibility of reconciling this with the claims made for his mercy, justice, equity, and—

### *THE BUDDHIST TEACHINGS ON THE ABOVE.*

**ENQUIRER.** What does Buddhism teach regarding the Soul?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It depends on whether you mean *exoteric* <note>original: "exoteric" refers to public, external teachings intended for the general public</note>, popular Buddhism, or its *esoteric* <note>original: "esoteric" refers to secret, inner teachings intended for a small group of initiates</note> teachings. # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

During this life, the *Skandhas* are continually changing. While a man at age forty is identical in personality with that same person as a youth of eighteen, he is nevertheless a different being because of the continual waste and repair of his body and the changes in his mind and character. However, in his old age, the man justly receives the rewards or experiences the suffering resulting from his thoughts and actions at every previous stage of his life. Likewise, the new being in the rebirth—being the same individuality as before (but not the same personality), with only a changed form or a new collection of *Skandhas*—justly reaps the consequences of his actions and thoughts in the previous existence." This is complex metaphysics, and it clearly does not express a disbelief in the Soul by any means.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

later editors of the Gospels regarding damnation and hellfire as the *word-for-word* <note>original: "verbatim"</note> utterances of Jesus. Neither Buddha nor "Christ" ever wrote anything themselves; instead, both spoke in allegories and used "dark sayings" <note>cryptic or symbolic parables intended for those with spiritual insight</note>, as all true Initiates <note>individuals who have been admitted into secret spiritual knowledge or mysteries</note> did, and will continue to do for a long time to come. Both sets of Scriptures treat these metaphysical questions very cautiously. Both the Buddhist and Christian records suffer from an excess of exotericism <note>original: "exotericism"; outward, public teachings intended for the masses, as opposed to "esoteric" inner meanings</note>; in both cases, the literal "dead-letter" meaning misses the intended point entirely.

<term>ENQUIRER.</term> Do you mean to suggest that neither the teachings of Buddha nor those of Christ have been rightly understood until now?

<term>THEOSOPHIST.</term> What I mean is exactly what you say. Both Gospels—the Buddhist and the Christian—were preached with the same goal in mind. Both reformers were passionate philanthropists and practical altruists, unmistakably preaching a form of Socialism of the noblest and highest type: self-sacrifice to the very end. "Let the sins of the whole world fall upon me so that I may relieve human misery and suffering!" cries Buddha; . . . "I would not let a single person cry whom I could save!" exclaims the "Prince-beggar" <note>a title for Gautama Buddha, who abandoned his royal life to live as a wandering monk</note>, dressed in the discarded rags of the burial grounds. "Come to me, all you who labor and are heavily burdened, and I will give you rest," is the appeal to the poor and the disinherited made by the "Man of Sorrows" [Jesus], who has <note>original: "hath"</note> nowhere to lay his head. The teachings of both emphasize boundless love for humanity, charity, forgiveness of injury, selflessness, and pity for the deluded masses. Both show the same contempt for riches and make no distinction between *mine* and *thine* <note>original: "meum" and "tuum"; Latin for "mine" and "yours," referring to the concept of private property or personal possession</note>. Their desire was to help the ignorant and the misled, whose burdens in life were too heavy, without necessarily revealing the sacred mysteries of initiation to *everyone*.

<summary>This page argues that both Jesus and Buddha were altruistic reformers whose original messages of self-sacrifice and universal love have been obscured by literalist interpretations and "exotericism." It highlights their shared focus on the poor and their common use of allegorical "dark sayings" to convey spiritual truths.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

...for them, there was enough hope and a sufficient inkling of the truth to support them during their heaviest hours. But the goals of both Reformers were frustrated because of the excessive zeal of their later followers. Because the words of the Masters were misunderstood and misinterpreted, look at the consequences!

<term>ENQUIRER.</term> But surely Buddha must have rejected the idea of the soul's immortality, if all the Western scholars of the East <note>original: "Orientalists"</note> and even his own priests say so!

<term>THEOSOPHIST.</term> The Arhats <note>enlightened Buddhist disciples who have attained liberation</note> began by following the policy of their Master, but the majority of the priests who followed them were not initiated <note>those who have not been admitted into the inner, secret spiritual knowledge</note>, just as happened in Christianity. Consequently, little by little, the great inner <note>original: "esoteric"</note> truths were almost lost. A proof of this is that, of the two existing sects in Ceylon <note>modern-day Sri Lanka</note>, the Siamese sect <note>referring to the Siyam Nikaya, a lineage of Buddhist monks in Sri Lanka originating from Thailand</note> believes death to be the absolute destruction of individuality and personality, while the other sect explains Nirvana <note>the ultimate state of spiritual peace and release from suffering</note> exactly as we Theosophists do.

<term>ENQUIRER.</term> But why, in that case, do Buddhism and Christianity represent the two opposite extremes of such a belief?

<term>THEOSOPHIST.</term> Because the conditions under which they were preached were not the same. In India, the Brahmins <note>the highest, priestly caste in Hindu society</note>—jealous of their superior knowledge and excluding every caste but their own from it—had driven millions of people into idolatry and almost into fetishism <note>the worship of physical objects believed to possess magical or spiritual powers</note>. Buddha had to strike a fatal blow against an overabundance of unhealthy imagination and fanatical superstition born of ignorance—the likes of which have rarely been seen before or since. Philosophical atheism <note>a system of thought that does not rely on a personal creator god, but rather on natural and spiritual laws</note> is better than such ignorant worship for those—

> ->“Who cry upon their gods and are not heard,<-
> ->Or are not heeded——”<-

<summary>This page explains that the original "esoteric" or inner truths of Buddhism and Christianity were lost as uninitiated priests took over. It also clarifies that Buddha's seeming "atheism" was a necessary reaction against the extreme superstitions and caste-based exclusion of his time.</summary>

<keywords>Arhats, Buddhism, Christianity, Ceylon (Sri Lanka), Nirvana, Brahmins, superstition, idolatry, esoteric vs exoteric, religious reform</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the original teachings of Buddha and Jesus were focused on relief for the suffering masses, but were eventually distorted by their followers.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. 81</header>

...and who live and die in mental despair. He had to first stop this muddy flood of superstition and uproot errors before he revealed the truth. And since he could not reveal everything—for the same good reason as Jesus, who reminded his disciples that the Mysteries of Heaven are not for the uneducated masses but for the chosen few <note>original: "elect"</note> alone, and therefore "he spoke to them in parables" (Matthew 13:11)—his caution led Buddha to hide too much. He even refused to tell the monk Vacchagotta whether there was, or was not, an "Ego" <note>In Theosophy, the "Ego" refers to the enduring spiritual self, as opposed to the temporary personality.</note> in man. When pressed for an answer, "the Exalted One <note>A title for the Buddha.</note> remained silent."*

<term>ENQUIRER.</term> This refers to Gautama, but in what way does it relate to the Gospels?

***

* Buddha gave a clear and direct <note>original: "unequivocal"</note> answer to Ananda—his initiated disciple who asked why he remained silent—in the dialogue translated by Oldenburg from the *Samyuttaka Nikaya* <note>A major collection of Buddhist scriptures.</note>: "If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me, 'Is there an Ego?' had answered 'The Ego exists,' then that, Ananda, would have confirmed the doctrine of the ascetics <note>original: "Samanas"</note> and priests <note>original: "Brahmanas"</note> who believed in a permanent, unchanging personality. If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me, 'Is there no Ego?' had answered, 'The Ego does not exist,' then that, Ananda, would have confirmed the doctrine of those who believed in total destruction <note>original: "annihilation"</note> at death. If I, Ananda, when the wandering monk Vacchagotta asked me, 'Is there an Ego?' had answered, 'The Ego exists,' would that have served my purpose, Ananda, by helping him understand that all forms of existence <note>original: "dhamma"</note> are not the true self? But if I, Ananda, had answered, 'The Ego does not exist,' then that would only have caused the wandering monk Vacchagotta to be thrown from one confusion <note>original: "bewilderment"</note> to another, thinking: 'Did my Ego not exist before? But now it exists no longer!'" This shows, better than anything else, that Gautama Buddha withheld such difficult philosophical <note>original: "metaphysical"</note> doctrines from the masses in order not to confuse them further. What he meant was the distinction between the temporary personal "I" and the Higher Self, which shines its light on the imperishable Ego—the true spiritual "I" of a human being.

<summary>This page explains why Buddha and Jesus used parables or silence when dealing with the public, arguing that they withheld complex spiritual truths to avoid confusing the uneducated. It details a specific conversation where Buddha explains that confirming or denying the "Ego" would only lead to more misunderstanding for those not yet initiated.</summary>

<keywords>Gautama Buddha, Ananda, Vacchagotta, Ego, Higher Self, Samyuttaka Nikaya, parables, Jesus, metaphysics, spiritual education</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between Buddha and Jesus, exploring how their silence on certain mysteries was a necessary precaution that unfortunately led to later religious divisions.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

THEOSOPHIST. Study history and reflect on it. At the time the events described in the Gospels are said to have occurred, a similar intellectual ferment was taking place across the entire civilized world, though with opposite results in the East and the West. The old gods were dying out. In Palestine, the educated classes followed the lead of the skeptical Sadducees <note>A Jewish sect that denied the resurrection and the existence of spirits.</note> into materialistic denials and a mere literal adherence to the Law of Moses <note>original: "dead-letter Mosaic form"</note>. In Rome, they fell into moral decay. Meanwhile, the lower and poorer classes chased after sorcery and foreign gods, or became hypocrites and Pharisees <note>A Jewish sect known for strict observance of rites and ceremonies, often criticized in the New Testament for hypocrisy.</note>. Once again, the time for a spiritual reform had arrived.

The cruel, <term>anthropomorphic</term> <gloss>human-like in form or personality</gloss> and jealous God of the Jews—with his bloody <note>original: "sanguinary"</note> laws of "an eye for an eye and a tooth for a tooth," of bloodshed and animal sacrifice—had to be moved to a secondary position. He needed to be replaced by the merciful "Father in Secret." This "Father" had to be shown not as an <term>extra-Cosmic</term> <gloss>existing outside of or separate from the physical universe</gloss> God, but as a divine Savior of mortal man, enshrined within his own heart and soul, in the poor and the rich alike. 

In the West, just as in India, the secrets of initiation could not be made public. This was to prevent "giving that which is holy to the dogs and casting pearls before swine," lest both the teacher and the truths revealed be trampled underfoot. Thus, the silence <note>original: "reticence"</note> of both Buddha and Jesus—regardless of whether Jesus lived through the historical period usually assigned to him <note>The author hints at the occult view that the historical Jesus may have lived a century earlier than commonly believed.</note>—led to unintended consequences. Because they both chose not to reveal the Mysteries of Life and Death plainly, their silence resulted in the total denials of Southern Buddhism <note>Now commonly called Theravada Buddhism; at the time, it was often interpreted by Westerners as a purely atheistic or "negative" philosophy.</note> on one hand, and on the other, the three clashing branches of the Christian Church and the three hundred sects found in Protestant England alone.

<summary>The author argues that Jesus and Buddha both arrived during times of spiritual decay to replace external, ritualistic religion with internal spirituality, but their necessary secrecy regarding deeper mysteries eventually led to religious fragmentation.</summary>

<keywords>Gospels, Sadducees, Pharisees, Law of Moses, Anthropomorphism, Father in Secret, Initiation, Southern Buddhism, Sectarianism, Jesus vs Buddha</keywords>

## VI.
### THEOSOPHICAL TEACHINGS REGARDING NATURE AND MAN.
---
#### ->*THE UNITY OF ALL IN ALL.*<-

ENQUIRER: Having told me what God, the Soul, and Man are *not*, according to your views, can you inform me what they *are*, according to your teachings?

THEOSOPHIST: In their origin and in eternity, these three—like the universe and everything within it—are one with the absolute Unity: that unknowable divine essence I spoke about some time ago. We do not believe in a *creation* <note>Theosophy rejects the idea of "creation out of nothing" (ex nihilo) by a personal deity.</note>, but rather in the periodical and consecutive appearances of the universe, moving from the subjective <note>The internal or non-physical state.</note> to the objective plane of being. This occurs at regular intervals of time, covering periods of immense duration.

ENQUIRER: Can you explain this subject in more detail?

THEOSOPHIST: As a first comparison to help you reach a more correct understanding, take the solar year; as a second, take the two halves of that year, which produce a single "day" and "night" each lasting six months at the North Pole. Now imagine, if you can, instead of a solar year of 365 days, ETERNITY. Let the sun...

<summary>This page introduces the Theosophical concept of Universal Unity, arguing that God, the Soul, and Man are emanations of one essence rather than separate creations. It describes the universe as appearing and disappearing in vast cycles rather than being created once.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Absolute Unity, Divine Essence, Subjective vs Objective, Cyclic Existence, Creationism vs Emanationism</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the cyclic nature of existence and the cosmological timeline of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

represent the universe, and the polar days and nights of six months each—*days and nights* lasting 182 trillions and quadrillions of years each, instead of 182 days each. Just as the sun rises every morning on our objective horizon out of its (to us) *subjective* and *opposite* <note>original: "antipodal"</note> space, so does the Universe emerge periodically on the plane of objectivity, issuing from the plane of subjectivity—the opposite of the former. This is the “Cycle of Life.” And as the sun disappears from our horizon, so does the Universe disappear at regular periods when the “Universal night” sets in. The Hindus call such alternations the “Days and Nights of Brahma,” or the time of *Manvantara* <note>A period of manifestation or activity</note> and that of *Pralaya* <note>A period of dissolution or rest</note>. Westerners may call them Universal Days and Nights if they prefer. During the latter (the nights) *All is in All*; every atom is resolved into one Homogeneity <note>A state of being all the same kind; uniform substance</note>.

---

->*EVOLUTION AND ILLUSION.*<-

ENQUIRER. But who is it that creates the Universe each time?
THEOSOPHIST. No one creates it. Science would call the process evolution; the pre-Christian philosophers and the Orientalists called it emanation. We, Occultists and Theosophists, see in it the only universal and eternal *reality* casting a periodical reflection of *itself* on the infinite depths of space. This reflection, which you regard as the objective *material* universe, we consider as a temporary *illusion* <note>original: "maya"</note> and nothing else. That alone which is eternal is real.
ENQUIRER. At that rate, you and I are also illusions.

<summary>This page explains the Theosophical concept of cyclic cosmic time through the "Days and Nights of Brahma" and argues that the material universe is a temporary reflection or illusion of an eternal reality.</summary>
<keywords>Manvantara, Pralaya, Days and Nights of Brahma, evolution vs emanation, cosmic cycles, illusion, reality, subjectivity vs objectivity</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between an Enquirer and a Theosophist regarding the nature of reality, the origin of the universe, and the relationship between the absolute and the manifest world.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

THEOSOPHIST. As fleeting personalities—one person today, another tomorrow—that is what we are. Would you call the sudden flashes of the *Aurora Borealis* (the Northern Lights) a "reality," even though it is as real as can be while you are looking at it? Certainly not; it is the cause that produces it—if that cause is permanent and eternal—which is the only reality, while the lights themselves are but a passing illusion.

ENQUIRER. All this does not explain to me how this illusion called the universe originates; how the conscious "to be" proceeds to manifest itself from the unconsciousness that "is."

THEOSOPHIST. It is "unconsciousness" only to our finite consciousness. We may truly paraphrase verse 5 in the 1st chapter of St. John and say, "and (Absolute) light (which appears as darkness to us) shines in darkness (which is our illusory material light); and the darkness comprehends it not." <note>original: "Et lux in tenebris lucet, et tenebrae eam non comprehenderunt."</note> This absolute light is also absolute and immutable law. Whether by radiation or emanation—we need not quarrel over terms—the universe passes out of its homogeneous subjectivity <note>A state where all things are of the same nature and have not yet taken individual form.</note> onto the first plane of manifestation. We are taught that there are seven such planes. With each plane, the universe becomes more dense and material until it reaches this, our plane. On this level, the only world whose physical composition is approximately known and understood by Science is the planetary or Solar system—one that is *unique unto itself* <note>original: "sui generis"</note>, we are told.

ENQUIRER. What do you mean by *unique unto itself*?

THEOSOPHIST. I mean that, although the fundamental law and the universal workings of the laws of Nature are uniform, our Solar system—like every other such system among the millions of others in the Cosmos—and even our Earth, has its own specific program of manifestation.

<summary>The Theosophist explains that the material universe is a passing illusion emanating from an absolute, eternal cause, moving through seven planes of increasing density.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, reality vs illusion, consciousness, Absolute Light, planes of manifestation, Solar system, cosmology, St. John's Gospel</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the diversity of life and consciousness throughout the universe, challenging the human-centric view of extraterrestrial life.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

manifestations differing from the respective programs of all others. We speak of the inhabitants of other planets and imagine that if they are *men*—that is, thinking entities—they must be as we are. The fancy of poets, painters, and sculptors never fails to represent even the angels as a beautiful copy of man, plus wings. We say that all this is an error and a delusion; because, if on this little earth alone one finds such a diversity in its flora, fauna, and mankind—from the seaweed to the cedar of Lebanon, from the jellyfish to the elephant, from the Bushman <note>A term formerly used to describe the San people of Southern Africa; used here by the author to represent one end of a perceived human spectrum.</note> and negro to the Apollo Belvedere <note>A famous ancient Greek statue representing the ideal of physical beauty in the Western tradition.</note>—then if you alter the cosmic and planetary conditions, there must be as a result quite a different flora, fauna, and mankind. 

The same laws will fashion quite a different set of things and beings even on this our plane, including all our planets. How much more different, then, must external nature be in other solar systems? How foolish it is to judge other *stars*, worlds, and human beings by our own, as physical science does!

**ENQUIRER:** But what are your data for this assertion?

**THEOSOPHIST:** What science in general will never accept as proof: the cumulative testimony of an endless series of Seers who have testified to this fact. Their spiritual visions—real explorations conducted by and through physical and spiritual senses untrammeled by blind flesh—were systematically checked and compared with one another, and their nature sifted. All that was not corroborated by unanimous and collective experience was rejected. Only that was recorded as established truth which, in various ages, under different climates, and throughout an untold series of incessant observations, was found to agree and receive confirmation.

<summary>The author argues that life on other planets must be vastly different from human life due to varying cosmic conditions and asserts that Theosophical knowledge is based on the verified, collective visions of generations of Seers.</summary>
<keywords>extraterrestrial life, anthropocentrism, Seers, spiritual vision, diversity of nature, planetary conditions, scientific proof</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between an Enquirer and a Theosophist regarding the scientific methodology of Theosophy and its reliance on spiritual observation.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Our scholars and students of the psycho-spiritual sciences constantly find further corroboration for their findings. As you can see, the methods they use do not differ from those used by students of the natural and physical sciences. The only difference is that our fields of research exist on two different planes, and our instruments are not made by human hands; for that reason, perhaps, they are actually more reliable. The retorts, accumulators, and microscopes of the chemist and naturalist may malfunction; the telescope and the astronomer’s time-keeping instruments <note>original: "horological instruments"</note> may be damaged; but our recording instruments are beyond the influence of weather or the elements.

ENQ. And therefore you have implicit faith in them?

THEO. "Faith" is a word not to be found in theosophical dictionaries. Instead, we speak of *knowledge based on observation and experience*. There is this difference, however: the observation and experience of physical science lead Scientists to nearly as many "working" hypotheses as there are minds to create them. In contrast, our *knowledge* permits us to add to our lore only those facts which have become undeniable, and which are fully and absolutely demonstrated. We do not hold two different beliefs or hypotheses on the same subject.

ENQ. Is it based on such data that you came to accept the strange theories we find in *Esoteric Buddhism*? <note>A seminal 1883 book by A.P. Sinnett which first introduced many Theosophical concepts to the Western public.</note>

THEO. Exactly. These theories may be slightly incorrect in their minor details, and even faulty in how they are explained by lay students; nevertheless, they are *facts* in nature and come nearer to the truth than any scientific hypothesis.

<summary>The Theosophist explains that their spiritual research follows a rigorous scientific method based on observation rather than faith, claiming their "spiritual instruments" are more reliable than physical ones.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, psycho-spiritual sciences, scientific method, Esoteric Buddhism, A.P. Sinnett, observation vs. faith, spiritual instruments</keywords>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

### ON THE SEVENFOLD CONSTITUTION OF OUR PLANET

**ENQUIRER:** I understand that you describe our Earth as forming part of a chain of earths?

**THEOSOPHIST:** We do. But the other six "earths," or globes, are not on the same plane of objectivity <note>original: "plane of objectivity"; meaning the physical level of existence that can be perceived by the senses</note> as our Earth is; therefore, we cannot see them.

**ENQUIRER:** Is that because of their great distance?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not at all, for we see planets and even stars at immeasurably greater distances with our naked eye. Instead, it is because those six globes are outside our physical means of perception, or our plane of being. It is not only that their material density, weight, or fabric are entirely different from those of our Earth and the other known planets; they are also (to us) on an entirely different *layer* of space, so to speak—a layer that cannot be perceived or felt by our physical senses. 

When I say "layer," please do not let your imagination suggest layers like geological strata or beds laid one over the other, for this would only lead to another absurd misconception. What I mean by "layer" is that plane of infinite space which, by its nature, cannot be detected by our ordinary waking perceptions, whether mental or physical. It exists in nature outside of our normal mentality or consciousness, outside of our three-dimensional space, and outside of our division of time. Each of the seven fundamental planes (or layers) in space—viewed as a whole, as the "pure space" of John Locke's definition <note>Locke defined space as the relation of distance between bodies, distinct from the matter that fills it</note>, rather than our...

<summary>The author explains the Theosophical concept of the "planetary chain," arguing that Earth is one of seven globes existing on different planes of reality that are imperceptible to normal human senses.</summary>
<keywords>Septenary Constitution, Planetary Chain, Planes of Existence, Perception, Dimensions of Space, John Locke, Consciousness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of different planes of existence and the states of human consciousness.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

finite space—has its own objectivity and subjectivity, its own space and time, its own consciousness and set of senses. But all this will be hardly comprehensible to one trained in modern ways of thought.

**ENQUIRER.** What do you mean by a different set of senses? Is there anything on our human plane <note>The physical world or level of reality we currently inhabit.</note> that you could bring as an illustration of what you say, just to give a clearer idea of what you may mean by this variety of senses, spaces, and respective perceptions?

**THEOSOPHIST.** None; except, perhaps, that which for Science would be rather a handy peg on which to hang a counter-argument. We have a different set of senses in dream-life, have we not? We feel, talk, hear, see, taste, and function in general on a different plane; the change of state of our consciousness being evidenced by the fact that a series of acts and events embracing years, as we think, pass ideally through our mind in one instant. Well, that extreme rapidity of our mental operations in dreams, and the perfect naturalness, for the time being, of all the other functions, show us that we are on quite another plane. Our philosophy teaches us that, as there are seven fundamental forces in nature, and seven planes of being, so there are seven states of consciousness in which man can live, think, remember, and have his being <note>original: "live, think, remember and have his being" — a reference to the biblical phrase "live and move and have our being" (Acts 17:28), here applied to the various states of the soul.</note>. To enumerate these here is impossible, and for this one has to turn to the study of Eastern metaphysics. But in these two states—the waking and the dreaming—every ordinary mortal, from a learned philosopher down to a poor untutored savage, has a good proof that such states differ.

**ENQUIRER.** You do not accept, then, the well-known explanations of biology and physiology to account for the dream state?

<summary>The author uses the experience of dreaming to illustrate how consciousness can function on different planes with different perceptions of time and sense. It introduces the Theosophical concept of seven planes of being and seven corresponding states of human consciousness.</summary>

THEOSOPHIST. We do not. We reject even the hypotheses of your psychologists, preferring the teachings of Eastern Wisdom. Believing in seven planes of cosmic <note>original: "Kosmic"</note> being and states of consciousness with regard to the Universe or the Macrocosm <note>The "great world" or the universe as a whole</note>, we stop at the fourth plane, finding it impossible to go beyond with any degree of certainty. But with respect to the Microcosm <note>The "little world," referring here to man as a reflection of the universe</note>, or man, we speculate freely on his seven states and principles.

ENQUIRER. How do you explain these?

THEOSOPHIST. We find, first of all, two distinct beings in man: the spiritual and the physical; the man who thinks, and the man who records as much of these thoughts as he is able to assimilate. Therefore, we divide him into two distinct natures: the upper or spiritual being, composed of three "principles" or *aspects*; and the lower or physical quaternary <note>A set of four</note>, composed of *four*—in all, *seven*.

### THE SEVENFOLD NATURE OF MAN.

ENQUIRER. Is it what we call Spirit and Soul, and the man of flesh?

THEOSOPHIST. It is not. That is the old Platonic division. Plato was an Initiate <note>One who has been admitted into secret religious or philosophical knowledge</note>, and therefore could not go into forbidden details; but he who is acquainted with the ancient <note>original: "archaic"</note> doctrine finds the seven in Plato’s various combinations of Soul and Spirit. He regarded man as constituted of two parts—one eternal, formed of the same essence as the Absolute, the other mortal and corruptible, deriving its constituent parts from the *minor* "created" Gods. Man is composed, he shows, of (1) A mortal...

<summary>The Theosophist explains that man is a sevenfold being divided into a spiritual triad and a physical quaternary, contrasting this with the simpler Platonic division of body, soul, and spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Septenary nature of man, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Plato, spiritual principles, quaternary, Eastern Wisdom, Theosophy</keywords>

body, (2) An immortal principle, and (3) A “separate mortal kind of Soul.” It is that which we respectively call the physical man, the Spiritual Soul or Spirit, and the animal Soul (the *Mind* <note>original: "Nous"</note> and *soul* <note>original: "psyche"</note>). This is the division adopted by Paul, another Initiate, who maintains that there is a psychical body which is sown in the corruptible (the astral soul or body), and a *spiritual* body that is raised in incorruptible substance. Even James (iii. 15) corroborates the same by saying that the “wisdom” (of our lower soul) does not descend from above, but is terrestrial (“psychical,” “demoniacal,” <note>original: "vide Greek text"</note>); while the other is heavenly wisdom. Now it is so plain that Plato and even Pythagoras, while speaking of only three “principles,” give them seven separate functions in their various combinations, that if we contrast our teachings, this will become quite clear. Let us take a brief view of these seven aspects by drawing two tables.

Now, what does Plato teach? He speaks of the *interior man* as being constituted of two parts—one immutable and always the same, formed of the same substance as Deity, and the other mortal and corruptible. These "two parts" are found in our upper *Triad* <note>The three higher principles: Atma, Buddhi, and Manas.</note> and the lower *Quaternary* <note>The four lower, mortal principles; see the previous table.</note>. He explains that when the Soul, *psuche* <note>Greek: ψυχή; the lower or emotional soul.</note>, "allies herself to the *Nous* <note>Greek: νοῦς; the higher mind or divine intellect.</note>...

\* In Mr. Sinnett’s *Esoteric Buddhism*, principles *d*, *e*, and *f* are respectively called the Animal, the Human, and the Spiritual Souls, which serves the purpose just as well. Although the principles in *Esoteric Buddhism* are numbered, this is, strictly speaking, useless. Only the dual *Monad* (*Atma-Buddhi*) can be thought of as the two highest numbers (the 6th and 7th). As for all the others, no general rule for numbering is possible, since only that "principle" which is predominant in a person should be considered the first and foremost. In some men, it is the higher Intelligence (*Manas*, or the 5th principle) which dominates the rest; in others, the Animal Soul (*Kama-rupa*) reigns supreme, exhibiting the most bestial instincts, and so on.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

(divine spirit or substance\*), she does everything correctly and successfully"; but the case is different when she attaches herself to *Anoia* <note>original: "Anoia"; Greek for folly or lack of understanding</note>, (folly, or the irrational animal Soul). Here, then, we have *Manas* <note>The Mind or thinking faculty</note> (or the Soul in general) in its two aspects: when attaching itself to *Anoia* (our *Kama rupa* <note>The "desire body" or seat of animal passions</note>, or the "Animal Soul" in "Esoteric Buddhism,") it moves toward entire annihilation, as far as the personal Ego is concerned. However, when allying itself to the *Nous* <note>The higher divine mind or Atma-Buddhi</note> it merges into the immortal, imperishable Ego; then its spiritual consciousness of the person that *was* becomes immortal.

## THE DISTINCTION BETWEEN SOUL AND SPIRIT.

ENQUIRER: Do you really teach—as you are accused of doing by some Spiritualists and French Spiritists—the annihilation of every personality?

THEOSOPHIST: We do not. But this question involves the duality of the human being—the individuality of the Divine Ego versus the personality of the human animal. It also involves the question of whether the real immortal Ego can appear in *Séance* rooms as a "materialized spirit." We deny this possibility, as already explained, and because of this, our opponents have started this nonsensical charge of "annihilation."

<margin>\* Paul <note>The Apostle Paul</note> calls Plato's *Nous* "Spirit"; but as this spirit is "substance," then, of course, *Buddhi* <note>The spiritual soul</note> and not *Atma* <note>The pure universal spirit</note> is meant, as the latter cannot philosophically be called "substance" under any circumstance. We include *Atma* among the human "principles" <note>The constituent parts of a human being in Theosophical doctrine</note> in order not to create additional confusion. In reality, it is not "human" but the universal absolute principle of which Buddhi, the Soul-Spirit, is the vehicle.</margin>

<summary>This page explains the dual nature of the Mind (Manas), which can either descend into animal folly or ascend to divine immortality, and clarifies that Theosophy does not teach the total annihilation of personality.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

**ENQUIRER:** You have just spoken of the *psyche* <note>original: "psyche"; here referring to the lower, emotional soul</note> running towards its entire annihilation if it attaches itself to *Anoia* <note>original: "Anoia"; Greek for "mindlessness" or "folly"</note>. What did Plato mean by this, and what do you mean?

**THEOSOPHIST:** I think it refers to the entire annihilation of the personal consciousness as an exceptional and rare case. The general and almost invariable rule is the merging of the personal consciousness into the individual or immortal consciousness of the Ego. This is a transformation or a divine transfiguration. In this process, only the lower *quaternary* <note>The four lower elements of a human being in Theosophical doctrine: the physical body, life force, astral body, and animal soul</note> is entirely annihilated. Would you expect the man of flesh, or the *temporary personality*—his shadow, the "astral" body, his animal instincts, and even physical life—to survive the "spiritual Ego" and become everlasting? Naturally, all of this ceases to exist, either at or soon after physical death. In time, it becomes entirely disintegrated and disappears from view, being annihilated as a whole.

**ENQUIRER:** Then you also reject the *resurrection of the flesh*?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Most decidedly we do! Why should we, who believe in the ancient, inner philosophy of the Ancients, accept the unphilosophical speculations of later Christian theology? Those ideas were merely borrowed from the outer, public systems of the Egyptian and Greek Gnostics.

**ENQUIRER:** The Egyptians revered nature spirits and even deified onions; your Hindus are *idolaters* to this day; the Zoroastrians worshipped, and still do worship, the sun; and the best Greek philosophers were either dreamers or materialists—look at Plato and Democritus. How can you compare your views to theirs?

**THEOSOPHIST:** It may seem that way in your modern Christian or even scientific teachings, but it is not so for unbiased minds. The Egyptians revered the "One-Only-One" as *Nout* <note>An Egyptian personification of the sky or the primordial cosmic waters</note>; and it is from this...

<summary>The Theosophist explains that while the lower personality and physical elements are annihilated after death, the spiritual Ego remains immortal; the text also rejects the literal resurrection of the body.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

It was from this word that Anaxagoras <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BCE) who was the first to bring philosophy to Athens.</note> earned his title *Nous*, or as he calls it, *Self-potent Mind*, the *leading motor of movement*, or the *first mover* <note>original: "primum-mobile"</note> of all things. For him, the *Mind* <note>original: "Nous"</note> was God, and the *Word* <note>original: "logos"</note> was man, his emanation. The *Mind* is the spirit (whether in the Universe <note>original: "Kosmos"</note> or in man), and the *Word*, whether the Universe or the astral body, is the emanation of the former; the physical body is merely the animal. Our external powers perceive *phenomena* <note>observable events or appearances</note>; our *Mind* alone is able to recognize their *noumena* <note>the underlying reality or "thing-in-itself" behind an appearance</note>. It is the *Word* alone, or the *noumenon*, that survives, because it is immortal in its very nature and essence. The *Word* in man is the Eternal Ego, that which reincarnates and lasts forever. But how can the vanishing external shadow—the temporary clothing of that divine Emanation which returns to the source from which it came—be that which is raised in *incorruptibility*?

ENQUIRER: Still, you can hardly escape the charge of having invented a new division of man’s spiritual and psychic constituents; for no philosopher speaks of them in this way, even though you believe that Plato does.

THEOSOPHIST: And I maintain that view. Besides Plato, there is Pythagoras, who also followed the same idea. He described the *Soul* as a self-moving Unit <note>original: "monad"</note> composed of three elements, the—

<margin>“Plato and Pythagoras,” says Plutarch, “divide the soul into two parts, the rational <note>original: "noëtic"</note> and the irrational <note>original: "agnoia"</note>; they hold that the part of the human soul which is rational is eternal. For though it is not God itself, it is the product of an eternal deity; but that part of the soul which is stripped of reason <note>original: "agnoia"</note> dies.” The modern term *Agnostic* comes from *Agnosis*, a related word. We wonder why Mr. Huxley, the creator of the word, would connect his great intellect with “the soul stripped of reason” which dies? Is it the exaggerated humility of the modern materialist?</margin>

<summary>The author uses the philosophies of Anaxagoras, Pythagoras, and Plato to argue that the human spirit (Nous) is an eternal emanation of the divine, while the lower, irrational soul is temporary and perishes. It challenges the modern Agnostic position by linking the term's etymology to the "irrational" or "dying" part of the soul.</summary>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY</header>

*Nous* (Spirit), the *phren* (mind), and the *thumos* (life, breath, or the *Nephesh* <note>original: "Nephesh"; a Hebrew term for the "living soul" or animal vitality</note> of the Kabbalists); these three correspond to our "Atma-Buddhi" (higher Spirit-Soul), to *Manas* (the Ego), and to *Kama-rupa* <note>original: "Kama-rupa"; the "desire-body" or seat of animal passions</note> in conjunction with the lower reflection of *Manas*. That which the Ancient Greek philosophers termed *Soul*, in general, we call Spirit, or *Spiritual Soul*, *Buddhi*, as the vehicle of *Atma* (the *Agathon* <note>original: "Agathon"; Greek for "The Good," referring to Plato’s Supreme Deity</note>). 

The fact that Pythagoras and others state that *phren* and *thumos* are shared by us with the brutes <note>meaning animals</note> proves that in this case the lower Manasic reflection (instinct) and *Kama-rupa* (animal living passions) are meant. And as Socrates and Plato accepted this clue and followed it, if to these five—namely, *Agathon* (Deity or Atma), *Psuche* (Soul in its collective sense), *Nous* (Spirit or Mind), *Phren* (physical mind), and *Thumos* (passions)—we add the *eidolon* <note>original: "eidolon"; an image or phantom</note> of the Mysteries, the shadowy form or the human double, and the *physical body*, it will be easy to demonstrate that the ideas of both Pythagoras and Plato were identical with ours. 

Even the Egyptians held to the Septenary <note>seven-fold</note> division. They taught that in its exit, the Soul (Ego) had to pass through its seven chambers, or principles: those it left behind, and those it took along with itself. The only difference is that, ever bearing in mind the penalty of revealing Mystery-doctrines—which was *death*—they gave out the teaching in a broad outline, while we elaborate it and explain it in its details. But though we do give out to the world as much as is lawful, even in our doctrine more than one important detail is withheld; these are details which those who study the esoteric <note>inner or secret</note> philosophy and are pledged to silence *are alone* entitled to know.

<summary>The author aligns Theosophical concepts of the seven-fold human nature with the philosophical systems of Pythagoras, Plato, and the Ancient Egyptians. It argues that while ancient civilizations kept these details secret under penalty of death, modern Theosophy provides a more detailed, though still partially veiled, explanation.</summary>

## THE GREEK TEACHINGS.

ENQUIRER: We have magnificent Greek and Latin, Sanskrit and Hebrew scholars. How is it that we find nothing in their translations that would afford us a clue to what you say?

THEOSOPHIST: Because your translators, despite their great learning, have turned the philosophers—the Greeks especially—into misty instead of mystic writers. Take Plutarch as an example, and read what he says regarding the "principles" of man. His descriptions were taken literally and attributed to metaphysical superstition and ignorance. Let me give you an illustration of this: "Man," says Plutarch, "is a compound; and they are *mistaken who think him to be compounded of two parts only*. For they imagine that the understanding <note>meaning the brain intellect</note> is a part of the soul <note>the upper Triad</note>, but they err in this no less than those who make the soul to be a part of the body—that is, those who make the <term>Triad</term> <note>the three higher, immortal principles of man</note> part of the corruptible, mortal <term>quaternary</term> <note>the four lower, physical and emotional principles</note>. For the understanding (<gloss>nous</gloss> <note>original Greek: νοῦς</note>) as far exceeds the soul, as the soul is better and more divine than the body. Now this composition of the soul (<gloss>psyche</gloss> <note>original Greek: ψυχή</note>) with the understanding (<gloss>nous</gloss>) makes reason; and with the body (or <gloss>thumos</gloss>, the animal soul) it makes passion; of which the one is the beginning or principle of pleasure and pain, and the other of virtue and vice. Of these three parts joined and compacted together, the earth has given the body, the moon the soul, and the sun the understanding for the generation of man."

This last sentence is purely allegorical, and will be compre— <note>text continues on next page</note>

<summary>The Theosophist argues that modern scholars misinterpret ancient Greek philosophy as superstition, using a passage from Plutarch to demonstrate the distinction between the physical body, the animal soul, and the divine intellect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the human constitution and the process of death, comparing Theosophical teachings with the philosophy of Plutarch and ancient Greek mysteries.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

This can be understood only by those who are versed in the esoteric science of correspondences <note>The study of how different levels of reality, such as planets and human faculties, relate to one another.</note> and know which planet is related to *every principle*. Plutarch divides these principles into three groups. He describes the body as a compound of the physical frame, the astral shadow, and the breath—the triple lower part which "was taken from earth and returns to earth." The middle principle and the instinctual soul <note>The *Kama-rupa* or desire-body in Theosophical terms.</note> make up the second part, derived *from and through* and ever influenced by the moon. Finally, only the higher part, or the *Spiritual Soul*—containing the Atmic and Manasic elements <note>Atma (Spirit) and Manas (Mind).</note>—is considered by him to be a direct emanation of the Sun, who represents *Agathon* <note>original: *Agathon*; the Supreme Good or Deity.</note>. This is proven by what he says further:

> "Now of the deaths we die, the one makes man two out of three, and the other makes him one out of two. The former occurs in the region and jurisdiction of Demeter; for this reason, the name given to the Mysteries, *teleo* <note>original: *τελεω*; meaning to complete, initiate, or die.</note>, resembled the name given to death, *teleutan*. The Athenians also formerly called the deceased 'sacred to Demeter.' As for the other death, it takes place in the moon or the region of Persephone."

Here you have our doctrine, which shows that man is a *septenary* <note>A sevenfold being.</note> during life; a *quintile* <note>A fivefold being, after the lower two elements have shed.</note> just after death in *Kamaloka* <note>The "place of desire," a semi-material plane where the soul is purged of earthly passions.</note>; and a threefold *Ego*—consisting of Spirit, Soul, and consciousness—in *Devachan* <note>The "dwelling of the gods," a blissful state of rest between incarnations.</note>. This separation—first in the "Meadows of Hades," as Plutarch calls *Kamaloka*, and then in *Devachan*—was a core part of the performances during the sacred Mysteries. In these rites, the candidates for initiation enacted the entire drama of death and the resurrection as a glorified spirit.

<margin>The Kabbalists, who know the relationship between Jehovah (the giver of life and children) and the Moon, as well as the Moon's influence on reproduction, will understand this point as clearly as some astrologers will.</margin>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

By this, I mean *Consciousness*. This is what Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 119 AD) was a Greek philosopher and biographer whose works are often cited in Theosophy for their mystical insights.</note> means when he says:

<margin>* Proserpina, or Persephone, represents here the post-mortem <term>Karma</term> (the law of cause and effect after death), which is said to regulate the separation of the lower from the higher "principles": the *Soul*, as *Nephesh* <note>original: "Nephesh" — a Hebrew term for the animal soul or breath of life</note>, which remains for a time in <term>Kama-loka</term> (the desire-realm), from the higher compound *Ego*, which enters the state of <term>Devachan</term>, or bliss.</margin>

<margin>† Until the separation of the higher, spiritual "principle" takes place from the lower ones, which remain in the Kama-loka until they disintegrate.</margin>

> "And as with the one, the terrestrial, so with the other celestial Hermes does dwell. This one suddenly and with violence plucks the soul from the body; but Proserpina mildly and over a long time disjoins the understanding from the soul.* For this reason she is called *Monogenes*, 'only begotten,' or rather *begetting one alone*; for the better part of man *becomes alone when it is separated by her*. Now both the one and the other happens in this way according to nature. It is ordained by Fate (*Fatum* <note>original Latin: "Fatum" — destiny or fate</note> or Karma) that every soul, whether with or without understanding (mind), when it has gone out of the body, should wander for a time—though not all for the same length of time—in the region lying between the earth and moon (Kama-loka).† For those who have been unjust and dissolute then suffer the punishment due to their offenses; but the good and virtuous are detained there until they are purified. Through expiation, they have purged out of them all the infections they might have contracted from the contagion of the body, as if from foul health. They live in the mildest part of the air, called the Meadows of Hades, where they must remain for a certain prefixed and appointed time. And then, as if they were returning from a wandering pilgrimage or a long exile back to their own country, they have a taste of joy—such as those principally receive who are initiated into the Sacred Mysteries—mixed with trouble, admiration, and each person's proper and peculiar hope."

This is Nirvanic bliss, and no Theosophist could describe in plainer, though <term>esoteric</term> (hidden) language, the mental joys of Devachan. In this state, every person has his paradise around him, built by his own consciousness. But you must beware of the general error—

<summary>This page uses the writings of Plutarch to explain the Theosophical concept of the soul's journey after death, specifically the separation of the higher mind from the animal soul in the state of Devachan.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

This is a mistake into which too many even of our Theosophists fall. Do not imagine that because man is called septenary <note>sevenfold</note>, then *quintuple* <note>fivefold</note> and a triad <note>threefold</note>, he is a compound of seven, five, or three distinct *entities*; or, as well expressed by a Theosophical writer, of skins to be peeled off like the skins of an onion. The "principles," as already said—excepting the body, the life-force, and the astral *image* <note>original: *eidolon*; the phantom or double of the physical body</note>, all of which disperse at death—are simply aspects and states of *consciousness*. 

There is but one real man, enduring through the cycle of life and immortal in essence, if not in form, and this is *Manas*, the Mind-man or embodied Consciousness. The objection made by the materialists, who deny the possibility of mind and consciousness acting without matter, is worthless in our case. We do not deny the soundness of their argument; but we simply ask our opponents, "Are you acquainted with all the *states of matter*, you who until now knew of only three? And how do you know whether that which we refer to as ABSOLUTE CONSCIOUSNESS or Deity—forever invisible and unknowable—is not that which, though it forever eludes our human *finite conception*, is still universal Spirit-matter or matter-Spirit in its *absolute infinitude*?" It is, then, one of the lowest aspects of this Spirit-matter—and one that is *divided* during its manifestations in the active universe <note>original: manvantaric; referring to a Manvantara, a period of cosmic activity</note>—which is the conscious *Ego* that creates its own paradise; a fool's paradise, it may be, but still a state of bliss.

**Enquirer:** But what is *Devachan*?

**Theosophist:** Literally, it is the "land of the gods"; a condition, a state of mental bliss. Philosophically, it is a mental condition analogous to, but far more vivid and real than, the most vivid dream. It is the state after death for most mortals.

<summary>The author clarifies that the "seven principles" of man are states of consciousness rather than physical layers, and introduces Devachan as a vivid, blissful post-death mental state.</summary>

# VII.

## ON THE VARIOUS POST-MORTEM STATES.
<note>original: "Post Mortem"; refers to states of existence after death.</note>

### *THE PHYSICAL AND THE SPIRITUAL MAN.*

**ENQUIRER.** I am glad to hear you believe in the immortality of the Soul.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Not of “the Soul,” but of the divine Spirit; or rather, in the immortality of the re-incarnating Ego.

**ENQUIRER.** What is the difference?

**THEOSOPHIST.** A very great one in our philosophy, but this is too profound and difficult a question to touch upon lightly. We shall have to analyze them separately, and then in conjunction. We may begin with Spirit. 

We say that the Spirit—the “Father in secret” mentioned by Jesus—or <term>Atman</term> <note>The highest spiritual self in Hindu and Theosophical philosophy.</note>, is not the individual property of any one person. Instead, it is the Divine essence which has no body and no form; it is weightless, invisible, and indivisible. It is that which does not *exist* (in a material sense) and yet *is*, just as the Buddhists say of <term>Nirvana</term> <note>The state of liberation from the cycle of suffering and rebirth.</note>. It only overshadows the mortal person. That which actually enters into a human and pervades the whole body is merely its omnipresent rays, or light, radiated through <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The faculty of spiritual intuition or the "Spiritual Soul."</note>, which is its vehicle and direct emanation. This is the...

<summary>The author distinguishes between the "Soul" and the "re-incarnating Ego," defining the Spirit (Atman) as a universal divine essence that overshadows the individual rather than being personal property.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

This is the secret meaning behind the assertions of almost all the ancient philosophers when they said that the “*rational* part of man’s soul” <note>In its generic sense, the word “rational” refers to something emanating from the Eternal Wisdom.</note> never entered wholly into the man, but only overshadowed him more or less through the *irrational* spiritual Soul or *Buddhi*. <note>“Irrational” in the sense that, as a pure emanation of the Universal Mind, it can have no individual reason of its own on this plane of matter. Just as the Moon borrows her light from the Sun and her life from the Earth, so *Buddhi*, receiving its light of Wisdom from *Atma*, gets its rational qualities from *Manas*. In itself <note>original: *Per se*</note>, as something homogeneous, it is devoid of attributes.</note>

**ENQUIRER:** I was under the impression that the “Animal Soul” alone was irrational, not the Divine Soul.

Man is a correlation of spiritual powers, as well as a correlation of chemical and physical forces, brought into function by what we call “principles.”

**ENQUIRER:** I have read a good deal upon the subject, and it seems to me that the notions of the older philosophers differed a great deal from those of the medieval Kabalists <note>Followers of the Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism that explores the nature of the divine and the universe.</note>, though they do agree in some particulars.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

<margin>The relationship between the Spirit and the astral body.</margin>
Regarding the astral principles, the Kabalists <note>original: "Kabalists"; followers of the Jewish mystical tradition known as Kabbalah</note> maintain that the human Spirit, detaching itself from the ocean of light and Universal Spirit, enters man’s Soul, where it remains throughout life imprisoned in the astral capsule. All Christian Kabalists still maintain the same, as they are unable to break quite loose from their anthropomorphic <note>human-centered or attributing human form to God</note> and Biblical doctrines.

**ENQUIRER:** And what do you say?

**THEOSOPHIST:** We say that we only allow the presence of the radiation of Spirit (or Atma <note>The highest principle in man; the pure self</note>) in the astral capsule, and only so far as that spiritual radiance is concerned. We say that man and the Soul have to conquer their immortality by ascending towards the unity with which, if successful, they will be finally linked and into which they are finally, so to speak, absorbed. The individualization of man after death depends on the spirit, not on his soul and body. Although the word “personality,” in the sense in which it is usually understood, is an absurdity if applied literally to our immortal essence, still the latter is—as our individual Ego—a distinct entity, immortal and eternal, in itself <note>original: "per se"</note>. *It is only in the case of black magicians or of criminals beyond redemption—criminals who have been such during a long series of lives—that the shining thread, which links the spirit to the personal soul from the moment of the birth of the child, is violently snapped. In these cases, the disembodied entity becomes divorced from the personal soul, and the latter is annihilated without leaving the smallest impression of itself on the former.* If that union between the lower, or personal Manas <note>The mind or thinking faculty</note>, and the individual reincarnating Ego has not been achieved during life, then the former is left to share the fate of the lower principles.

<summary>The author explains that immortality is not guaranteed but must be earned through the union of the personal soul with the eternal Spirit, warning that extreme wickedness can sever this connection and lead to the soul's annihilation.</summary>

animals, to gradually dissolve into ether, and have its personality annihilated. But even then the Ego remains a distinct being. It (the spiritual Ego) only loses one Devachanic state <note>Devachan: a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note>—after that special, and in that case indeed useless, life—as that idealized *Personality*, and is reincarnated almost immediately after enjoying its freedom as a planetary spirit for a short time.

ENQUIRER: It is stated in *Isis Unveiled* <note>A major work by H.P. Blavatsky published in 1877</note> that such planetary Spirits or Angels, “the gods of the Pagans or the Archangels of the Christians,” will never be men on our planet.

THEOSOPHIST: Quite right. Not “such,” but *some* classes of higher Planetary Spirits. They will never be men on this planet because they are liberated Spirits from a previous, earlier world, and as such they cannot become men again on this one. Yet all these will live again in the next and far higher Great Manvantara <note>original: Mahamanvantara; a vast period of cosmic activity</note>, after this “Great Age” and the “Dissolution of Brahma” <note>original: Brahma pralaya; a period of cosmic rest lasting approximately 311 trillion years, or "16 figures"</note> is over. For you must have heard, of course, that Eastern philosophy teaches us that mankind consists of such “Spirits” imprisoned in human bodies? The difference between animals and men is this: the former are ensouled by the “principles” *potentially*, while the latter are ensouled by them *actually*. Do you understand the difference now?

ENQUIRER: Yes; but this specialization has been the stumbling block of philosophers <note>original: metaphysicians</note> in all ages.

THEOSOPHIST: It was. The whole inner teaching <note>original: esotericism</note> of the Buddhist philosophy is based on this mysterious teaching, understood by so few.

->* See “*The Secret Doctrine*,” Vol. II, stanzas.<-

Both the *human Spirit* (or the individuality), the reincarnating Spiritual Ego, and Buddhi, the Spiritual soul, are pre-existent. Both were originally formed from the Eternal Ocean of light; but as the Fire-Philosophers <note>A term for 16th and 17th-century Hermeticists and Alchemists</note>, the medieval Theosophists, expressed it, there is a visible as well as invisible spirit in fire. Empedocles firmly believed all men and animals to possess two souls; and in Aristotle we find that he calls one the reasoning soul, *mind* <note>original: νοῦς (nous)</note>, and the other, the animal soul, *breath of life* <note>original: ψυχή (psyche)</note>. According to these philosophers, the reasoning soul comes from *within* the universal soul, and the other from *without*.

That matter, we say, is co-eternal with Spirit, and is not our visible, tangible, and divisible matter, but its extreme refinement <note>original: sublimation</note>. Pure Spirit is but one remove from the *non-Spirit*, or the *absolute all*. Unless you admit that man was evolved out of this primordial Spirit-matter, and represents a regular

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

<center>*THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY*</center>

...progressive scale of "principles" from *meta*-Spirit <note>original: "meta-Spirit"; referring to that which is beyond or above spirit</note> down to the grossest matter, how can we ever come to regard the *inner* man as immortal, and at the same time as a spiritual Entity and a mortal man?

ENQUIRER: Then why should you not believe in God as such an Entity?

THEOSOPHIST: Because that which is infinite and unconditioned can have no form, and cannot be a being—at least not in any Eastern philosophy worthy of the name. An "entity" is immortal, but it is only so in its ultimate essence, not in its individual form. When it reaches the last point of its cycle, it is absorbed into its primordial <note>original: "primordial"; existing from the very beginning</note> nature; it becomes spirit, and at that point, it loses its name of "Entity."

Its immortality as a form is limited only to its life-cycle or the *Mahamanvantara* <note>original: "Mahamanvantara"; a vast period of manifestation or a "Great Day of Brahma" lasting billions of years</note>; after which it is one and identical with the Universal Spirit, and no longer a separate Entity. As to the *personal Soul*—by which we mean the spark of consciousness that preserves in the Spiritual Ego the idea of the personal "I" of the last incarnation—this lasts, as a separate, distinct recollection, only throughout the Devachanic period <note>original: "Devachanic period"; the blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note>. After that time, it is added to the series of other innumerable incarnations of the Ego, much like the remembrance in our memory of one specific day out of a whole year. Will you bind the infinitude you claim for your God to finite conditions? That alone which is indissolubly cemented by *Atma* (that is, *Buddhi-Manas* <note>The union of the spiritual soul and the higher mind</note>) is immortal. The Soul of man (that is, of the personality) *in itself* <note>original: "per se"</note> is neither immortal, eternal, nor divine. As the *Zohar* <note>A foundational work of Jewish Kabbalah</note> says (vol. iii., p. 616), "the soul, when sent to this earth, puts on an earthly garment to preserve herself here, so she..."

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

*Nephesh* <note>original: "nephesh"; a Hebrew term often translated as "soul," but which Theosophists argue refers to the lower, animal vitality</note>, that which God breathes into Adam (Genesis chapter 2), is often mistaken for the immortal soul. Here are some instances: "And God created... every *nephesh* (life) that moves" (Genesis 1:21), meaning animals; and (Genesis 2:7) it is said: "And man became a *nephesh*" (living soul), which shows that the word *nephesh* was applied indifferently to immortal man and to mortal beast. "And surely your blood of your *nepheshim* (lives) will I require; at the hand of every beast will I require it, and at the hand of man" (Genesis 9:5). "Escape for *nephesh*" (translated as "escape for your life") (Genesis 19:17). "Let us not kill him," reads the English version (Genesis 37:21). "Let us not kill his *nephesh*," is the Hebrew text. "*Nephesh* for *nephesh*," says Leviticus (17:8). "He that kills any man shall surely be put to death," literally "He that smites the *nephesh* of a man" (Leviticus 24:17); and from verse 18 and following it reads: "And he that kills a beast (*nephesh*) shall make it good... Beast for beast," whereas the original text has it "*nephesh* for *nephesh*." How could man kill that which is immortal? And this explains also why the Sadducees <note>A Jewish sect during the Second Temple period known for denying the resurrection of the dead</note> denied the immortality of the soul, as it also affords another proof that very probably the Mosaic Jews—the uninitiated at any rate—never believed in the soul’s survival at all.

### ON ETERNAL REWARD AND PUNISHMENT; AND ON NIRVANA.

**ENQUIRER:** It is hardly necessary, I suppose, to ask you whether you believe in the Christian dogmas of Paradise and Hell, or in future rewards and punishments as taught by the Orthodox churches?

THEOSOPHIST. As they are described in your catechisms, we reject them absolutely; least of all would we accept the idea of their eternity. But we believe firmly in what we call the *Law of Retribution*, and in the absolute justice and wisdom guiding this Law, or Karma. Hence, we positively refuse to accept the cruel and unphilosophical belief in eternal reward or eternal punishment. We say with Horace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus (65–8 BCE), a leading Roman lyric poet.</note>:

->“Let rules be fixed that may our rage contain,<-
->And punish faults *with a proportioned pain*;<-
->But do not flay him who deserves alone<-
->A whipping for the fault that he has done.”<-
<note>Original Latin from Horace's Satires, I.3: "Adsit / Regula, peccatis quae poenas irroget aequas, / Ne scutica dignum horribili sectere flagello."</note>

This is a rule for all men, and a just one. Are we to believe that God, whom you make the embodiment of wisdom, love, and mercy, is less entitled to these attributes than mortal man?

ENQUIRER. Have you any other reasons for rejecting this dogma?

THEOSOPHIST. Our chief reason for it lies in the fact of reincarnation. As already stated, we reject the idea of a new soul created for every newly-born babe. We believe that every human being is the bearer, or *Vehicle*, of an *Ego* <note>In Theosophy, the "Ego" refers to the permanent, immortal individuality that reincarnates, rather than the modern psychological "ego" or personality.</note> of the same age as every other *Ego*; because all *Egos* are of the same *essence* and belong to the primeval emanation from one universal infinite *Ego*. Plato calls the latter the *Logos* <note>original: logos; meaning the "Word" or the objective expression of Divine Thought</note> (or the second manifested God). We call it the manifested divine principle, which is one with the universal mind or soul—not the anthropomorphic <note>term: anthropomorphic; having human characteristics or form</note>, extra-cosmic <note>term: extra-cosmic; existing outside of the universe</note>, and *personal God* in which so many Theists believe. Pray, do not confuse the two.

ENQUIRER. But where is the difficulty, once you accept a manifested—

THEOSOPHIST. Because that which is *impersonal* can hardly create, plan, and think at its own sweet will and pleasure. Being a universal *Law*, immutable <note>unchanging over time</note> in its periodical manifestations—those of radiating and manifesting its own essence at the beginning of every new cycle of life—it is not supposed to create men, only to repent a few years later of having created them. If we are to believe in a divine principle at all, it must be one which is as much absolute harmony, logic, and justice, as it is absolute love, wisdom, and impartiality. A God who would create every soul for the space of one brief span of life, regardless of whether it must animate the body of a wealthy, happy man, or that of a poor suffering wretch—hapless from birth to death though he has done nothing to deserve his cruel fate—would be a senseless *fiend* rather than a God. (See below, “On the Punishment of the Ego.”) Why, even the Jewish philosophers, who were believers in the Mosaic Bible (interpreted esoterically <note>intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with a specialized knowledge</note>, of course), never entertained such an idea; moreover, they believed in reincarnation, just as we do.

ENQUIRER. Can you give me some instances as proof of this?

THEOSOPHIST. I most decidedly can. Philo Judaeus <note>A Hellenistic Jewish philosopher from Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE)</note> says in *On Dreams* (page 455): “The air is full of them (souls); those which are nearest the earth, descending to be tied to mortal bodies, <gloss>return to other bodies</gloss> <note>original Greek: *palindromousi authis*</note>, being desirous to live in them.” In the *Zohar* <note>The foundational work in the literature of Jewish mystical thought known as Kabbalah</note>, the soul is made to plead for her freedom before God: “Lord of the Universe! I am happy in this world, and do not wish to go into another world, where I...”

These ideas on reincarnation and the trinity of man <note>The concept that a human consists of three parts: body, soul, and spirit</note> were held by many of the early Christian Fathers. It is the jumble made by the translators of the New Testament and ancient philosophical treatises between "soul" and "spirit" that has caused many misunderstandings. It is also one of the many reasons why Buddha, Plotinus <note>A major Greek-speaking philosopher of the ancient world and founder of Neoplatonism</note>, and so many other Initiates are now accused of having longed for the total extinction of their souls. To them, “absorption into the Deity” or “reunion with the universal soul” meant something very different than the modern idea of annihilation. The personal soul must, of course, be disintegrated into its particles before it is able to link its purer essence forever with the immortal spirit. But the translators of both the *Acts* and the *Epistles*, who laid the foundation of the *Kingdom of Heaven*, and the modern commentators on the *Buddhist Sutra* of the *Foundation of the Kingdom of Righteousness*, have muddled the meaning of the great apostle of Christianity as much as they have the meaning of the great
