<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between soul and spirit, contrasting Christian biblical translations with Buddhist philosophy regarding the nature of existence and the afterlife.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

The reformer of India <note>Likely referring to Gautama Buddha</note>. The former [translators] have smothered the word *psychikos* <note>original: *ψυχικος*; meaning "soulish" or "natural" as opposed to spiritual</note>, so that no reader imagines it to have any relation with the *soul*; and with this confusion of *soul* and *spirit* together, Bible readers get only a perverted sense of anything on the subject. On the other hand, the interpreters of Buddha have failed to understand the meaning and object of the Buddhist four degrees of *Dhyâna* <note>States of profound meditation or mental concentration</note>. 

Ask the Pythagoreans, "Can that spirit, which gives life and motion and partakes of the nature of light, be reduced to nonexistence?" "Can even that sensitive spirit in brutes <note>term: animals</note> which exercises memory—one of the rational faculties—die and become nothing?" observe the Occultists. In Buddhistic philosophy, *annihilation* means only a dispersion of matter, in whatever form or *semblance* of form it may be; for everything that has form is temporary, and is, therefore, really an illusion. 

In eternity, the longest periods of time are as a wink of the eye. So it is with form. Before we have time to realize that we have seen it, it is gone like an instantaneous flash of lightning, and passed forever. When the spiritual *entity* breaks loose forever from every particle of matter, substance, or form, and becomes once again a spiritual breath: then only does it enter upon the eternal and unchangeable *Nirvana*, lasting as long as the cycle of life has lasted—an eternity, truly. And then that Breath, existing in *Spirit*, is *nothing* because it is *all*; as a form, a semblance, or a shape, it is completely annihilated; but as absolute Spirit it still exists, for it has become *Be-ness* <note>A Theosophical term for pure, absolute existence beyond the state of "being" something specific</note> itself. The very word used, "absorbed in the universal essence," when spoken of the "Soul" as Spirit, means "*union with*." It can never mean annihilation, as that would mean eternal separation.

<summary>The author argues that "annihilation" in Buddhism refers to the dissolution of temporary forms rather than the destruction of the spirit, defining Nirvana as a state of universal union rather than nonexistence.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

**ENQUIRER:** Do you not lay yourself open to the accusation of preaching annihilation by the language you yourself use? You have just spoken of the <term>Soul</term> <note>In this context, the "Soul" refers to the lower, personal aspects of a human being.</note> of man returning to its primordial elements.

**THEOSOPHIST:** But you forget that I have given you the differences between the various meanings of the word “<term>Soul</term>,” and shown the loose way in which the term “<term>Spirit</term>” has been hitherto translated. We speak of an animal, a human, and a *spiritual* <term>Soul</term>, and distinguish between them. 

Plato, for instance, calls the “rational <term>Soul</term>” that which we call <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The spiritual soul or the faculty of intuition.</note>, though he adds the adjective “spiritual” to it. However, that which we call the reincarnating <term>Ego</term>, or <term>Manas</term> <note>The mind or the thinking principle.</note>, he calls <term>Spirit</term>, or <term>Nous</term> <note>original Greek: "Nous," meaning mind or intellect.</note>, etc. In contrast, we apply the term <term>Spirit</term>, when standing alone and without any qualification, to <term>Atma</term> <note>The pure, universal Spirit.</note> alone. 

Pythagoras repeats our archaic doctrine when stating that the <term>Ego</term> (<term>Nous</term>) is eternal with <term>Deity</term>; that the soul only passed through various stages to arrive at divine excellence; while the <term>thymos</term> <note>original Greek: "thymos," referring to the seat of passions and emotions.</note> returned to the earth, and even the <term>phren</term> <note>original Greek: "phren," here referring to the lower mind or rationalizing faculty.</note>, the lower <term>Manas</term>, was eliminated. Again, Plato defines <term>Soul</term> (<term>Buddhi</term>) as “the motion that is able to move itself.” “<term>Soul</term>,” he adds in his work *Laws*, Book X, “is the most ancient of all things, and the commencement of motion,” thus calling <term>Atma-Buddhi</term> “<term>Soul</term>,” and <term>Manas</term> “<term>Spirit</term>,” which we do not.

“<term>Soul</term> was generated prior to body, and body is posterior and secondary, as being according to nature, ruled over by the ruling <term>soul</term>.” “The <term>soul</term> which administers all things that are moved in every way, administers likewise the heavens.”

“<term>Soul</term> then leads everything in heaven, and on earth, and in the sea, by its movements—the names of which are, to will, to consider, to take care of, to consult, to form opinions true and false, to be in a state of joy, sorrow, confidence, fear, hate, love, together with all such primary movements.”

<summary>The Theosophist clarifies that "annihilation" only applies to the lower aspects of the soul, using the terminology of Plato and Pythagoras to distinguish between the eternal Spirit and the transitory personal elements.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Spirit, Buddhi, Ego, Manas, Nous, Atma, Pythagoras, Plato, Deity, thymos, phren, Atma-Buddhi, Theosophy, Laws X</keywords>

<page-num>116</page-num>
# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

movements as are allied to these. . . . Being a goddess herself, she ever takes as an ally *Mind* <note>original: "Nous"</note>, a god, and disciplines all things correctly and happily; but when she is allied with *Folly* <note>original: "Annoia"</note>—not *mind*—it works out everything the contrary.”

In this language, as in the Buddhist texts, the negative is treated as essential existence. *Annihilation* comes under a similar interpretation <note>original: "exegesis"</note>. The positive state is essential being, but no manifestation as such. When the spirit, in Buddhist terminology, enters *Nirvana*, it loses objective existence but retains subjective being. To objective minds, this is becoming absolute “nothing”; to subjective minds, it is <term>No-THING</term>—meaning nothing that can be displayed to the senses. Thus, their *Nirvana* means the certainty of individual immortality in *Spirit*, not in the Soul. The Soul, though “the most ancient of all things,” is still—along with all the other *Gods*—a finite emanation in its *forms* and individuality, even if not in its substance.

ENQUIRER: I do not quite grasp the idea yet, and would be thankful to have you explain this to me with some illustrations.

THEOSOPHIST: No doubt it is very difficult to understand, especially for someone brought up in the regular orthodox ideas of the Christian Church. Moreover, I must tell you one thing: unless you have thoroughly studied the separate functions assigned to all the human “principles” <note>The different components of a human being, such as physical, emotional, and spiritual layers</note> and the state of all these *after death*, you will hardly realize the depth of our Eastern philosophy.

<summary>This page explains the distinction between "nothingness" and "No-thingness" in Eastern philosophy, clarifying that Nirvana represents spiritual immortality rather than total extinction. It emphasizes that the Soul is a finite emanation, whereas the Spirit represents absolute, subjective being.</summary>

## ON THE VARIOUS "PRINCIPLES" IN MAN.

ENQUIRER. I have heard a good deal about this constitution of the “inner man,” as you call it, but I could never make “head or tail of it,” as Gabalis <note>A reference to the 1670 satirical novel *Comte de Gabalis* by Abbé de Villars, which deals with hidden mysteries and elemental spirits.</note> expresses it.

THEOSOPHIST. Of course, it is most difficult and, as you say, “puzzling” to correctly understand and distinguish between the various *aspects*—which we call the “principles”—of the real Ego. It is even more difficult because there is a notable difference in how these principles are numbered by various Eastern schools, even though at the bottom there is the same identical foundation of teaching.

ENQUIRER. Do you mean the Vedantins <note>Followers of Vedanta, one of the six schools of Hindu philosophy.</note>, for instance? Don’t they divide your seven “principles” into five only?

THEOSOPHIST. They do; but though I would not presume to dispute the point with a learned Vedantin, I may still state as my private opinion that they have an obvious reason for it. To them, only that compound spiritual aggregate consisting of various mental aspects is called *Man* at all. In their view, the physical body is something beneath contempt and merely an *illusion* <note>original: "Maya"</note>. Nor is the Vedanta the only philosophy to calculate in this manner. Lao-Tze, in his *Tao-te-King*, mentions only five principles because he, like the Vedantins, omits two: namely, the spirit (Atma) and the physical body—the latter of which he actually calls “the corpse.” Then there is the *Taraka Raja Yoga* School. Its teaching recognizes only three “principles” in practice; but then, in reality, their *Sthulopadhi* <term>the physical basis or substance of a thing</term>, or the physical body, in its—

<summary>The Theosophist explains that while different Eastern schools (such as Vedanta and Taoism) number the "principles" of man differently, they all share a common underlying doctrine regarding the human constitution.</summary>
<keywords>principles of man, inner man, Ego, Vedanta, Lao-Tze, Tao-te-King, Atma, Taraka Raja Yoga, Sthulopadhi, Eastern philosophy</keywords>

waking conscious state, their *Sukshmopadhi* <note>Sanskrit: "subtle vehicle" or the body used in the dream state</note>, the same body in *Svapna* <note>Sanskrit: the dreaming state of consciousness</note>, or the dreaming state, and their *Karanopadhi* <note>Sanskrit: "causal vehicle"</note> or “causal body,” or that which passes from one incarnation to another, are all dual in their aspects, and thus make six. THEOSOPHIST. Just the same. We could easily make of the body the vehicle of the “vital Double”; of the latter the vehicle of Life or *Prana* <note>Sanskrit: breath or life-force</note>; of *Kamarupa* <note>Sanskrit: "desire-form," the seat of animal passions</note>, or (animal) soul, the vehicle of the *higher* and the *lower* mind, and make of this six principles, crowning the whole with the one immortal spirit. In Occultism <note>The study of hidden spiritual realities</note> every qualitative change in the state of our consciousness gives to man a new aspect. If that state prevails and becomes part of the living and acting Ego <note>The "I" or the conscious self</note>, it must be (and is) given a special name, to distinguish the man in that particular state from the man he is when he places himself in another state.

ENQUIRER. It is just that which it is so difficult to understand.

THEOSOPHIST. It seems to me very easy, on the contrary, once you have seized the main idea: that is, that man acts on one or another plane of consciousness in strict accordance with his mental and spiritual condition. But such is the materialism of the age that the more we explain, the less people seem capable of understanding.

Well, if we take these three groups or representative entities and subdivide them according to the occult teaching, what do we get?

First of all, Spirit (in the sense of the Absolute, and therefore, the indivisible ALL), or *Atma* <note>Sanskrit: The supreme self or innermost essence.</note>. As this can neither be located nor limited in philosophy—being simply that which *is* in Eternity, and which cannot be absent from even the tiniest geometrical or mathematical point of the universe of matter or substance—it ought not to be called, in truth, a "human" principle at all. Rather, and at best, it is in Metaphysics that point in space which the human Monad <note>The unified combination of the two highest principles (Atma and Buddhi) that survives death and reincarnates.</note> and its vehicle, man, occupy for the period of every life. 

Now, that point is as imaginary as man himself, and in reality is an illusion, a *maya* <note>original: *maya*; a Sanskrit term referring to the illusory nature of the phenomenal world</note>; but then for ourselves, as for other personal Egos, we are a reality during that fit of illusion called life. We have to take ourselves into account, in our own fancy at any rate, if no one else does. To make it more conceivable to the human intellect when first attempting the study of Occultism, and to solve the basics of the mystery of man, Occultism calls this seventh principle the synthesis of the sixth, and gives it for a vehicle the **Spiritual Soul, Buddhi** <note>Sanskrit: The faculty of higher intuition and spiritual discernment.</note>. This mystery is never given to anyone, with the exception of irrevocably pledged *chelas* <note>original: "chelas"; a Sanskrit term for a devoted disciple or student of a spiritual teacher</note>, or those, at any rate, who can be safely trusted. **ENQUIRER.** But why should not man be represented by two “principles” or two aspects, rather?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Every man has these two principles in him, one more active than the other. In rare cases, one of these is entirely stunted in its growth, so to speak, or paralyzed by the strength and predominance of the other *aspect*, in whatever direction. These, then, are what we call the two principles or aspects of *Manas*: the higher and the lower. The former, the higher *Manas*, or the thinking, conscious Ego, gravitates toward the spiritual Soul (*Buddhi*); and the latter, or its instinctual principle, is attracted to *Kama* <note>The seat of animal desires, passions, and emotions</note>, the seat of animal desires and passions in man.

For it is the *Buddhi-Manas* which is called the *Causal body* (the united 5th and 6th Principles) and which is the *Consciousness* that connects it with every personality it inhabits on earth. ### VIII.
## ON RE-INCARNATION OR RE-BIRTH.
---
### *WHAT IS MEMORY ACCORDING TO THE THEOSOPHICAL TEACHING?*

ENQUIRER. The most difficult thing for you to do will be to explain and give reasonable grounds for such a belief. No Theosophist has ever yet succeeded in bringing forward a single valid proof to shake my skepticism. First of all, you have against this theory of reincarnation the fact that no single person has yet been found to remember that they have lived—least of all who they were—during a previous life.

THEOSOPHIST. Your argument, I see, tends toward the same old objection: the loss of memory in each of us regarding our previous incarnation. You think it invalidates our doctrine? My answer is that it does not, and that at any rate, such an objection cannot be final.

ENQUIRER. I would like to hear your arguments.

THEOSOPHIST. They are short and few. Yet when you take into consideration (**a**) the utter inability of the best modern psychologists to explain to the world the nature of mind; and (**b**) their complete ignorance of its potentialities and higher states, you have to admit that this objection is based on an *a* <note>The text cuts off at "a," likely leading into the term "a priori," meaning reasoning based on theoretical deduction rather than observation.</note>

<summary>The Enquirer challenges the doctrine of reincarnation by pointing out that people do not remember their past lives, to which the Theosophist responds by highlighting the limitations of modern psychology in understanding the mind.</summary>
<keywords>reincarnation, rebirth, memory, skepticism, psychology, nature of mind, Theosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between an Enquirer and a Theosophist regarding the nature of the human mind and the afterlife, specifically focusing on the mechanics of memory.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY</header>

<note>original: *a priori*</note> From cause to effect—a conclusion drawn from *first impressions* <note>original: *primâ facie*</note> and circumstantial evidence more than anything else. Now, what is “*memory*” in your conception, pray?

ENQ. That which is generally accepted: the faculty in our mind of remembering and of retaining the knowledge of previous thoughts, deeds, and events.

THEO. Please add to this that there is a great difference between the three accepted forms of memory. Besides memory in general, you have *Remembrance*, *Recollection*, and *Reminiscence*, have you not? Have you ever thought over the difference? Memory, remember, is a generic name.

ENQ. Yet, all these are only synonyms.

THEO. Indeed, they are not—not in philosophy, at all events. Memory is simply an innate power in thinking beings, and even in animals, of reproducing past impressions by an association of ideas. These are principally suggested by objective things or by some action on our external sensory organs. Memory is a faculty depending entirely on the more or less healthy and normal functioning of our physical brain; and *remembrance* and *recollection* are the attributes and handmaidens of that memory. But *reminiscence* is an entirely different thing. “*Reminiscence*” is defined by the modern psychologist as something intermediate between *remembrance* and *recollection*, or “a conscious process of recalling past occurrences, but without that full and varied reference to particular things which characterizes *recollection*.” Locke <note>John Locke (1632–1704), an influential English philosopher often called the "Father of Liberalism."</note>, speaking of recollection and remembrance, says: “When *an idea again recurs without the operation of the like object on the external sensory, it is remembrance*; if it be...

<summary>The Theosophist distinguishes between physical memory (remembrance and recollection) and the higher faculty of reminiscence, arguing that standard memory depends entirely on the physical brain.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

sought after by the mind, and with pain and endeavor found and brought again into view, it is *recollection*." <note>The author is referencing John Locke’s *An Essay Concerning Human Understanding*.</note> But even Locke leaves *reminiscence* without any clear definition, because it is no faculty or attribute of our *physical memory*, but an intuitional perception apart from and outside our physical brain. It is a perception which covers all those visions in man which are regarded as *abnormal*—from the pictures suggested by genius to the *delirium* <note>original: "ravings"</note> of fever and even *madness*. Because these are called into action by the ever-present knowledge of our spiritual Ego, they are classed by science as having no *existence* outside of our imagination. 

Occultism and Theosophy, however, regard *reminiscence* in an entirely different light. For us, while *memory* is physical and temporary <note>original: "evanescent"</note> and depends on the physiological conditions of the brain—a fundamental proposition with all teachers of memory-training <note>term: mnemonics</note>, who have the researches of modern scientific psychologists to back them—we call *reminiscence* the *memory of the soul*. And it is this *memory* which gives the assurance to almost every human being, whether he understands it or not, of his having lived before and having to live again. Indeed, as Wordsworth has it:

> "Our birth is but a sleep and a forgetting,
> The soul that rises with us, our life's star,
> Has elsewhere had its setting,
> And comes from afar."

**ENQUIRER:** If it is on this kind of memory—poetry and abnormal fancies, by your own confession—that you base your doctrine, then you will convince very few, I am afraid.

**THEOSOPHIST:** I did not “confess” it was a fancy. I simply said that physiologists and scientists in general regard such reminiscences as such.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

As for those who dismiss these experiences as hallucinations and fancy, they are welcome to that learned conclusion. We do not deny that such visions of the past and glimpses far back into the corridors of time are abnormal when contrasted with our normal daily life experience and physical memory. But we do maintain, along with Professor W. Knight, that "the absence of memory regarding any action done in a previous state cannot be a conclusive argument against our having lived through it." Every fair-minded opponent must also agree with what is said in Butler’s *Lectures on Platonic Philosophy*—"that the feeling of extravagance with which it (pre-existence) affects us has its secret source in materialistic or semi-materialistic prejudices." 

Besides this, we maintain that memory—as Olympiodorus <note>A 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher known for his commentaries on Plato.</note> called it—is simply *fantasy* <note>original: "phantasy"</note>, and the most unreliable thing in us. 

<margin>“Fantasy,” says Olympiodorus (in his commentary on *Plato's Phaedo*), “is an impediment to our intellectual conceptions. Therefore, when we are moved by the inspiring influence of the Divine, if fantasy intervenes, the inspired energy ceases; for inspiration <note>original: "enthusiasm," used here in the classical sense of being possessed by a god</note> and the ecstatic state are contrary to each other. Should it be asked whether the soul is able to act <note>original: "energise"</note> without fantasy, we reply that its perception of universal truths proves that it is able. It has perceptions, therefore, independent of fantasy; at the same time, however, fantasy accompanies its actions, just as a storm pursues one who sails upon the sea.”</margin>

Ammonius Saccas <note>The 3rd-century founder of the Neoplatonic school in Alexandria.</note> asserted that the only faculty in man directly opposed to prognostication, or looking into the future, is *memory*. Furthermore, remember that memory is one thing and mind or thought is another. One is a recording machine—a register which very easily gets out of order; the other (thoughts) are eternal and imperishable. Would you refuse to believe in the existence of certain things or men only because your physical eyes have not seen them? Would not the collective testimony of—

ENQUIRER: But don’t you think that these are too fine distinctions to be accepted by the majority of mortals?

THEOSOPHIST: Say rather by the majority of materialists. And to them we say, behold: even in the short span of ordinary existence, memory is too weak to register all the events of a lifetime. How frequently do even most important events lie dormant in our memory until awakened by some association of ideas, or aroused to function and activity by some other link. This is especially the case with people of advanced age, who are always found suffering from feebleness of recollection. When, therefore, we remember what we know about the physical and the spiritual principles in man, it is not the fact that our memory has failed to record our preceding life and lives that ought to surprise us, but the contrary, were it to happen.

### WHY DO WE NOT REMEMBER OUR PAST LIVES?

ENQUIRER: You have given me a bird’s eye view of the seven principles <note>Theosophy teaches that a human being consists of seven "principles" or layers, ranging from the physical body to the highest spiritual essence.</note>; now how do they account for our complete loss of any recollection of having lived before?

THEOSOPHIST: Very easily. Since those “principles” which we call physical—none of which is denied by science, though it...

Reincarnation means that this Ego will be furnished with a *new body*, a *new brain*, and a *new memory*. Therefore, it would be as absurd to expect this memory to remember that which it has never recorded as it would be idle to examine under a microscope a shirt never worn by a murderer, seeking on it for the stains of blood which are to be found only on the clothes he actually wore. It is not the clean shirt that we have to question, but the clothes worn during the commission of the crime; and if these are burnt and destroyed, how can you get at them?

**ENQUIRER:** Yes! But how can you arrive at the certainty that the crime was ever committed at all, or that the "man in the clean shirt" ever lived before?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not by physical processes, most assuredly; nor by relying on the testimony of that which no longer exists. But there is such a thing as circumstantial evidence, since our wise laws accept it—perhaps even more than they should. To become convinced of the fact of reincarnation and past lives, one must put oneself in *rapport* <note>original: rapport; meaning a state of harmonious connection or spiritual resonance</note> with one’s real permanent Ego, not one’s fleeting memory.

## THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

**ENQUIRER.** But how can people believe in that which they *do not know*, nor have ever seen, much less put themselves in *rapport* <note>original: rapport; meaning a close and harmonious relationship or connection</note> with it?

**THEOSOPHIST.** If people—including the most learned—will believe in Gravity, Ether, Force, and other scientific concepts, which are abstractions and "working hypotheses" that they have neither seen, touched, smelled, heard, nor tasted, why should other people not believe, on the same principle, in one’s permanent Ego? It is a far more logical and important "working hypothesis" than any other.

**ENQUIRER.** What is, finally, this mysterious eternal principle? Can you explain its nature so as to make it understandable to everyone?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It is the Ego which reincarnates, the *individual* and immortal—not the personal—"I." It is, in short, the vehicle of the Atma-Buddhic Monad <note>The combination of the highest spiritual self (Atma) and the spiritual soul or discernment (Buddhi)</note>. This is the entity that is rewarded in Devachan <note>A blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth; a "heaven-world"</note> and punished on earth. Finally, it is that to which only the reflection of the *Skandhas*, or attributes, of every incarnation attaches itself.*

\* There are five <term>Skandhas</term> <note>In Buddhist philosophy, these are the five functions or aspects that constitute a sentient being</note> or attributes in the Buddhist teachings: <term>Rupa</term> (form or body), material qualities; <term>Vedana</term>, sensation; <term>Sanna</term>, abstract ideas; <term>Samkhara</term>, tendencies of mind; <term>Vinnana</term>, mental powers. Through these, we are conscious of existence and communicate with the world about us.

**ENQUIRER.** What do you mean by *Skandhas*?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Just what I said: "attributes," among which is memory. All of these perish like a flower, leaving behind them only a faint perfume. Here is another paragraph from H. S. Olcott’s "Buddhist Catechism"† which bears directly upon the—

† By H. S. Olcott, President and Founder of the Theosophical Society. The accuracy of the teaching is sanctioned by the Reverend H. Sumangala, High Priest of the Sripada and Galle, and Principal of the Widyodaya Parivena (College) at Colombo, as being in agreement with the Canon of the Southern Buddhist Church.

This proves to you that while the undying qualities of the personality—such as love, goodness, charity, etc.—attach themselves to the immortal <term>Ego</term>, photographing on it, so to speak, a permanent image of the divine aspect of the man who was, his material <term>Skandhas</term> (those which generate the most marked <term>Karmic</term> <note>Relating to Karma, the law of cause and effect</note> effects) are as evanescent as a flash of lightning. They cannot impress the new brain of the new personality; yet their failure to do so in no way impairs the identity of the reincarnating <term>Ego</term>.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Not quite; something of each personality must survive, unless the person was an *absolute materialist* with not even a chink in his nature for a spiritual ray to pass through. This survival occurs because the personality leaves its eternal impress on the incarnating permanent <term>Self</term>.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

The Mind is alone omniscient <note>all-knowing</note>; and, secondly, because Devachan <note>the "dwelling of the gods," a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note> is the idealized continuation of the terrestrial life just left behind. It is a period of retributive adjustment and a reward for unmerited wrongs and sufferings undergone in that specific life. The Mind is omniscient only *potentially* in Devachan, and *in fact* exclusively in Nirvana, when the Ego is merged into the Universal Mind-Soul. 

Yet it becomes *almost* omniscient during those hours on earth when certain abnormal conditions and physiological changes in the body set the *Ego* free from the chains of matter. Thus, the examples cited above of somnambulists <note>sleepwalkers or those in a hypnotic trance</note>—such as a poor servant speaking Hebrew and another playing the violin—provide an illustration of this point. 

This does not mean that the explanations of these two facts offered to us by medical science are untrue. In those cases, one girl had, years before, heard her master (a clergyman) read Hebrew works aloud, and the other had heard an artist playing a violin at their farm. However, neither could have performed these tasks as perfectly as they did if they had not been filled with the spirit of THAT which is omniscient, owing to its nature being identical with the Universal Mind. In this instance, the higher principle acted upon the Skandhas <note>the five attributes or "bundles" that make up the worldly personality</note> and moved them; in the other case, the personality being paralyzed, the true individuality manifested itself. Please do not confuse the two.

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

## *ON INDIVIDUALITY AND PERSONALITY.*

**ENQUIRER:** But what is the difference between the two? I confess that I am still in the dark. Indeed, it is just that difference that you cannot emphasize too much to us.

**THEOSOPHIST:** Even in his *Buddhist Catechism*, Colonel Olcott—forced by the logic of Esoteric philosophy—found himself obliged to correct the mistakes of previous Orientalists <note>Scholars who study the languages and cultures of Asia.</note> who made no such distinction, and he gives the reader his reasons for it. Thus he says: 

"The successive appearances upon the earth, or 'descents into generation,' of the parts of a certain being held together by *Tanha* <note>original: *tanhaically*; Tanha is the 'thirst' or craving for life that causes rebirth.</note> (*Skandhas* <note>The five attributes or 'bundles' that make up a worldly individual: form, sensation, perception, mental formations, and consciousness.</note>), are a succession of personalities. In each birth, the **PERSONALITY** differs from that of a previous or next succeeding birth. Karma, the god from the machine <note>original: *DEUS EX MACHINA*; a literary device where a complex problem is suddenly solved by an outside force; here, the law of cause and effect.</note>, masks (or shall we say reflects?) itself now in the personality of a sage, again as an artisan, and so on throughout the string of births. 

But though personalities ever shift, the one line of life along which they are strung, like beads, runs unbroken; it is ever that *particular line*, never any other. It is therefore individual—an individual vital undulation which began in Nirvana (the subjective side of nature), just as a light or heat wave through the ether began at its dynamic source. This life-wave is moving through the objective side of nature under the impulse of Karma and the creative direction of *Tanha* (the unsatisfied desire for existence), and it leads through many cyclic changes back to Nirvana. 

Mr. Rhys-Davids <note>A prominent 19th-century British scholar of the Pali language and founder of the Pali Text Society.</note> calls that which passes from personality to personality along the individual chain 'character' or 'doing.' Since 'character' is not a mere metaphysical abstraction, but the sum of one’s mental qualities and moral propensities, would it not help to dispel what Mr. Rhys-Davids calls 'the desperate expedient of a mystery' (*Buddhism*, p. 101) if we regarded the life-undulation as individuality, and each of its series of birth-manifestations as a separate personality? 

The perfect individual, speaking from a Buddhist perspective, is a Buddha, I should say; for a Buddha is but the rare flower of humanity, without the least supernatural admixture. And as countless generations ('four *asankheyas* <note>An incredibly large number, often translated as 'incalculable' or 10 to the power of 140.</note> and a hundred thousand cycles,' Fausböll and Rhys-Davids’ **BUDDHIST BIRTH STORIES**, p. 18) are required to develop a *man* into a Buddha, and the *iron will* to become one runs throughout all the successive births, what shall we call that which thus wills and perseveres? Character? No, it is one’s individuality: an individuality but partly manifested in any one birth, but built up of fragments from all the births." (*Buddhist Catechism, Appendix A. 137*.)

<summary>This page uses excerpts from Colonel Olcott’s Buddhist Catechism to define "Individuality" as the enduring, unbroken line of life (the soul's journey) and "Personality" as the temporary mask or role assumed in a single incarnation.</summary>

**THEOSOPHIST.** I try to; but alas, it is harder with some than to make them feel a reverence for childish impossibilities, simply because they are *orthodox*, and because orthodoxy is respectable. To understand the idea well, you first have to study the dual sets of "principles": the *spiritual*, or those which belong to the imperishable Ego; and the *material*, or those principles which make up the ever-changing bodies or the series of personalities of that Ego. Let us fix permanent names to these, and say that:

I. ***Atma***, the "**Higher Self**," is neither your Spirit nor mine, but like sunlight, it shines on all. It is the universally diffused "**divine principle**," and is inseparable from its one and absolute **Meta-Spirit** <note>original: "Meta-Spirit," referring to the ultimate source beyond spirit</note>, just as a sunbeam is inseparable from sunlight.

II. ***Buddhi*** (the spiritual soul) is only its vehicle. Neither each separately, nor the two collectively, are of any more use to the body of man than sunlight and its beams are for a mass of granite buried in the earth, unless the **divine Duad** <note>The pair of Atma and Buddhi</note> is assimilated by, and reflected in, some **consciousness**. Neither Atma nor Buddhi are ever reached by **Karma** <note>The law of cause and effect</note>, because the former is the highest aspect of Karma—its working agent *itself* in one aspect—and the other is unconscious on *this plane* of existence. This consciousness or mind is:

III. ***Manas***,<margin>* **Mahat**, or the "**Universal Mind**," is the source of **Manas**. The latter is Mahat (that is, mind) in man. Manas is also called ***Kshetrajña***, "embodied Spirit," because it is, according to our philosophy, the ***Manasa-putras***, or "**Sons of Mind**."</margin> the derivation or product in a reflected form

<insert>It is a portion of the Universal Mind <note>original: "Mahat"</note> which *created*, or rather *produced*, the *thinking man* (called *Manu* <note>The Sanskrit root for "man" and "to think"</note>) by incarnating into the mankind of the *Third Race* <note>In Theosophical history, the stage of humanity where self-consciousness first developed</note> during our current evolutionary cycle or "Round." It is *Manas* <note>The Mind-principle</note>, therefore, which is the real incarnating and permanent **Spiritual Ego**, the **INDIVIDUALITY**; our various and countless personalities are only its external masks.</insert>

This is the principle of *Ahamkara* <note>The "I-making" faculty</note>, "the conception of I," or **EGO-SHIP**. Therefore, when it is inseparably united to the first two higher principles <note>Atma and Buddhi</note>, it is called the **SPIRITUAL** Ego and *Taijasi* (the radiant). This is the real Individuality, or the divine man. It is this **Ego** which—having originally incarnated in the senseless human form that was animated by, but unconscious of, the presence of the dual monad <note>The spirit-soul</note>—made that human-like form a *real man*. It is that **Ego**, that "**Causal Body**," which overshadows every personality that Karma forces it to incarnate into. This Ego is held responsible for all the sins committed through, and in, every new body or personality—the fleeting masks which hide the true **Individual** through the long series of rebirths.

ENQUIRER: But is this just? Why should this Ego receive punishment as the result of deeds which it has forgotten?

THEOSOPHIST: It has not forgotten them; it knows and remembers its misdeeds as well as you remember what you did yesterday. Just because the memory of that bundle of physical compounds called the "body" does not recollect what its predecessor (the personality *that was*) did, do you imagine that the real Ego has forgotten them? You might as well say it is unjust that the new boots on the feet of a boy, who is being whipped for stealing apples, should be punished for something they know nothing about.

## THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

ENQUIRER. But are there no modes of communication between the spiritual and human consciousness or memory?

THEOSOPHIST. Of course there are; but they have never been recognized by your modern scientific psychologists. To what do you attribute intuition, the "voice of conscience," premonitions, vague undefined reminiscences, and so on, if not to such communications? I wish that at least the majority of educated men had the fine spiritual perceptions of Coleridge, who shows how intuitive he is in some of his comments. Hear what he says regarding the probability that "all thoughts are in themselves imperishable." "If the intelligent faculty (referring to sudden 'revivals' of memory) should be rendered more comprehensive, it would require only a different and appropriate organization—the *celestial body* instead of the *terrestrial body*—to bring before every human soul the *collective experience of its whole past existence* (or rather, *existences*)." And this *celestial body* is our Manasic Ego <note>The "Manasic Ego" refers to the higher mind or the permanent, reincarnating individuality in Theosophical philosophy.</note>.

### ON THE REWARD AND PUNISHMENT OF THE EGO.

ENQUIRER. I have heard you say that the *Ego*, regardless of what the life of the person he inhabited may have been on Earth, is never visited with *post-mortem* <note>original: "post-mortem"; meaning after death</note> punishment.

THEOSOPHIST. Never, except in very exceptional and rare cases which we will not discuss here, as the nature of that "punishment" in no way resembles any of your theological concepts of damnation.

**ENQUIRER.** But if it is punished in this life for the misdeeds committed in a previous one, then it is this <term>Ego</term> <note>The permanent, spiritual self that reincarnates, as opposed to the temporary personality.</note> that ought to be rewarded also, whether here, or when disincarnated.

**THEOSOPHIST.** And so it is. If we do not admit of any punishment outside of this earth, it is because the only state the Spiritual Self knows of, hereafter, is that of unalloyed bliss.

**ENQUIRER.** What do you mean?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Simply this: *crimes and sins committed on a plane of objectivity and in a world of matter, cannot receive punishment in a world of pure subjectivity*. We believe in no hell or paradise as physical locations; in no objective hell-fires and worms that never die, nor in any Jerusalems with streets paved with sapphires and diamonds. What we believe in is a *post-mortem* <note>original: "post-mortem" — after death</note> state or mental condition, such as we are in during a vivid dream. We believe in an immutable law of absolute Love, Justice, and Mercy. And believing in it, we say: “Whatever the sin and dire results of the original <term>Karmic</term> <note>Relating to Karma, the law of cause and effect.</note> transgression of the now incarnated <term>Egos</term> \* no man (or the outer material and

<note>\* It is on this transgression that the cruel and illogical dogma of the Fallen Angels has been built. It is explained in Volume II of the *Secret Doctrine*. All our “<term>Egos</term>” are thinking and rational entities (<term>Manasa-putras</term> <note>Sanskrit: "Sons of Mind," beings who provide the spark of intellect to humanity.</note>) who had lived, whether under human or other forms, in the preceding <term>life-cycle</term> (<term>Manvantara</term> <note>Sanskrit: A period of manifestation or cosmic activity.</note>), and whose <term>Karma</term> it was to incarnate in the *man* of this one. It was taught in the <term>MYSTERIES</term> <note>Ancient secret religious rites or wisdom traditions.</note> that, having delayed to comply with this law (or having “refused to create” as Hinduism says of the <term>Kumaras</term> <note>Sanskrit: "Youths," virginal ascetic beings in Hindu mythology.</note> and Christian legend says of the Archangel Michael), that is, having failed to incarnate in due time, the bodies predestined for them became defiled (See Stanzas 8 and 9 in the “<term>Slokas of Dzyan</term>,” <note>Verses from an ancient, esoteric text cited by Blavatsky.</note> Volume II of the *Secret Doctrine*, pages 19 and 20). This is the source of the "original sin" of the senseless forms and the punishment of the <term>Egos</term>. What is meant by the rebellious angels being hurled down into Hell is simply explained by these pure Spirits or <term>Egos</term> being imprisoned in bodies of unclean matter and flesh.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the justice of the afterlife and the nature of human responsibility for actions committed during a single lifetime.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

<page-num>139</page-num>

The periodical form of the Spiritual Entity <note>The human soul as it appears in a single incarnation.</note> cannot be held, with any degree of justice, responsible for the consequences of his birth. He does not ask to be born, nor can he choose the parents that will give him life. In every respect, he is a victim of his environment, the child of circumstances over which he has no control. If each of his transgressions were impartially investigated, one would find in nine out of every ten cases that he was the one sinned against, rather than the sinner. 

Life is at best a heartless play, a stormy sea to cross, and a heavy burden often too difficult to bear. The greatest philosophers have tried in vain to fathom and find out its *reason for being* <note>original: "raison d'être"</note>, and all have failed except those who had the key to it—namely, the Eastern sages. Life is, as Shakespeare describes it:

> “. . . but a walking shadow—a poor player,
> That struts and frets his hour upon the stage,
> And then is heard no more. It is a tale
> Told by an idiot, full of sound and fury,
> Signifying nothing . . . .”

It signifies nothing in its separate parts, yet it is of the greatest importance in its collectivity, or series of lives. At any rate, almost every individual life is, in its full development, a sorrow. And are we to believe that poor, helpless man—after being tossed about like a piece of rotten timber on the angry billows of life—is to be punished by an <term>everlastingness</term> <note>original: "sempiternity"</note> of damnation, or even a temporary punishment, if he proves too weak to resist those waves? 

Never! Whether a great or an average sinner, good or bad, guilty or innocent, once delivered of the burden of physical life, the tired and worn-out <term>Manu</term> <note>A Sanskrit term for the "thinking Ego" or the conscious mind of the human being.</note> has won...

<summary>The author argues that individuals are largely victims of their circumstances and environment, making eternal punishment for earthly sins unjust. Life is presented as a series of struggles that only make sense when viewed as a collective chain of incarnations rather than a single event.</summary>

<keywords>Spiritual Entity, reincarnation, justice, karma, environment, Eastern sages, Shakespeare, Manu, Ego, suffering</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the state of the soul after death and the mechanics of divine justice.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

the right to a period of absolute rest and bliss. The same unerringly wise and just—rather than merely merciful—Law, which inflicts upon the incarnated Ego <note>The "Ego" here refers to the permanent spiritual self that reincarnates.</note> the Karmic punishment for every sin committed during the preceding life on Earth, also provides for the now disembodied Entity a long lease of mental rest. This involves the entire oblivion of every sad event—even to the smallest painful thought—that took place in its last life as a personality, leaving in the soul-memory only the reminiscence of that which was blissful or led to happiness. Plotinus <note>A major Greek-speaking philosopher (c. 204–270 CE) and founder of Neoplatonism.</note>, who said that our body was the true river of Lethe <note>In Greek mythology, Lethe was the river of forgetfulness in the underworld.</note>, for "souls plunged into it forget all," meant more than he said. For, just as our terrestrial body is like Lethe, so is our *celestial body* in Devachan <note>term: Devachan; the "dwelling of the gods," a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth.</note>, and much more so.

ENQUIRER: Then am I to understand that the murderer, the transgressor of divine and human law in every shape, is allowed to go unpunished?

THEOSOPHIST: Who ever said that? Our philosophy has a doctrine of punishment as stern as that of the most rigid Calvinist <note>A follower of the Protestant theological system of John Calvin, known for emphasizing the sovereignty of God and the inevitability of divine decrees.</note>, only far more philosophical and consistent with absolute justice. No deed, not even a sinful thought, will go unpunished; the latter is punished even more severely than the former, as a thought is far more powerful in creating evil results than even a deed. We believe in an unerring law of Retribution, called KARMA, which asserts itself in a natural chain of causes and their unavoidable results.

ENQUIRER: And how, or where, does it act?

THEOSOPHIST: Every laborer is worthy of his hire, says Wisdom in the—

> "Truly, I say to you, that whoever looks at a woman to lust after her, has committed adultery with her already in his heart." (Matthew 5:28.)

<summary>This page explains that while the soul enjoys a period of blissful oblivion (Devachan) between lives, the law of Karma ensures absolute justice by punishing every sinful deed and thought in future incarnations.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Devachan, Plotinus, Lethe, divine justice, retribution, reincarnation, Theosophy</keywords>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

Gospel; every action, good or bad, is a prolific parent, says the Wisdom of the Ages. Put the two together, and you will find the "why." After allowing the Soul—having escaped from the pangs of personal life—a sufficient, even a hundredfold compensation, Karma, with its army of *Skandhas* <note>original: Skandhas; the attributes or "bundles" of personal characteristics that make up a human being</note>, waits at the threshold of *Devachan* <note>original: Devachan; a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note>, from where the *Ego* re-emerges to assume a new incarnation. 

It is at this moment that the future destiny of the now-rested *Ego* trembles in the scales of just Retribution, as it now falls once again under the sway of active Karmic law. It is in this rebirth which is ready for it—a rebirth selected and prepared by this mysterious, inexorable, but in the equity and wisdom of its decrees, infallible LAW—that the sins of the previous life of the *Ego* are punished. Only, it is into no imaginary Hell, with theatrical flames and ridiculous tailed and horned devils, that the *Ego* is cast, but truly onto this earth, the plane and region of his sins, where he will have to atone for every bad thought and deed. As he has sown, so will he reap. Reincarnation will gather around him all those other *Egos* who have suffered, whether directly or indirectly, at the hands, or even through the unconscious influence, of the past *personality*. They will be thrown by Nemesis <note>The Greek goddess of divine retribution or vengeance</note> in the way of the new man, who conceals the *old*, the eternal *Ego*, and . . . .

**Enquirer:** But where is the equity you speak of, since these new "personalities" are not aware of having sinned or been sinned against?

**Theosophist:** Must the coat torn to shreds from the back of the man who stole it—by another man who was robbed of it and recognizes his property—be regarded as having been treated unfairly? The new...

<summary>This page explains that the "punishment" for past sins occurs on Earth through reincarnation rather than in a mythological Hell, as the Ego returns from a restful state to face the consequences of its previous actions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between the temporary personality and the enduring individuality in relation to the laws of Karma and Reincarnation.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

“**Personality**” is no better than a fresh suit of clothes with its specific characteristics, color, form, and qualities; but the real man who wears it is the same culprit as of old. It is the **individuality** who suffers through his “**personality**.” And it is this, and this alone, that can account for the terrible—though still only *apparent*—injustice in the distribution of life's circumstances to humanity. 

When your modern philosophers succeed in showing us a good reason why so many apparently innocent and good men are born only to suffer during a whole lifetime; why so many are born poor to the point of starvation in the slums of great cities, abandoned by fate and men; why, while these are born in the gutter, others open their eyes to light in palaces; why a noble birth and fortune seem often given to the worst of men and only rarely to the worthy; and why there are beggars whose *inner* selves are peers to the highest and noblest of men—when this, and much more, is satisfactorily explained by either your philosophers or theologians, then only, but not until then, will you have the right to reject the theory of <term>reincarnation</term> <note>The rebirth of a soul in a new body.</note>. 

The highest and grandest of poets have dimly perceived this truth of truths. Shelley believed in it, and Shakespeare must have thought of it when writing on the worthlessness of Birth. Remember his words:

“Why should my birth keep down my mounting spirit?
Are not all creatures subject unto time?
There’s legions now of beggars on the earth,
That their original did spring from Kings,
And many monarchs now, whose fathers were
The riff-raff of their age . . . . . .”

Alter the word “fathers” into “Egos”—and you will have the truth.

<summary>The author argues that reincarnation is the only logical explanation for the apparent injustices of birth and social station, using the distinction between the temporary "personality" and the eternal "individuality."</summary>

# IX.
## ON KAMA-LOKA AND DEVACHAN.
### ON THE FATE OF THE LOWER “PRINCIPLES.”

**ENQUIRER:** You spoke of *Kama-loka*; what is it?

**THEOSOPHIST:** When a person dies, his lower three principles leave him forever; that is, the physical body, the life-energy <note>original: "life" or Prana</note>, and the vehicle of that energy, the astral body or the double of the *living* man. After this, his remaining four principles—the central or middle principle, which is the animal soul or <term>Kama-rupa</term> <note>The "desire-form" or seat of animal passions</note>, along with what it has absorbed from the lower <term>Manas</term> <note>The lower mind or intellect tied to earthly matters</note>, and the higher triad—find themselves in <term>Kama-loka</term>. 

The latter is an astral locality, the *limbo* <note>original: "limbus"</note> of scholastic theology, the *Hades* of the ancients. Strictly speaking, it is a locality only in a relative sense. It has neither a definite area nor a boundary, but exists *within* subjective space; that is, it is beyond our physical sensory perceptions. Still, it exists, and it is there that the astral <term>eidolon</term>s <note>An image, ghost, or phantom double of the deceased</note> of all beings that have lived, animals included, await their *second death*. For animals, this death comes with the disintegration and the entire fading out of their astral particles to the very last. For the human <term>eidolon</term>, it begins when the <term>Atma-Buddhi-Manasic triad</term> <note>The three-fold higher self: Spirit, Spiritual Soul, and Mind</note> is said to—

<summary>This page defines Kama-loka as a subjective astral realm where the soul's lower principles and "astral double" dwell after physical death, awaiting a final separation from the higher self.</summary>
<keywords>Kama-loka, afterlife, astral body, Kama-rupa, Manas, Atma-Buddhi-Manas, second death, Hades, Theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the separation of the higher and lower principles of the human constitution after death.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

“separate” itself from its lower principles—or the reflection of the *former personality*—by falling into the Devachanic state <note>Devachan: a blissful, dream-like intermediate state between death and rebirth.</note>.

**ENQUIRER:** And what happens after this?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Then the *Kama-rupic* phantom <note>Kama-rupa: the "desire-body" or astral shell left behind after death</note>, remaining deprived of its informing thinking principle (the higher *Manas* <note>Manas: the mind or intellect</note>), collapses. Because the lower aspect of the mind no longer receives light from the higher mind and no longer has a physical brain to work through, it breaks down.

**ENQUIRER:** In what way?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Well, it falls into a state similar to a frog when certain portions of its brain are removed by a vivisector <note>A person who performs experimental surgery on live animals.</note>. It can think no more, even on the lowest animal plane. From that point on, it is no longer even the "lower Manas," since this “lower” aspect is nothing without the “higher.”

**ENQUIRER:** And is it *this* nonentity which we find materializing in séance rooms with mediums?

**THEOSOPHIST:** It is this nonentity. It is a true nonentity, however, only regarding its powers of reasoning or thinking. It is still an *entity* in an astral and fluidic sense. This is shown in certain cases when, having been magnetically and unconsciously drawn toward a medium, it is revived for a time and lives through the medium by *proxy*, so to speak. This “spook,” or the *Kama-rupa*, may be compared to a *jellyfish*. The jellyfish has an ethereal, gelatinous appearance as long as it is in its own element, the water (which represents the *medium’s specific aura*). But as soon as it is thrown out of the water, it dissolves in the hand or on the sand, especially in sunlight. In the medium’s aura, it lives a kind of vicarious life; it reasons and speaks either through the medium’s brain or...

<summary>This page explains the fate of the "Kama-rupa" or astral shell after the higher mind departs for Devachan, arguing that spiritualist "materializations" are merely mindless, decaying remnants revived by a medium's energy.</summary>

those of other persons present. But this would lead us too far, and upon other people’s grounds, whereon I have no desire to trespass. Let us keep to the subject of reincarnation.

**ENQUIRER.** What of the latter? How long does the incarnating *Ego* <note>The permanent, spiritual self that survives death and takes on new bodies.</note> remain in the Devachanic state? <note>Devachan: A state of blissful consciousness between death and the next rebirth, often compared to a subjective "heaven."</note>

**THEOSOPHIST.** This, we are taught, depends on the degree of spirituality and the merit or demerit of the last incarnation. The average time is from ten to fifteen centuries, as I already told you.

**ENQUIRER.** But why could not this *Ego* manifest and communicate with mortals as Spiritualists claim? **THEOSOPHIST.** Nor are they forced to, unless they happen to prefer truth to fiction, however “consoling” it may be. Uncongenial as our doctrines may be to Spiritualists, yet nothing of what we believe in and teach is half as selfish and cruel as what they preach.

**ENQUIRER.** I do not understand you. What is selfish?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Their doctrine of the return of Spirits—the real “personalities” as they say—and I will tell you why. If Devachan—call it “paradise” if you like, a “place of bliss and of supreme happiness,” if it is anything—is such a place (or rather, state), logic tells us that no sorrow or even a shade of pain can be experienced there. “God shall wipe away all the tears from the eyes” <note>A reference to Revelation 21:4.</note> of those in paradise, we read in the book of many promises. And if the “Spirits of the dead” are enabled to return and see all that is going on on earth, and especially in *their homes*, what kind of bliss can be in store for them?

<summary>The author argues that the "Ego" remains in the blissful state of Devachan for centuries and disputes the Spiritualist idea of spirits returning to Earth, arguing that witnessing earthly suffering would destroy the peace of paradise.</summary>

## WHY THEOSOPHISTS DO NOT BELIEVE IN THE RETURN OF PURE "SPIRITS."

ENQUIRER. What do you mean? Why should this interfere with their bliss?

THEOSOPHIST. Simply this; and here is an instance. A mother dies, leaving behind her little helpless children—orphans whom she adores—and perhaps a beloved husband also. We say that her "Spirit" or *Ego*—that individuality which is now entirely saturated, for the entire <term>Devachanic period</term> <note>The period between death and rebirth spent in a state of subjective bliss.</note>, with the noblest feelings held by its late *personality* (for example, love for her children, pity for those who suffer, and so on)—we say that it is now entirely separated from the "vale of tears." We believe that its future bliss consists in that blessed ignorance of all the woes it left behind. <term>Spiritualists</term> <note>Followers of the 19th-century movement believing in direct communication with the spirits of the dead through mediums.</note> say, on the contrary, that the spirit is as vividly aware of these woes—and even more so than before—because "Spirits see more than mortals in the flesh do." 

We say that the bliss of the <term>Devachanee</term> <note>One who is experiencing the state of Devachan.</note> consists in its complete conviction that it has never left the earth, and that there is no such thing as death at all. We believe that the *post-mortem spiritual consciousness* of the mother will represent to her that she lives surrounded by her children and all those whom she loved. No gap and no link will be missing to make her disembodied state a condition of the most perfect and absolute happiness. The Spiritualists deny this point blank. According to their doctrine, unfortunate man is not liberated even by death from the sorrows of this life. Not a drop from the life-cup of pain and suffering will miss his lips;

<summary>The author contrasts the Theosophical view of a blissful, subjective afterlife (Devachan) with the Spiritualist view, arguing that true peace after death requires a separation from the suffering of the living.</summary>
<keywords>Devachan, Ego, afterlife, Spiritualism, consciousness, suffering, motherhood, post-mortem state</keywords>

<page-num>147</page-num>

And whether he wants to or not <note>original: "nolens volens"</note>, since he sees everything now, he shall drink it to the bitter dregs. Thus, the loving wife, who during her lifetime was ready to save her husband from sorrow at the price of her heart’s blood, is now doomed to see—in utter helplessness—his despair, and to register every hot tear he sheds for her loss. Worse than that, she may see the tears dry too soon, and another beloved face shine on him, the father of her children. She may find another woman replacing her in his affections, and be doomed to hear her orphans giving the holy name of “mother” to one indifferent to them, and to see those little children neglected, if not ill-treated. According to this doctrine, the “gentle wafting to immortal life” becomes, without any transition, the entrance into a new path of mental suffering! And yet, the columns of the *Banner of Light* <note>The oldest and most influential Spiritualist newspaper in the United States, published from 1857 to 1907.</note>, the veteran journal of the American Spiritualists, are filled with messages from the dead—the “dear departed ones”—who all write to say how very happy they are! Is such a state of knowledge consistent with bliss? If so, then “bliss” stands in such a case for the greatest curse, and orthodox damnation must be a relief in comparison to it!

<margin>ENQUIRER.</margin> But how does your theory avoid this? How can you reconcile the theory of the Soul’s omniscience <note>The state of knowing everything.</note> with its blindness to that which is taking place on earth?

<margin>THEOSOPHIST.</margin> Because such is the law of love and mercy. During every Devachanic period <note>Devachan: In Theosophy, a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth where the soul rests in a subjective paradise.</note> the Ego, omniscient as it is in itself <note>original: "per se"</note>, clothes itself, so to speak, with the *reflection* of the “personality” that was. I have just told you that the *ideal* flowering of all the abstract—and therefore undying and eternal—qualities or attributes, such as love and mercy, the love of the...

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY

good, the true, and the beautiful that ever spoke in the heart of the living “personality,” clung after death to the <term>Ego</term> <note>The permanent, immortal self that reincarnates, as opposed to the temporary personality.</note>, and therefore followed it to <term>Devachan</term> <note>A state of blissful consciousness between earthly lives; a "heaven-world."</note>. For the time being, then, the <term>Ego</term> becomes the ideal reflection of the human being it was when last on earth, and *that* reflection is not omniscient. Were it all-knowing, it would never be in the state we call <term>Devachan</term> at all.

ENQUIRER: What are your reasons for this?

THEOSOPHIST: If you want an answer on the strict lines of our philosophy, then I will say that it is because everything is *illusion* (*<term>Maya</term>*) <note>original: "Maya"; the concept that the phenomenal world is a deceptive appearance of the true reality.</note> outside of eternal truth, which has neither form, color, nor limitation. He who has placed himself beyond the veil of *maya*—and such are the highest <term>Adepts</term> and <term>Initiates</term> <note>Highly evolved spiritual teachers who have mastered the hidden laws of nature.</note>—can have no <term>Devachan</term>. As to the ordinary mortal, his bliss in it is complete. It is an *absolute oblivion* of all that gave him pain or sorrow in the past <term>incarnation</term>, and even oblivion of the fact that such things as pain or sorrow exist at all. The *<term>Devachanee</term>* <note>One who is currently experiencing the state of Devachan.</note> lives its intermediate cycle between two <term>incarnations</term> surrounded by everything it had aspired to in vain, and in the companionship of everyone it loved on earth. It has reached the fulfillment of all its soul-yearnings. And thus it lives throughout long centuries an existence of *unalloyed* happiness, which is the reward for its sufferings in earth-life. In short, it bathes in a sea of *uninterrupted* <term>felicity</term> <note>Great happiness or bliss.</note> spanned only by events of still greater <term>felicity</term> in degree.

with such delusions? Have you never met men and women living for years in a fool’s paradise? At any rate, it is a far more fascinating prospect than the orthodox golden harp with a pair of wings. 

The assurance that “the soul that lives ascends frequently and runs familiarly through the streets of the heavenly Jerusalem, visiting the patriarchs and prophets, saluting the apostles, and admiring the army of martyrs” may seem of a more pious character to some. # *THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.*

The sufferings of those from whom it is severed by death and whom it loves best, without being able to help them, would be a maddening thought. <note>The author argues that if a departed soul could actually see the misery of their living loved ones, heaven would be a place of torment rather than rest.</note>

ENQUIRER: There is something in your argument. I confess to having never seen it in this light.

THEOSOPHIST: Just so, and one must be selfish to the core and utterly devoid of the sense of retributive justice to have ever imagined such a thing. We are with those whom we have lost in material form, and far, far nearer to them now than when they were alive. And it is not only in the fancy of the *Devachanee* <note>original: "Devachanee"; a person experiencing the state of Devachan, a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note>, as some may imagine, but in reality. For pure divine love is not merely the blossom of a human heart, but has its roots in eternity. Spiritual holy love is immortal, and <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect; the sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences.</note> brings sooner or later all those who loved each other with such a spiritual affection to incarnate once more in the same family group. 

Again we say that love beyond the grave—illusion though you may call it—has a magic and divine potency which reacts on the living. A mother’s *Ego* <note>In Theosophy, the "Ego" refers to the permanent spiritual self that reincarnates, not the modern psychological definition of the "I".</note> filled with love for the imaginary children it sees near itself, living a life of happiness as real to it as when on earth—that love will always be felt by the children in the flesh. It will manifest in their dreams, and often in various events—in *providential* <note>occurring through divine foresight or intervention</note> protections and escapes, for love is a strong shield and is not limited by space or time. As with this Devachanic “mother,” so with the rest of human relationships and attachments, save those that are purely selfish or material. Analogy will suggest to you the rest.

ENQUIRER: In no case, then, do you admit the possibility of the communication of the living with the *disembodied spirit*?

<summary>The author explains that while the departed soul in Devachan is in a state of subjective bliss, their spiritual love remains a powerful, protective force that influences their living loved ones on Earth.</summary>

THEOSOPHIST. Yes, there is a case, and even two exceptions to the rule. The first exception occurs during the few days that immediately follow a person's death and before the *Ego* <note>The "Ego" here refers to the permanent spiritual self, not the modern psychological ego.</note> passes into the Devachanic state <note>original: "Devachanic state"; a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth.</note>. Whether any living mortal—except in a few exceptional cases—has derived much benefit from the return of the spirit into the objective plane is another question. (These exceptions occur when the intensity of the desire in the dying person to return for some purpose forced the higher consciousness to remain awake; in those instances, it was really the *individuality*, the "Spirit," that communicated.) Generally, the spirit is dazed after death and very soon falls into what we call "*pre-devachanic unconsciousness*." The second exception is found in the *Nirmanakayas* <note>original: "Nirmanakayas"; enlightened beings who choose to remain in the Earth's sphere to help humanity rather than entering a state of rest.</note>.

ENQUIRER. What about them? And what does the name mean to you?

THEOSOPHIST. It is the name given to those who have won the right to Nirvana <note>A state of liberation from the cycle of rebirth and suffering.</note> and cyclic rest, yet have renounced that state out of pity for mankind and those they left on earth. They do not enter "Devachan," as that is an illusion of our consciousness—a happy dream—and those who are fit for Nirvana must have entirely lost every desire or possibility of being affected by the world's illusions. Such an adept <term>adept: a highly skilled person; in this context, one who has attained secret knowledge and spiritual power</term>, or Saint, or whatever you may call him, believes it is a selfish act to rest in bliss while mankind groans under the burden of misery produced by ignorance. Therefore, he renounces Nirvana and determines to remain invisible in *spirit* on this earth. These beings have no material body, as they have left it behind; but otherwise, they remain with all their spiritual principles even in *astral life* <note>A level of existence beyond the physical, often associated with energy and emotion.</note> within our sphere. Such beings can and do communicate with a few chosen individuals, though certainly not through *ordinary mediums*.

<summary>The Theosophist explains two exceptions to the rule against spirit communication: the brief period immediately following death and the voluntary intervention of "Nirmanakayas," enlightened beings who forgo spiritual rest to assist humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Ego, Devachan, Nirmanakayas, Nirvana, spiritualism, afterlife, altruism, astral life, adept</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Enquirer and the Theosophist regarding the nature of spiritual existence and the specific role of Nirmanakayas.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

**ENQUIRER:** I have asked you the question about *Nirmanakayas* <note>In Theosophy, a Nirmanakaya is a being who has attained enlightenment but chooses to remain in the earthly sphere in an invisible form to help humanity.</note> because I read in some German and other works that it was the name given to the terrestrial appearances or bodies assumed by Buddhas in the Northern Buddhist teachings.

**THEOSOPHIST:** So they are, only the Orientalists <note>19th-century Western scholars who studied Eastern languages and cultures.</note> have confused this terrestrial body by understanding it to be *objective* and *physical* instead of purely astral and subjective.

**ENQUIRER:** And what good can they do on earth?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not much as regards individuals, as they have no right to interfere with Karma <note>The law of cause and effect; the spiritual principle where actions determine future outcomes.</note>, and can only advise and inspire mortals for the general good. Yet they perform more beneficent actions than you imagine.

**ENQUIRER:** Science would never subscribe to this, nor even modern psychology. For them, no portion of intelligence can survive the physical brain. What would you answer them?

**THEOSOPHIST:** I would not even go to the trouble of answering, but would simply say, in the words given to "M.A. Oxon" <note>The pseudonym of William Stainton Moses (1839–1892), a prominent English cleric and spiritualist.</note>, "Intelligence is perpetuated after the body is dead. Though it is not a question of the brain only. . . . It is reasonable to propound the indestructibility of the human spirit from what we know" (*Spirit Identity*, p. 69).

**ENQUIRER:** But "M.A. Oxon" is a Spiritualist?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Quite so, and the only *true* Spiritualist I know of, though we may still disagree with him on many a minor question. Apart from this, no Spiritualist comes nearer to the occult truths than he does. Like any one of us, he speaks incessantly "of the surface dangers that beset the ill-equipped, feather-headed muddler with the occult, who crosses the threshold without..."

<summary>The Theosophist explains that Nirmanakayas are subjective, astral beings rather than physical ones, and cites the spiritualist M.A. Oxon to argue for the survival of intelligence after death.</summary>
<keywords>Nirmanakaya, Buddhism, Karma, astral body, M.A. Oxon, William Stainton Moses, Spiritualism, occultism, consciousness after death</keywords>

"counting the cost." <note>original: "Some things that I do know of Spiritualism and some that I do not," a reference to a lecture by a prominent Spiritualist.</note> Our only disagreement rests in the question of "Spirit Identity." Otherwise, I, for one, coincide almost entirely with him, and accept the three propositions he embodied in his address of July, 1884. It is this eminent Spiritualist, rather, who disagrees with us, not we with him.

**ENQUIRER:** What are these propositions?

**THEOSOPHIST:** 
1. That there is a life coincident with, and independent of, the physical life of the body.
2. That, as a necessary corollary, this life extends beyond the life of the body (we say it extends throughout *Devachan* <term>the "land of the gods," a blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</term>).
3. That there is communication between the inhabitants of that state of existence and those of the world in which we now live.

All depend, you see, on the minor and secondary aspects of these fundamental propositions. Everything depends on the views we take of Spirit and Soul, or *Individuality* and *Personality*. Spiritualists confuse the two "into one"; we separate them, and say that, with the exceptions mentioned before, no Spirit will revisit the earth, though the animal Soul may. But let us return once more to our direct subject, the *Skandhas* <term>the five attributes or "bundles" that make up a human personality in Buddhist philosophy</term>.

**ENQUIRER:** I begin to understand better now. It is the Spirit, so to say, of those *Skandhas* which are the most ennobling, which, attaching themselves to the incarnating *Ego* <note>The permanent, reincarnating self as opposed to the temporary personality.</note>, survive and are added to the stock of its angelic experiences. And it is the attributes connected with the material *Skandhas*, with selfish and personal motives, which—

disappearing from the field of action between two incarnations, reappear at the subsequent incarnation as <term>Karmic</term> <note>Karma: The law of cause and effect; the sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences.</note> results to be atoned for; and therefore the Spirit will not leave <term>Devachan</term> <note>Devachan: In Theosophy, a temporary intermediate state between death and rebirth; a place of bliss where the soul rests.</note>. ->A FEW WORDS ABOUT THE SKANDHAS.<-

ENQUIRER. What becomes of the other, the lower <term>Skandhas</term> <note>Skandhas: In Buddhist and Theosophical thought, these are the five "bundles" or attributes that make up a human personality (form, feeling, perception, mental formations, and consciousness).</note> of the personality, after the death of the body? Are they quite destroyed?

THEOSOPHIST. They are and yet they are not—a fresh metaphysical and occult mystery for you. They are destroyed as the working stock in hand of the personality; they remain as *Karmic effects*, as germs, hanging in the atmosphere of the terrestrial plane, ready to come to life, as so many avenging fiends, to attach themselves to the new personality of the Ego when it reincarnates.

ENQUIRER. This really passes my comprehension, and is very difficult to understand.

THEOSOPHIST. Not once you have assimilated all the details. For then you will see that for logic, consistency, profound philosophy, divine mercy and equity, this doctrine of <term>Reincarnation</term> has not its equal on earth. It is a belief in a perpetual progress for
