--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Enquirer and the Theosophist regarding the evolution of the soul and the differences between Theosophical and Christian views on salvation.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
-> THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

Each incarnating Ego <note>The "Ego" here refers to the permanent spiritual self that reincarnates, not the modern psychological ego.</note>, or divine soul, moves through an evolution from the outward to the inward, from the material to the spiritual. At the end of each stage, it arrives at absolute unity with the divine Principle. From strength to strength, from the beauty and perfection of one plane to the greater beauty and perfection of another—with gains of new glory, fresh knowledge, and power in each cycle—such is the destiny of every Ego. In this way, the Ego becomes its own Savior in every world and incarnation.

**Enquirer.** But Christianity teaches the same thing. It also preaches progression.

**Theosophist.** Yes, but with the addition of something else. It tells us of the *impossibility* of attaining salvation without the aid of a miraculous Savior; therefore, it dooms to perdition all those who will not accept that dogma. This is precisely the difference between Christian theology and Theosophy. The former enforces belief in the descent of the Spiritual Ego into the *Lower Self* <note>The "Lower Self" refers to the temporary, earthly personality and its physical desires.</note>; the latter teaches the necessity of endeavoring to elevate oneself to the *Christos* <note>original: "Christos" (Greek); here referring to the universal Christ-principle or spiritual enlightenment.</note>, or *Buddhi* state <note>original: "Buddhi" (Sanskrit); the faculty of divine intuition and spiritual wisdom.</note>.

**Enquirer.** By teaching the annihilation of consciousness in case of failure, however, don’t you think that it amounts to the annihilation of *Self* in the opinion of those who are not mathematically or philosophically minded?

**Theosophist.** From the standpoint of those who believe in the *literal* resurrection of the body—and insist that every bone, every artery, and every atom of flesh will be raised bodily on the Judgment Day—of course it does. If you still insist that it is the perishable form and finite qualities that make up the *immortal* man, then we shall hardly understand each other. And if you do not understand that, by limiting the existence of every Ego to one life—

on earth, you make of Deity an ever-drunken Indra <note>A Vedic deity often depicted in later literal interpretations of the Puranas as having human failings.</note> of the Puranic dead letter, a cruel Moloch <note>An ancient Near Eastern deity associated with child sacrifice; used here to signify a bloodthirsty or heartless god.</note>, a god who makes an inextricable mess on Earth and yet claims thanks for it—then the sooner we drop the conversation, the better.

This is the point which interests most people. THEOSOPHIST: In one sense, we can acquire more knowledge; that is, we can develop further any faculty which we loved and strove after during life, provided it is concerned with abstract and ideal things, such as music, painting, poetry, etc., since <term>Devachan</term> is merely an idealized and subjective continuation of earth-life.

It is the rest of the <term>soul</term> from the toils of life.

ENQUIRER: But the scientific materialists assert that after the death of man nothing remains; that the human body simply disintegrates into its component elements; and that what we call the <term>soul</term> is merely a temporary <term>self-consciousness</term> produced as a byproduct of organic action, which will evaporate like steam. Is not theirs a strange state of mind?

### ON POST-MORTEM AND POST-NATAL CONSCIOUSNESS.
<note>Post-mortem: after death; Post-natal: after birth.</note>

ENQUIRER. But if human self-consciousness survives death as a general rule, why should there be exceptions?

THEOSOPHIST. In the fundamental principles of the spiritual world, no exception is possible. However, there are rules for those who choose to see, and rules for those who prefer to remain blind.

ENQUIRER. Quite so, I understand. This is like the error of a blind man who denies the existence of the sun because he does not see it. But after death, his spiritual eyes will certainly compel him to see. Is this what you mean?

THEOSOPHIST. He will not be compelled, nor will he see anything. Having persistently denied the continuation of existence after death during his life, he will be unable to see it. Because his spiritual capacity was stunted during life, it cannot develop after death, and he will remain blind. By insisting that he *must* see it, you evidently mean one thing and I mean another. You—

Between the *Ego* (the "I"), or *Ishvara* <note>original: Iswara; the Lord or the collective consciousness of the manifested deity</note> and *Prajna* <note>original: Pragna; individual wisdom or spiritual perception</note>, there is in reality no more difference than between a forest and its trees, or a lake and its waters, as the *Mandukya Upanishad* <note>original: Mundakya; an ancient Sanskrit text regarding the nature of reality and the self</note> teaches. One or even hundreds of trees that are dead from loss of vitality, or uprooted, are still incapable of preventing the forest from being a forest.

ENQUIRER: But, as I understand it, *Buddhi* <note>the spiritual soul or the faculty of intuition</note> represents the forest in this simile, and *Manas-Taijasi* <note>the human mind illuminated by the spirit</note> represents the trees. And if *Buddhi* is immortal, how can that which is similar to it—that is, *Manas-Taijasi*—entirely lose its consciousness until the day of its new incarnation? I cannot understand it.

THEOSOPHIST: You cannot understand it because you are mixing up an abstract representation of the whole with its temporary changes of form. Remember that while it can be said that *Buddhi-Manas* <note>the union of the spiritual soul and the human mind</note> is unconditionally immortal, the same cannot be said of the lower *Manas* <note>the everyday, terrestrial mind</note>, and even less of *Taijasi*, which is merely an attribute. Neither of these—neither *Manas* nor *Taijasi*—can exist apart from *Buddhi*, the divine soul. This is because the first (*Manas*) is, in its lower aspect, a qualifying characteristic of the earthly personality; and the second (*Taijasi*) is identical with the first, because it is the same *Manas* but with the light of *Buddhi* reflected upon it. In its turn, *Buddhi* would remain only an impersonal spirit with—

---

<margin>
*Ishvara* is the collective consciousness of the manifested deity, *Brahma*—that is, the collective consciousness of the Host of *Dhyan Chohans* <note>high spiritual beings or "Lords of Light"</note> (see *The Secret Doctrine*); and *Prajna* is their individual wisdom.
</margin>

<margin>
*Taijasi* means "the radiant" as a result of its union with *Buddhi*; that is, *Manas*, the human soul, illuminated by the radiance of the divine soul. Therefore, *Manas-Taijasi* may be described as the radiant mind—the *human reason* lit by the light of the spirit. *Buddhi-Manas* is the revelation of the divine *plus* human intellect and self-consciousness.
</margin>

without this element which it borrows from the human soul, which conditions it and makes of it—in this illusive Universe—*as it were* something separate from the universal soul for the entire period of the cycle of incarnation. Say rather that *Buddhi-Manas* <note>The union of the Spiritual Soul (Buddhi) and the Intellectual Soul (Manas).</note> can neither die nor lose its compound self-consciousness in Eternity, nor the recollection of its previous incarnations in which the two—that is, the spiritual and the human soul—had been closely linked together. 

But it is not so in the case of a materialist, whose human soul not only receives nothing from the divine soul, but even refuses to recognize its existence. You can hardly apply this principle to the attributes and qualities of the human soul, for it would be like saying that because your divine soul is immortal, therefore the bloom on your cheek must also be immortal; whereas this bloom, like Taijasi <note>original: Taijasi; refers to the radiant or "solar" state of the soul when illuminated by the spirit.</note>, is simply a transitory phenomenon.

**ENQUIRER:** Do I understand you to say that we must not mix in our minds the noumenon <note>original: noumenon; a thing as it is in itself, as distinct from how it appears to the senses.</note> with the phenomenon <note>original: phenomenon; an observable event or appearance.</note>, the cause with its effect?

**THEOSOPHIST:** I do say so, and repeat that, when limited to Manas <note>The human mind or soul.</note> or the human soul alone, the radiance of Taijasi itself becomes a mere question of time. This is because both immortality and consciousness after death become, for the terrestrial personality of man, simply conditioned attributes; they depend entirely on conditions and beliefs created by the human soul itself during the life of its body. Karma <note>The law of cause and effect.</note> acts incessantly: we reap in our *after-life* only the fruit of that which we have ourselves sown in this one.

**ENQUIRER:** But if my Ego <note>The "I" or the permanent individuality.</note> can, after the destruction of my body, become plunged in a state of entire unconsciousness, then where can be the punishment for the sins of my past life?

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Karma and the state of the soul after death, specifically addressing the concept of compensation for suffering.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.
### Page 161

**THEOSOPHIST.** Our philosophy teaches that Karmic punishment reaches the **Ego** <note>The permanent, spiritual self that reincarnates, as opposed to the temporary personality.</note> only in its next incarnation. After death, it receives only the reward for the unmerited sufferings endured during its past incarnation. <margin>* Some Theosophists have taken exception to this phrase, but the words are those of the **Master** <note>A reference to the Mahatmas or spiritual teachers believed to guide the Theosophical Society.</note>, and the meaning attached to the word “unmerited” is that given above. In the **T.P.S.** <note>Theosophical Publication Society</note> pamphlet Number 6, a phrase—criticized subsequently in the journal *Lucifer*—was used which was intended to convey the same idea. In form, however, it was awkward and open to the criticism directed against it; but the essential idea was that men often suffer from the effects of the actions done by others, effects which thus do not strictly belong to their own **Karma**—and for these sufferings they of course deserve compensation.</margin> The whole punishment after death, even for the materialist, consists, therefore, in the absence of any reward, and the utter loss of the consciousness of one’s bliss and rest. 

**Karma** is the child of the terrestrial **Ego**, the fruit of the actions of the tree which is the objective personality visible to all. It is just as much the fruit of all the thoughts and even motives of the spiritual “I.” But **Karma** is also the tender mother who heals the wounds inflicted by her during the preceding life, before she will begin to torture this **Ego** by inflicting new ones upon him. 

If it may be said that there is not a mental or physical suffering in the life of a mortal which is not the direct fruit and consequence of some sin in a preceding existence, on the other hand, there is a second factor to consider. Since the individual does not preserve the slightest recollection of that past sin in his actual life, and feels himself not deserving of such punishment—and therefore thinks he suffers for no guilt of his own—this alone is sufficient to entitle the human soul to the fullest consolation, rest, and bliss in his post-mortem <note>original: "post-mortem"; meaning after death</note> existence. Death comes to our spiritual selves ever as a deliverer and friend. For the materialist, who, not—

<summary>This page explains that while Karma punishes individuals in future lives, the period after death is a time of rest and compensation for "unmerited" sufferings caused by others or felt as unjust by the current personality.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Ego, reincarnation, post-mortem state, unmerited suffering, justice, Theosophy, afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the state of the human consciousness after death and the relationship between the personal self and the eternal Ego.</meta>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Notwithstanding his materialism, if he was not a bad man, the interval between the two lives will be like the unbroken and peaceful sleep of a child—either entirely dreamless, or filled with pictures of which he will have no definite perception. Meanwhile, for the average mortal, it will be a dream as vivid as life, and full of realistic bliss and visions.

**ENQUIRER:** Then the personal man must always go on suffering blindly the Karmic <note>Karmic: relating to Karma, the law of cause and effect where one's actions determine their future destiny.</note> penalties which the Ego <note>Ego: in Theosophy, the "Individual" or permanent spiritual self, as opposed to the "personality" of a single lifetime.</note> has incurred?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not quite. At the solemn moment of death, every person—even when death is sudden—sees the whole of their past life marshalled before them in its minutest details. For one short instant, the *personal* self becomes one with the *individual* and all-knowing *Ego*. This instant is enough to show them the whole chain of causes that have been at work during their life. They see and now understand themselves as they truly are, unadorned by flattery or self-deception. They read their life, remaining as a spectator looking down into the arena they are quitting; they feel and know the justice of all the suffering that has overtaken them.

**ENQUIRER:** Does this happen to everyone?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Without any exception. We are taught that very good and holy people see not only the life they are leaving, but even several preceding lives in which the causes were produced that made them what they were in the life just closing. They recognize the law of Karma in all its majesty and justice.

**ENQUIRER:** Is there anything corresponding to this before rebirth?

**THEOSOPHIST:** There is. Just as a person at the moment of death has a...

Just as a person has a retrospective insight into the life he has led at the moment of death, so too, at the moment he is reborn onto earth, the *Ego* <note>The "Higher Self" or permanent individuality in Theosophy</note> awakes from the state of Devachan <note>A temporary state of bliss or "heaven" between incarnations</note> and has a prospective vision of the life which awaits him. He realizes all the causes that have led to it. He realizes them and sees the future because it is in the interval between Devachan and rebirth that the *Ego* regains his full *manasic* consciousness <note>Mind-consciousness; from the Sanskrit 'Manas' meaning mind</note>. For a short time, he becomes again the god he was before he first descended into matter and incarnated in the first man of flesh, in compliance with Karmic law. The “golden thread” sees all its “pearls” and does not miss a single one of them.

## *WHAT IS REALLY MEANT BY ANNIHILATION.*

ENQUIRER: I have heard some Theosophists speak of a "golden thread" on which their lives were strung. What do they mean by this?

THEOSOPHIST: In the Hindu Sacred books, it is said that that which undergoes periodical incarnation is the *Sutratma*, which means literally the “Thread Soul” <note>original: *Sutratma*, from 'sutra' (thread) and 'atman' (soul)</note>. It is a synonym for the reincarnating *Ego*—the *Manas* <note>The mind or intellect</note> conjoined with *Buddhi* <note>The spiritual soul or faculty of intuition</note>—which absorbs the mental recollections of all our preceding lives. It is called this because the long series of human lives are strung together on that one thread, like pearls on a string. In some of the Upanishads <note>Ancient Sanskrit texts containing the central philosophical concepts of Hinduism</note>, these recurrent rebirths are likened to the life of a mortal which oscillates periodically between sleep and waking.

ENQUIRER: This, I must say, does not seem very clear, and I will tell you why. For the man who awakes, another day commences, but that man is the same in soul and body as he was the day before;

<summary>This page explains the "Thread Soul" (Sutratma) concept, describing how the reincarnating Ego previews its upcoming life and maintains a continuous connection across multiple incarnations like pearls on a string.</summary>
<keywords>Ego, Devachan, Manas, Buddhi, Sutratma, Thread Soul, Reincarnation, Karma, Upanishads, Theosophy</keywords>

The simile <note>Referring to a previous comparison between sleep and death</note> does not seem to me quite correct. The man who arises from sleep remembers quite clearly what he has done yesterday, the day before, and even months and years ago. But none of us has the slightest recollection of a preceding life or of any fact or event concerning it. . . . I may forget in the morning what I have dreamt during the night, still I know that I have slept and have the certainty that I lived during sleep; but what recollection can I have of my past incarnation until the moment of death? How do you reconcile this?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Some people do recollect their past incarnations during life; but these are Buddhas and Initiates. This is what the Yogis call *Samma-Sambuddha* <note>Sanskrit: Perfect, complete enlightenment; the ability to remember all past lives</note>, or the knowledge of the whole series of one's past incarnations.

**ENQUIRER.** But we ordinary mortals who have not reached *Samma-Sambuddha*, how are we to understand this simile?

**THEOSOPHIST.** By studying it and trying to understand more correctly the characteristics and the three kinds of sleep. Sleep is a general and immutable law for man as for beast, but there are different kinds of sleep and still more different dreams and visions.

**ENQUIRER.** But this takes us to another subject. Let us return to the materialist who, while not denying dreams—which he could hardly do—yet denies immortality in general and the survival of his own individuality.

**THEOSOPHIST.** And the materialist, without knowing it, is right. One who has no inner perception of, and faith in, the immortality of his soul—in that man the soul can never become *Buddhi-taijasi* <note>Sanskrit: The "radiant" or spiritualized mind; the state where the human soul merges with the divine spirit</note>, but will remain simply *Manas* <note>Sanskrit: The mind or human intellect</note>, and for *Manas* alone there is no...

If a person cannot be convinced of anything that cannot be proven to him by his five senses, or by scientific reasoning based exclusively on the data provided by those senses—despite their inadequacy—and if he rejects every spiritual manifestation and accepts physical life as the only conscious existence, then it shall be done unto them according to their beliefs. They will lose their personal Ego <note>In this context, "Ego" refers to the temporary, personal identity or "I" of the current lifetime.</note> and will plunge into a dreamless sleep until a new awakening.

ENQUIRER: Is that really so?

THEOSOPHIST: Almost so. Remember the practically universal teaching of the two kinds of conscious existence: the terrestrial <note>earthly</note> and the spiritual. The latter must be considered real for the very reason that it is inhabited by the eternal, changeless, and immortal Monad <note>term: Monad; the unified essence of the spirit and soul that survives through all incarnations.</note>. In contrast, the incarnating Ego dresses itself up in new garments entirely different from those of its previous incarnations. In these temporary identities, everything except its spiritual prototype is doomed to a change so radical that it leaves no trace behind.

ENQUIRER: How can that be? Can my conscious earthly "I" perish—not only for a time, like the consciousness of the materialist, but so entirely as to leave no trace behind?

THEOSOPHIST: According to the teaching, it must perish in its fullness, except for that principle which, having united itself with the Monad, has thereby become a purely spiritual and indestructible essence, one with the Monad in Eternity. But in the case of an absolute materialist, in whose personal "I" no *Buddhi* <note>term: Buddhi; the faculty of spiritual intuition or the "Spiritual Soul" that connects the human mind to the divine spirit.</note> has ever reflected itself, how can the *Buddhi* carry away into Eternity even one particle of that earthly personality? Your spiritual "I" is immortal; but from your present self, it can carry away into Eternity only that which has become worthy of immortality—namely, the aroma alone of the flower that has been mown down by death.

**ENQUIRER:** Well, and what of the flower—the terrestrial "I"?

These hours mark the periods of terrestrial life and spiritual life. It is true that these periods are limited in their duration. It is also true that the very number of such stages in Eternity—alternating between sleep and awakening, illusion and reality—has a beginning and an end. On the other hand, however, the spiritual pilgrim is eternal. 

Therefore, the hours of his life after death <note>original: "post-mortem"</note> are the only reality in our conception. During these times, he is disembodied and stands face to face with truth, rather than the mirages of his transitory earthly existences, throughout the period of that pilgrimage which we call "the cycle of <term>re-births</term>." Such intervals, despite their limitations, do not prevent the <term>Ego</term> from following the path to its last transformation. While ever perfecting itself, the Ego moves undeviatingly, though gradually and slowly, toward the goal of becoming a divine being. These intervals and stages help toward this final result instead of hindering it; and without such limited...

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Without these intervals, the divine **Ego** <note>The permanent spiritual self that survives death.</note> could never reach its ultimate goal. I have already given you a familiar illustration by comparing the **Ego**, or the **individuality**, to an actor, and its numerous and various incarnations to the parts it plays. Would you call these parts or their costumes the individuality of the actor himself? Like that actor, the **Ego** is forced to play many parts—some of which may be unpleasant to it—during the "cycle of necessity" <note>The long period of required incarnations in the material world.</note> up to the very threshold of **Paranirvana** <note>The highest state of spiritual existence and liberation.</note>. 

But as the bee collects its honey from every flower, leaving the rest as food for the earthly worms, so does our spiritual individuality, whether we call it <term>Sutratma</term> <note>Literally "thread-soul"; the immortal element that links all incarnations together.</note> or <term>Ego</term>. It collects only the nectar of spiritual qualities and self-consciousness from every terrestrial personality into which <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect that determines the conditions of one's life.</note> forces it to incarnate. It unites all these into one whole and emerges from its chrysalis as the glorified <term>Dhyan Chohan</term> <note>A high-ranking celestial being or "Lord of Light."</note>. So much the worse for those terrestrial personalities from which it could collect nothing. Such personalities certainly cannot consciously outlive their terrestrial existence.

**ENQUIRER:** Thus, then, it seems that for the terrestrial personality, immortality is still conditional. Is immortality itself, then, not unconditional?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Not at all. But immortality cannot touch the *non-existent*; for everything that exists as <term>SAT</term> <note>Sanskrit for "Absolute Being" or "Truth."</note>, or emanates from <term>SAT</term>, immortality and **Eternity** are absolute. Matter is the opposite pole of spirit, and yet the two are one. The essence of all this—that is, Spirit, Force, and Matter, or the three in one—is as endless as it is beginningless. However, the form acquired by this triple unity during its incarnations—its external appearance—is certainly...

**Theosophist:** No one ever said such a thing. No materialist, however unbelieving, can die forever in the fullness of his spiritual individuality. **Enquirer:** But surely this is annihilation?

**Theosophist:** Certainly not. One can sleep a "dead sleep" <note>a deep, dreamless state</note> and miss several stations during a long railway journey, without the slightest recollection or consciousness, and then awake at another station and continue the journey past innumerable other halting-places until the end of the trip or the goal is reached. Three kinds of sleep were mentioned to you: the dreamless, the chaotic, and the one which is so real that to the sleeping man...

From this point forward, we have started our expositions of, and discussion about, the "Principles," using their Sanskrit names instead of immediately coining English equivalents for the use of Theosophists. We must try and remedy this now.

**ENQUIRER:** You will do well to do so, as it may avoid further confusion; it seems to me that no two Theosophical writers have hitherto agreed to call the same "Principle" by the same name.

"The human soul, once launched on the streams of evolution as a human individuality <note>The "reincarnating Ego," or "Human Soul," as he called it; known as the *Causal Body* <note>original: *Karana Sharira*</note> among the Hindus.</note>, passes through alternate periods of physical and relatively spiritual existence. It passes from the one plane, or stratum, or condition of nature to the other under the guidance of its Karmic <note>term: Karma refers to the law of cause and effect; "Karmic affinities" are the natural attractions or connections created by past actions.</note> affinities. It lives in incarnations the life which its Karma has pre-ordained, modifying its progress within the limitations of circumstances, and—developing fresh Karma by its use or abuse of opportunities—it returns..."

to spiritual existence (<term>Devachan</term> <note>A state of blissful rest between incarnations, similar to a temporary heaven.</note>) after each physical life—passing through the intervening region of <term>Kamaloka</term> <note>A "desire-world" or semi-material plane where the soul sheds its lower earthly passions before moving on.</note>—for rest and refreshment and for the gradual absorption into its essence of the life’s experience gained “on earth” or during physical existence, which constitutes cosmic progress. This view of the matter will, moreover, have suggested many related inferences to anyone thinking over the subject. 

For instance, the transfer of consciousness from the Kamaloka stage to the Devachanic stage of this progression would necessarily be gradual*; in truth, no hard-and-fast line separates the varieties of spiritual conditions. Even the spiritual and physical planes—as psychic faculties in living people show—are not so hopelessly walled off from one another as materialistic theories would suggest. All states of nature are all around us simultaneously and appeal to different perceptive faculties, and so on. . . . 

It is clear that during physical existence, people who possess psychic faculties remain in connection with the planes of superphysical consciousness. Although most people may not be endowed with such faculties, we all are capable of entering into conditions of consciousness that the five physical senses have nothing to do with, as shown by the phenomena of sleep, and especially those of <term>somnambulism</term> <note>Sleepwalking or states of altered consciousness.</note> or <term>mesmerism</term> <note>An early term for hypnosis or the influence of "animal magnetism."</note>. 

We—the souls within us—are not, as it were, altogether adrift in the ocean of matter. We clearly retain some surviving interest or rights in the shore from which, for a time, we have floated off. The process of incarnation, therefore, is not fully described when we speak of an *alternate* existence on the physical and spiritual planes, picturing the soul as a complete entity slipping entirely from one state of existence to the other. The more correct definitions of the process would probably represent incarnation as taking place on this physical plane of nature by reason of an efflux emanating from the soul. The spiritual realm would all the while be the proper habitat of the Soul, which would never entirely quit it; and

<margin>* The length of this “transfer” depends, however, on the degree of spirituality in the former personality of the disembodied <term>Ego</term> <note>In Theosophy, the "Ego" refers to the permanent, reincarnating spiritual self, not the modern psychological ego.</note>. For those whose lives were very spiritual, this transfer, though gradual, is very rapid. The time becomes longer for those who are materialistically inclined.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinctions between the various components of the human constitution, specifically clarifying the terminology used for the "Higher Self" versus the "Higher Ego."</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

that non-materializable portion of the Soul which abides permanently on the spiritual plane may fitly, perhaps, be spoken of as the HIGHER SELF."

This “Higher Self” is ATMA, and of course it is “non-materializable,” as Mr. Sinnett says. Even more, it can never be “objective” <note>original: "objective" — meaning it cannot be perceived as an external object or thing separate from the observer</note> under any circumstances, even to the highest spiritual perception. For Atman or the “Higher Self” is really Brahma, the ABSOLUTE, and indistinguishable from it. In hours of <term>Samadhi</term> <note>A state of intense concentration or deep meditation where the individual consciousness merges with the universal</note>, the higher spiritual consciousness of the Initiate is entirely absorbed in the ONE essence, which is Atman; and therefore, being one with the whole, there can be nothing objective for it. 

Now some of our Theosophists have got into the habit of using the words “Self” and “Ego” as synonymous, and of associating the term “Self” with only man’s higher individual or even personal “Self” or Ego, whereas this term ought never to be applied except to the One universal Self. Hence the confusion. Speaking of <term>Manas</term> <note>The mind or the thinking faculty</note>, the “causal body,” we may call it—when connecting it with the Buddhic radiance—the “HIGHER EGO,” never the “Higher Self.” For even <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The spiritual soul or the faculty of intuition</note>, the “Spiritual Soul,” is not the SELF, but the vehicle only of SELF. All the other “Selves”—such as the “Individual” self and “personal” self—ought never to be spoken or written of without their qualifying and characteristic adjectives.

Thus in this most excellent essay on the “Higher Self,” this term is applied to the *sixth* principle or <term>Buddhi</term> (of course in conjunction with <term>Manas</term>, as without such union there would be no *thinking* principle or element in the spiritual soul); and has in consequence given rise to just such misunderstandings. The statement that “a child does not acquire its *sixth* principle...

<summary>The author clarifies the distinction between the "Higher Self" (Atma), which is universal and absolute, and the "Higher Ego" (Manas-Buddhi), which is the individual thinking principle. She warns against using these terms interchangeably to avoid spiritual confusion.</summary>
<keywords>Atma, Brahma, Samadhi, Manas, Buddhi, Higher Self, Higher Ego, spiritual soul, human constitution, terminology</keywords>

The statement that a child does not become a morally responsible being capable of generating <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect; the moral consequences of one's actions.</note> until they are seven years old proves what is meant in this context by the <term>HIGHER SELF</term>. Therefore, the talented author is quite justified in explaining that after the “<term>Higher Self</term>” has entered the human being and saturated the personality—which occurs only in those with more refined constitutions—with its consciousness, “people with psychic faculties may indeed perceive this <term>Higher Self</term> through their finer senses from time to time.” 

However, those who limit the term “<term>Higher Self</term>” strictly to the Universal <term>Divine Principle</term> are also “justified” in misunderstanding him. For when we read—without being prepared for this shifting of <term>metaphysical terms</term>*—that while “fully manifesting on the physical plane . . . the <term>Higher Self</term> still remains a conscious spiritual <term>Ego</term> on the corresponding plane of <term>Nature</term>,” we are likely to interpret the “<term>Higher Self</term>” in that sentence as “<term>Atma</term>” <note>The supreme universal spirit.</note> and the spiritual <term>Ego</term> as “<term>Manas</term>” <note>The mind or intellect.</note> or rather *<term>Buddhi-Manas</term>* <note>The spiritual or intuitive mind.</note>, and immediately criticize the entire explanation as incorrect.

To avoid such misunderstandings from now on, I propose to translate the <term>Occult Eastern terms</term> literally into their English equivalents and offer these for future use:

->**THE <term>HIGHER SELF</term> IS**<-

{ <term>Atma</term>, the inseparable ray of the Universal and <term>One Self</term>. It is the <term>God above</term> us, rather than merely within us. Happy is the person who succeeds in saturating their *<term>inner Ego</term>* with it!

* The “<term>Shifting of Metaphysical terms</term>” mentioned here refers only to the variation in how Eastern expressions are translated into English. Until now, there have never been established English terms for these concepts; every <term>Theosophist</term> <note>A student of Theosophy, a philosophy seeking knowledge of the divine through spiritual ecstasy and intuition.</note> has had to coin their own terms to express their thoughts. It is high time, then, to settle on a definite set of names.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the human constitution according to Theosophical doctrine, specifically defining the different aspects of the "Ego" and the principle of Life.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY</header>

**THE SPIRITUAL divine Ego is** the Spiritual soul or *Buddhi* <note>The faculty of intuition or spiritual discernment</note>, in close union with *Manas*, the mind-principle. Without this union, it is no Ego at all, but only the *Atmic Vehicle* <note>The carrier or vessel for the highest universal spirit</note>.

**THE INNER, or HIGHER “Ego” is** *Manas*, the so-called “Fifth” Principle, considered independently of *Buddhi*. The Mind-Principle is only the Spiritual Ego when merged into one with *Buddhi*. A materialist is assumed to have no such Ego in him, however great his intellectual capacities may be. It is the permanent *Individuality* or the “Re-incarnating Ego.”

**THE LOWER, or PERSONAL “Ego” is** the physical man in conjunction with his *lower* Self—that is, animal instincts, passions, desires, and so on. It is called the “false personality” and consists of the lower *Manas* <note>The intellect focused on worldly matters</note> combined with *Kama-rupa* <note>The "desire-form" or emotional body</note>, operating through the physical body and its phantom or “double” <note>The ethereal counterpart to the physical body</note>.

The remaining “Principle,” “*Prana*” or “Life,” is, strictly speaking, the radiating force or energy of *Atma* <note>The supreme Universal Self</note>. It is the lower or rather—in its effects—more physical aspect of the Universal Life and the One Self, because it is manifesting in the world. *Prana*, or Life, permeates the whole being of the objective Universe; it is called a “principle” only because it is an indispensable factor and the *power behind the scenes* <note>original: "deus ex machina," literally "a god from a machine," referring to a providential force that drives the mechanism of the living man</note> of the living man.

ENQUIRER: This division, being so much simplified in its combinations, will work better, I believe. The other system is much too metaphysical.

THEOSOPHIST: If outsiders as well as Theosophists would agree to it, it would certainly make matters much more comprehensible.

<summary>This page defines the three aspects of the human "Ego"—Spiritual, Higher, and Lower—and explains Prana as the vital energy driving the human mechanism.</summary>
<keywords>Ego, Buddhi, Manas, Atma, Prana, Individuality, Personality, Kama-rupa, Five Principles, Human Constitution</keywords>

->ON THE NATURE OF OUR THINKING PRINCIPLE.<-

->THE MYSTERY OF THE EGO.<-

ENQUIRER. I perceive in the quotation you brought forward a little while ago from the *Buddhist Catechism* a discrepancy that I would like to hear explained. It is there stated that the Skandhas <note>original: "Skandhas"; the five attributes or elements of conditioned existence that make up a human being</note>—memory included—change with every new incarnation. And yet, it is asserted that the reflection of the past lives, which, we are told, are entirely made up of Skandhas, “must survive.” At the present moment I am not quite clear in my mind as to what it is precisely that survives, and I would like to have it explained. What is it? Is it only that “reflection,” or those Skandhas, or always that same Ego, the Manas <note>original: "Manas"; the Sanskrit term for the mind or the "thinking faculty"</note>?

THEOSOPHIST. I have just explained that the re-incarnating Principle, or that which we call the *divine man*, is indestructible throughout the life cycle: indestructible as a thinking *Entity*, and even as an ethereal form. The “reflection” is only the spiritualized remembrance, during the Devachanic period <note>original: "Devachanic period"; the blissful intermediate state between death and rebirth</note>, of the former personality—Mr. A. or Mrs. B.—with which the *Ego* identifies itself during that period. Since the latter is but the continuation of the earth-life, so to speak—the very peak and highest point in an unbroken series of the few happy moments in that now past

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

existence, the *Ego* <note>The permanent, spiritual self.</note> has to identify itself with the *personal* consciousness of that life, if anything of it is to remain.

<margin>ENQUIRER.</margin> This means that the *Ego*, despite its divine nature, passes every such period between two incarnations in a state of mental clouding, or temporary insanity.

<margin>THEOSOPHIST.</margin> You may regard it however you like. Believing that, outside the ONE Reality, nothing is more than a passing illusion—the whole Universe included—we do not view it as insanity. Instead, we see it as a very natural sequence or development of the terrestrial <note>Earthly.</note> life. What is life? It is a bundle of the most varied experiences, of daily changing ideas, emotions, and opinions. In our youth, we are often enthusiastically devoted to an ideal, or to some hero or heroine whom we try to follow and emulate. A few years later, when the freshness of our youthful feelings has faded and sobered down, we are the first to laugh at our former fancies. 

And yet, there was a day when we had so thoroughly identified our own personality with that of the ideal in our mind—especially if it was that of a living being—that the former was entirely merged and lost in the latter. Can it be said of a man of fifty that he is the same being that he was at twenty? The *inner man* is the same; the outward living personality is completely transformed and changed. Would you also call these changes in human mental states "insanity"?

<margin>ENQUIRER.</margin> How would *you* name them, and especially, how would you explain the permanence of one and the fleeting nature <note>original: "evanescence"</note> of the other?

<margin>THEOSOPHIST.</margin> We have our own doctrine ready, and to us it offers no difficulty. The clue lies in the double consciousness of our mind, and also in the dual nature of the mental “*principle*.”

<summary>The Theosophist explains that the Ego's state between lives is a natural development of earthly experience rather than insanity, comparing the shifts in consciousness to the way a person changes from youth to middle age.</summary>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

There is a spiritual consciousness—the *Manasic* <note>Relating to Manas, the Sanskrit term for the Mind or the human soul</note> mind illumined by the light of *Buddhi* <note>The spiritual soul or the faculty of intuition</note>—which subjectively perceives abstractions. There is also the sentient consciousness (the lower *Manasic* light), which is inseparable from our physical brain and senses. This latter consciousness is held in subjection by the brain and physical senses; because it is equally dependent on them, it must, of course, fade out and finally die with the disappearance of the brain and physical senses. It is only the former kind of consciousness, whose root lies in eternity, which survives and lives forever, and may, therefore, be regarded as immortal. Everything else belongs to passing illusions.

ENQUIRER: What do you really understand by "illusion" in this case?

THEOSOPHIST: It is very well described in the previously mentioned essay on “The Higher Self.” Its author says:

> “The theory we are considering (the interchange of ideas between the *Higher Ego* and the lower self) harmonizes very well with the treatment of this world in which we live as a phenomenal world of illusion <note>original: "maya"</note>. The spiritual planes of nature, on the other hand, are the noumenal <note>The world of things as they are in themselves, rather than how they appear to the senses</note> world or plane of reality. That region of nature in which the permanent soul is rooted is more real than the region in which its transitory blossoms appear for a brief space, only to wither and fall to pieces while the plant recovers energy to send forth a fresh flower. Supposing that only flowers were perceptible to our ordinary senses, and their roots existed in a state of nature that was intangible and invisible to us; philosophers in such a world who divined that there were such things as roots in another...”

<summary>This page distinguishes between the immortal spiritual consciousness and the mortal physical consciousness, using the metaphor of a permanent root and transitory flowers to explain the concept of worldly illusion.</summary>

plane of existence would be apt to say of the flowers, "These are not the real plants; they are of no relative importance, merely illusive phenomena of the moment."

This is what I mean. The world in which the transitory and fleeting flowers of personal lives blossom is not the real, permanent world. The real world is the one in which we find the root of consciousness—that root which is beyond illusion and dwells in eternity.

ENQUIRER: What do you mean by the root dwelling in eternity?

THEOSOPHIST: I mean by this root the thinking entity, the Ego which incarnates, whether we regard it as an "*Angel*," "*Spirit*," or a *Force*. Of everything that falls under our sensory perceptions, only what grows directly from, or is attached to, this invisible root above can partake of its immortal life. Therefore, every noble thought, idea, and aspiration of the personality it inhabits—proceeding from and fed by this root—must become permanent. As for the physical consciousness, since it is a quality of the sentient but lower "principle" (<term>Kama-rupa</term> <note>Sanskrit: The "desire-body" or seat of animal instincts</note> or animal instinct, illuminated by the lower <term>manasic</term> <note>Relating to Manas, the mind</note> reflection), or the human Soul—it must disappear. That which displays activity while the body is asleep or paralyzed is the higher consciousness; our memory registers such experiences only feebly and inaccurately—because it does so automatically—and often fails to be even slightly impressed by them.

“...that he, whom you regard as ‘the God of Gods’ and the One living God, is *so weak* as to allow evil (or the Devil) to have the best of *him* as much as of all his creatures—whether while he remains in Heaven, or during the time he was incarnated on this earth?” You are sure to reply again: “This is a Mystery; and we are forbidden to pry into the mysteries of God.” Not being forbidden to do so by our religious philosophy, I answer your question: unless a God descends as an *Avatar* <note>A divine incarnation or "descent" of a deity into earthly form</note>, no divine principle can be anything other than cramped and paralyzed by turbulent, animal matter. Heterogeneity <note>The state of being diverse or composed of different parts; here, the chaotic material world</note> will always have the upper hand over homogeneity <note>The state of being all the same or uniform; here, the pure spiritual essence</note> on this plane of illusions. The nearer an essence is to its root-principle, Primordial Homogeneity, the more difficult it is for that essence to assert itself on earth. Spiritual and divine powers lie dormant in every human being; and the wider the sweep of a person's spiritual vision, the mightier will be the God within them. But because few people can feel that God—and since, as a general rule, deity is always bound and limited in our thoughts by our earlier conceptions and the ideas instilled in us from childhood—it is very difficult for you to understand our philosophy.

ENQUIRER. And is it this Ego of ours which is our God?

THEOSOPHIST. Not at all; “A God” is not the universal deity, but only a spark from the one ocean of Divine Fire. Our God *within* us, or “our Father in Secret,” is what we call the “HIGHER SELF,” or *Atma* <note>The Sanskrit term for the supreme universal self or spirit</note>. Our incarnating Ego was a God in its origin, as were all the primeval emanations of the One Unknown Principle. But since its “fall into Matter,” having to incarnate throughout...

<page-num>182</page-num>
# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Throughout the cycle, in succession from first to last, the spirit is no longer a free and happy god, but a poor pilgrim on his way to regain that which he has lost. I can answer you more fully by repeating what is said of the **INNER MAN** in ***Isis Unveiled*** <note>A major work by H.P. Blavatsky published in 1877</note> (Volume II, page 593):

> "From the remotest antiquity, mankind as a whole has always been *convinced of the existence of a personal spiritual entity within the personal physical man*. This inner entity was more or less divine, according to its proximity to the 'crown' <note>Referring to the highest spiritual principle or the divine source</note>. The closer the union, the more serene man’s destiny and the less dangerous the external conditions. This belief is neither bigotry nor superstition, but only an ever-present, instinctive feeling of the proximity of another spiritual and invisible world. Though this world is subjective <note>internal or perceived by the mind</note> to the senses of the outward man, it is perfectly objective <note>real and external</note> to the *inner ego*. Furthermore, they believed that there are external and internal conditions which affect the determination of our will upon our actions. They rejected fatalism, for fatalism implies a blind course directed by some even blinder power. But they believed in *destiny or Karma*, which from birth to death every man is weaving thread by thread around himself, as a spider does its cobweb. This destiny is guided by that presence termed by some the *guardian angel*, or our more intimate *astral inner man* <note>The subtle, spiritual body that survives the physical one</note>, who is but too often the 'evil genius' of the man of flesh, or the *personality*. Both of these lead **MAN**, but one of them must prevail; and from the very beginning of the invisible affray <note>struggle or battle</note>, the stern and implacable law of compensation and retribution steps in and takes its course, following faithfully the fluctuations of the conflict. When the last strand is woven, and man is seemingly enwrapped under the empire of this *self-made destiny*, it then either fixes him like an inert shell against an immovable rock, or like a feather carries him away in a whirlwind raised by his own actions."

Such is the destiny of the **MAN**—the true *Ego* <note>The permanent, reincarnating individuality</note>, not the *Automaton* <note>The physical body or temporary personality</note>, the shell that goes by that name. It is for him to become the conqueror over matter.

### THE COMPLEX NATURE OF MANAS.

**ENQUIRER:** But you wanted to tell me something of the essential nature of **Manas** <term>the mind-principle or the reincarnating Ego</term>, and of the relation in which the **Skandhas** <term>the five attributes or "bundles" that make up a physical personality, such as form, sensation, and perception</term> of physical man stand to it?

**THEOSOPHIST:** It is this nature—mysterious, ever-changing <note>original: "Protean," referring to the Greek sea-god Proteus who could change his shape at will</note>, beyond any grasp, and almost shadowy in its connections with the other principles—that is most difficult to realize, and still more so to explain. **Manas** is a "principle," and yet it is also an "Entity," an individuality, or an **Ego**. He is a "God," and yet he is doomed to an endless cycle of incarnations, for each of which he is made responsible, and for each of which he has to suffer. All this seems as contradictory as it is puzzling; nevertheless, there are hundreds of people, even in Europe, who realize all this perfectly, for they understand the **Ego** not only in its integrity but in its many aspects. Finally, if I am to make myself understood, I must begin at the beginning and give you the genealogy of this **Ego** in a few lines.

**ENQUIRER:** Please, continue.

**THEOSOPHIST:** Try to imagine a "Spirit," a celestial Being—whether we call it by one name or another—that is divine in its essential nature, yet not pure enough to be *one with the* **ALL** <note>the ultimate, universal divine source</note>. In order to achieve this, it must purify its nature so as to finally reach that goal. It can do so only by passing individually and personally—that is, both spiritually and physically—through every experience and feeling that exists in the manifold or "differentiated" Universe <note>the physical and mental world of separate things, as opposed to the unified divine source</note>. It has, therefore, after having gained such...

<summary>The Theosophist begins to explain the complex nature of Manas (the mind or Ego) as both a divine entity and a being subject to the cycle of suffering and reincarnation. experience in the lower kingdoms <note>the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms</note>, and having ascended higher and higher with every rung on the ladder of existence, it must pass through every experience on the human planes. In its very essence it is **THOUGHT**, and is therefore called in its plural form *Manasa putra* <note>original Sanskrit: "Sons of the (Universal) mind"</note>. This individualized "Thought" is what we Theosophists call the real human **EGO**—the thinking Entity imprisoned in a body of flesh and bones. * (**a**) Their essential, inherent characteristic, which is the heaven-aspiring mind (higher *Manas*).
* (**b**) The human quality of thinking, or animal thought <note>original: "cogitation"</note>, which is rationalized because of the superiority of the human brain; this is the *Kama*-tending <term>desire-driven</term> or lower *Manas*. 

One gravitates toward **BUDDHI** <term>the spiritual soul or higher intuition</term>; the other tends downward toward the seat of passions and animal desires. These lower desires have no room in **DEVACHAN** <term>a blissful state of consciousness between death and rebirth</term>, nor can they associate with the divine triad <note>The three highest principles of man: Atma, Buddhi, and Manas</note> which ascends as **ONE** into mental bliss. **ENQUIRER:** But how is it that this doctrine, which you say is as old as thinking men, has found no room in, say, Christian theology?

Theology calls the Ego the "Angel" that God gives us at the moment of our birth to take care of our Soul. Instead of holding that "Angel" responsible for the transgressions of the poor, helpless "Soul," it is the latter which—according to theological logic—is punished for all the sins of both flesh and mind! ->THE DOCTRINE IS TAUGHT IN ST. JOHN'S GOSPEL.<-

**ENQUIRER:** To this, those who follow this belief might answer that even if orthodox dogma promises the unrepentant sinner and materialist a difficult time in a rather too literal Hell <note>original: "Inferno"</note>, it also gives them a chance for repentance until the very last minute. Furthermore, they do not teach annihilation or the loss of personality, which they consider the same thing.

**THEOSOPHIST:** While the Church may teach nothing of the kind, Jesus, on the other hand, does; and that means something, at least to those who place Christ higher than Christianity.

**ENQUIRER:** Does Christ teach anything of the sort?

Christ—or at least the fourth Gospel—teaches <term>reincarnation</term> <note>the rebirth of a soul in a new body</note> as well as the annihilation of the personality, if you only look past the literal meaning <note>original: "dead letter"</note> and hold to the inner, spiritual sense <note>original: "esoteric Spirit"</note>. Atma <note>the universal, supreme Spirit</note> is the Husbandman—the Spiritual Ego or *Buddhi* <note>the spiritual soul</note> (Christos) is the Vine, while the animal and vital Soul—the personality—is the "branch." "I am the true vine, and my Father is the Husbandman. Every branch in me that does not bear fruit he takes away . . . As the branch cannot bear fruit by itself unless it remains in the vine, neither can you, unless you remain in me. I am the Vine—you are the branches. If a man does not remain in me, he is thrown out like a branch and withers; they are gathered and thrown into the fire and burned."

We explain it in this way: because we do not believe in the literal hell-fires that theology claims lie behind the threat to the "branches," we say that the "Husbandman" represents Atma, the symbol for the infinite, impersonal Principle;* while the Vine stands for the Spiritual Soul, *Christos*, and each "branch" represents a new incarnation.

**ENQUIRER:** But what proof do you have to support such an arbitrary interpretation?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Universal symbolism <note>original: "symbology"</note> confirms its correctness and proves that it is not arbitrary. Hermas <note>an early Christian writer, author of "The Shepherd of Hermas"</note> says of "God" that he "planted the Vineyard"—meaning he created mankind. In the *Kabbalah* <note>original: "Kabala"; a system of Jewish mysticism</note>, it is shown that the "Ancient of Ancients" <note>original: "Aged of the Aged"</note>, or the "Long Face" <note>a Kabbalistic term for the first emanation of the divine</note>, plants a vineyard (representing mankind) and a vine (meaning Life). They will never accept it.

**THEOSOPHIST.** That cannot change a fact of nature—if it truly is a fact—nor can it prevent such a thing from occasionally happening. If you break or damage the centripetal motion of the earthly soul—which naturally tends toward the center that attracts it—or if you halt its progress by clogging it with a heavier weight of matter than it can bear, or than is fit for the Devachanic state <note>the "land of the gods"; a blissful, temporary state of consciousness between death and rebirth</note>, the harmony of the whole will be destroyed.

Personal life—or perhaps more accurately, its ideal reflection—can only continue if it is sustained by this twofold force. That is, it requires the close union of *Buddhi* and *Manas* in every rebirth or personal life. The slightest deviation from this harmony damages it; and when it is destroyed beyond any hope of recovery, the two forces separate at the moment of death. 

During a brief interval, the *personal form* (called interchangeably *Kama rupa* <note>the "desire body" or astral shell left behind after death</note> and *Mayavi rupa* <note>the "illusion body" or a thought-form</note>) remains. The spiritual flowering of this form attaches itself to the Ego and follows it into Devachan, giving the permanent individuality its personal character for the time being <note>original: *pro tem.*</note>, so to speak. Meanwhile, the rest of the form is carried off to remain in Kamaloka <note>the "place of desire"; a semi-material plane where the soul's desires are purged before it can move on</note> to be gradually annihilated. 

For it is after the death of the utterly depraved—those who are unspiritual and wicked beyond redemption—that the critical and supreme moment arrives. During life, the INNER SELF (*Manas*) makes an ultimate and desperate effort to unite some part of the personality with itself and the high, glimmering ray of the divine *Buddhi*. If this effort is thwarted—if this ray is increasingly shut out by the ever-thickening crust of the physical brain—then the Spiritual EGO (or *Manas*), once freed from the body, remains severed entirely from the ethereal relic of the personality. The reincarnationists of the Allan Kardec School <note>a French movement founded by Allan Kardec that taught immediate rebirth, often within the same family</note> believe in an arbitrary and immediate reincarnation. ## XI.

# ON THE MYSTERIES OF REINCARNATION.

---

### *PERIODICAL REBIRTHS.*

**ENQUIRER.** You mean, then, that we have all lived on earth before, in many past incarnations, and shall go on living in this way?

**THEOSOPHIST.** I do. The life-cycle—or rather, the cycle of conscious life—begins with the separation of the mortal "animal-man" into sexes <note>Theosophical doctrine suggests that early humanity was originally androgynous before separating into male and female.</note>. It will end with the close of the last generation of humans, in the seventh round and seventh race of mankind <note>In Theosophical cosmology, humanity evolves through seven "Root Races" during seven "Rounds" or cycles of planetary existence.</note>. Considering we are only in the fourth round and fifth race, the duration of this cycle is more easily imagined than expressed.

**ENQUIRER.** And we keep on incarnating in new personalities all the time?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Absolutely; because this life-cycle, or period of incarnation, may be best compared to an individual human life. Just as each human life is composed of days of activity separated by nights of sleep or inaction, so, in the incarnation-cycle, an active life is followed by a <term>Devachanic rest</term> <note>original: "Devachanic rest." Devachan is a Tibetan-derived term for a blissful, dream-like state of consciousness between earthly lives.</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** And it is this succession of births that is generally defined as reincarnation?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Exactly. It is only through these births that the perpetual...

<summary>This page introduces the Theosophical concept of reincarnation, explaining it as a cyclical process of activity and rest that spans vast evolutionary periods known as "rounds" and "races."</summary>
<keywords>reincarnation, life-cycle, Root Races, Rounds, Devachan, human evolution, gender separation, rebirth</keywords>

<page-num>198</page-num>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

progress of the countless millions of Egos toward final perfection and final rest (a rest lasting as long as the period of activity) can be achieved.

**ENQUIRER.** And what is it that regulates the duration or specific qualities of these incarnations?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Karma, the universal law of retributive justice <note>Retributive justice refers to the principle that every action has an inevitable and proportional consequence.</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** Is it an intelligent law?

**THEOSOPHIST.** To a materialist <note>A materialist believes that only physical matter exists and that spiritual or mental phenomena are merely the results of material interactions.</note>—who calls the law of cycles <note>original: "periodicity"</note> (which regulates the arrangement of the various celestial bodies and all other laws of nature) "blind forces" and "mechanical laws"—Karma would surely seem to be nothing more than a law of chance. For us, no adjective or description can truly define it because it is impersonal and not an entity; it is a universal, active law. If you ask me whether there is a guiding intelligence behind it, I must answer that I do not know. However, if you ask me to define its effects and our beliefs about them, I can say that the experience of thousands of ages has shown us that its effects are absolute and unerring fairness, wisdom, and intelligence. In its results, Karma is an unfailing corrector of human injustice and of all the failures of nature. It is a stern righter of wrongs—a law of retribution that rewards and punishes with complete impartiality. In the strictest sense, it is "no respecter of persons" <note>This phrase, originally from the New Testament (Acts 10:34), indicates that the law does not show favoritism or bias based on a person’s status or identity.</note>. On the other hand, it can neither be appeased <note>original: "propitiated"</note> nor turned aside by prayer. This belief is common to both Hindus and Buddhists, who both believe in Karma.

**ENQUIRER.** Christian dogmas <note>Established doctrines or principles laid down by a religious authority.</note> contradict both of those religions on this point, and I doubt whether any Christian will accept this teaching.

**THEOSOPHIST.** No; and Inman <note>Thomas Inman (1820–1876) was an English scholar and physician known for his controversial studies of ancient religious symbolism and mythology.</note> gave the reason for it many years ago.

<summary>This page defines Karma as the impersonal, universal law of retributive justice that governs the qualities and duration of human incarnations through unerring fairness. The Theosophist explains that unlike Christian doctrine, Karma cannot be altered by prayer or favoritism.</summary>

> “Buddhists believe that every act, word, or thought has its consequence, which will appear sooner or later in the present or in the future state. Evil acts will produce evil consequences, and good acts will produce good consequences: prosperity in this world, or birth in heaven (<term>Devachan</term>) . . . in the future state.”
<note>Devachan: In Theosophy, this refers to a temporary state of blissful consciousness between physical lives, similar to a personal paradise or heaven.</note>

**ENQUIRER.** Christians believe the same thing, don’t they?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Oh, no; they believe in the pardon and the forgiveness of all sins. They are promised that if they only believe in the blood of Christ (an innocent victim!), in the blood offered by Him for the atonement <note>original: "expiation"</note> of the sins of all mankind, it will make up for every mortal sin. But we believe neither in <term>vicarious atonement</term> nor in the possibility of the forgiveness of even the smallest sin by any god—not even by a “personal Absolute” or “Infinite,” if such a thing could even exist. What we believe in is strict and impartial justice. Our idea of the unknown Universal Deity, represented by <term>Karma</term>, is that
<note>vicarious atonement: The Christian doctrine that Jesus Christ died as a substitute for sinners, taking their punishment upon himself so they could be forgiven.</note>
<note>Karma: The universal law of cause and effect; the principle that every action has an inevitable and proportional consequence.</note>

...it is a Power which cannot fail, and can, therefore, have neither wrath nor mercy, only absolute Equity <note>Equity here refers to perfect, impartial justice or fairness.</note>, which leaves every cause, great or small, to work out its inevitable effects. The saying of Jesus: "With whatever measure you use, it will be measured to you again" (Matthew 7:2), neither by expression nor implication points to any hope of future mercy or salvation by proxy <note>Salvation by proxy refers to the doctrine of "vicarious atonement," or the belief that one can be saved from their sins through the sacrifice of someone else (like Jesus) rather than through their own actions.</note>. This is why, recognizing as we do in our philosophy the justice of this statement, we cannot recommend too strongly mercy, charity, and forgiveness of mutual offenses. *Do not resist evil, and return good for evil*, are Buddhist precepts, and were first preached in view of the implacability <note>Implacability: The quality of being unstoppable or unable to be changed or appeased.</note> of Karmic law. For a person to take the law into their own hands is, in any case, a sacrilegious presumption. Human Law may use restrictive but not punitive measures; but a man who, believing in Karma, still revenges himself and refuses to forgive every injury—thereby failing to return good for evil—is a criminal and only hurts himself. 

As Karma is sure to punish the man who wronged him, the person who seeks to inflict an additional punishment on his enemy, and who adds his own small contribution <note>original: "mite"</note> instead of leaving that punishment to the great Law, only creates a cause for the future reward of his own enemy and a future punishment for himself. The unfailing Regulator <note>In this context, the "Regulator" is Karma, the impersonal force that maintains moral balance in the universe.</note> affects the quality of each incarnation based on the one that preceded it; and the sum of the merit or demerit in previous lives determines it.

**ENQUIRER.** Are we then to infer a person's past from their present circumstances?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Only so far as to believe that his present life is what it justly should be, to atone for the sins of the past life. Of course—except for seers and great <term>adepts</term> <note>Adepts: Highly advanced individuals who have attained mastery over their physical and spiritual natures and possess esoteric knowledge.</note>—we cannot...

### WHAT IS KARMA?

ENQ. But what is Karma?

THEO. As I have said, we consider it to be the *Ultimate Law* of the Universe, the source, origin, and fountain of all other laws which exist throughout Nature. Karma is the unerring law which adjusts effect to cause on the physical, mental, and spiritual planes of being. As no cause remains without its due effect—from the greatest to the least, from a cosmic disturbance down to the movement of your hand—and as like produces like, *Karma* is that unseen and unknown law which adjusts each effect to its cause wisely, intelligently, and equitably, tracing the latter back to the person who produced it. Though the law itself is unknowable, its action can be perceived.

ENQ. Then it is the “Absolute” or the “Unknowable” again, and is not of much value as an explanation for the problems of life?

THEO. On the contrary. For although we do not know what Karma is in itself <note>original: "*per se*"</note> and in its essence, we do know how it works, and we can define and describe its mode of action with accuracy. We only do not know its ultimate *Cause*, just as modern philosophy universally admits that the ultimate *Cause* of anything is “unknowable.”

<summary>This page defines Karma as the ultimate, unerring law of the universe that perfectly balances cause and effect across physical and spiritual planes. While its fundamental essence is unknowable, its governing role in aligning consequences with actions is observable and central to understanding life's problems.</summary>

<keywords>Karma, Ultimate Law, cause and effect, Theosophy, justice, the Unknowable, cosmic law, reincarnation, philosophy</keywords>

ENQ. And what does <term>Theosophy</term> <note>A religious and philosophical system based on ancient wisdom, seeking to understand the nature of the universe and humanity’s place within it.</note> have to say regarding the solution to the practical needs of humanity? What explanation does it offer for the terrible suffering and extreme poverty <note>original: "dire necessity"</note> prevalent among the so-called "lower classes"?

THEO. To be direct, according to our teaching, all these great social evils—the distinction of classes in society, the inequality of the sexes in the affairs of life, and the unequal distribution of capital and labor—all are due to what we briefly but accurately call <term>KARMA</term>.

ENQ. But surely all these evils, which seem to fall upon the masses somewhat randomly <note>original: "indiscriminately"</note>, are not actually deserved and INDIVIDUAL <term>Karma</term>?

THEO. No, they cannot be so strictly defined. We cannot say that every specific environment or the particular conditions of life in which a person finds themselves are nothing more than the punishing <note>original: "retributive"</note> <term>Karma</term> which that individual generated in a previous life. 

We must not lose sight of the fact that every part <note>original: "atom"</note> is subject to the general law governing the whole body to which it belongs. Here we encounter the broader reach of the <term>Karmic law</term>. Do you not see that the total sum of individual <term>Karma</term> becomes the <term>Karma</term> of the nation to which those individuals belong? Furthermore, the sum total of <term>National Karma</term> constitutes the <term>Karma</term> of the entire World. The evils you speak of are not unique to the individual or even to one nation; they are more or less universal. It is through this broad principle of <term>human interdependence</term> that the <term>law of Karma</term> finds its rightful and fair expression <note>original: "equable issue"</note>.

ENQ. Do I understand, then, that the <term>law of Karma</term> is not necessarily just an individual law?

THEO. That is just what I mean. It is impossible that Karma could readjust the balance of power in the world’s life and progress unless it had a broad and general line of action. It is held as a truth among Theosophists that the interdependence of Humanity is the cause of what is called <term>Distributive Karma</term> <note>Also known as collective or group Karma; the idea that individuals share in the moral consequences of the actions of their nation, race, or community.</note>, and it is this law which provides the solution to the great question of collective suffering and its relief. It is an occult law, moreover, that no man can rise above his individual failings without lifting—even if only a little—the whole body of which he is an essential <note>original: "integral"</note> part. In the same way, no one can sin, nor suffer the effects of sin, alone. In reality, there is no such thing as “<term>Separateness</term>” <note>The "heresy of separateness" is the mistaken belief that an individual is an independent entity, disconnected from the rest of humanity and the universe.</note>; and the nearest approach to that selfish state which the laws of life permit is in one’s intent or motive.

ENQ. And are there no means by which this distributive or national Karma might be concentrated <note>original: "concentred"</note> or collected, so to speak, and brought to its natural and legitimate fulfillment without all this prolonged <note>original: "protracted"</note> suffering?

THEO. As a general rule, and within certain limits which define the age to which we belong, the law of Karma cannot be hastened or slowed down in its fulfillment. But of this I am certain: the point of possibility in either of these directions has never yet been reached. Listen to the following account <note>original: "recital"</note> of one phase of national suffering, and then ask yourself whether—admitting the working power of individual, relative, and distributive Karma—these evils are not capable of extensive modification and general relief. What I am about to read to you is from the pen of a "National Savior" <note>This likely refers to a Mahatma or "Master of Wisdom" whom Blavatsky regarded as a spiritual guide for humanity.</note>, one who, having overcome the Self and being free to choose his path, has elected to serve Humanity in...

If by any sacrifice one could win the power to save these people, the cost would not be worth counting; but, you see, **THEY** must be changed—and how can that be achieved? In the condition they are in now, they would not profit from any environment in which they might be placed; and yet, in their present surroundings, they must continue to rot. It breaks my heart, this endless, hopeless misery, and the brutish degradation that is at once its result and its root. It is like the <term>banyan tree</term> <note>An Indian tree whose branches grow down into the soil to become new trunks. Here, it symbolizes a cycle of misery that continuously replants itself.</note>; every branch roots itself and sends out new shoots. What a difference between these feelings and the peaceful scene at Hampstead! and yet we...

it always *does* act so as to restore harmony and preserve the balance of equilibrium, by which the universe exists.

<margin>ENQUIRER.</margin> Give me an illustration.

<margin>THEOSOPHIST.</margin> Later on, I will give you a full illustration. For now, think of a pond. A stone falls into the water and creates disturbing waves. These waves oscillate back and forth until at last—due to the operation of what physicists call the <term>law of the dissipation of energy</term> <note>original: "dissipation of energy"; the principle that energy spreads out and eventually leads to a state of rest or equilibrium.</note>—they are brought to rest, and the water returns to its condition of calm tranquility. Similarly, *all action*, on every plane of existence, produces a disturbance in the balanced harmony of the universe. The vibrations produced will continue to roll back and forth (if the area is limited) until equilibrium is restored. But since each such disturbance starts from a specific point, it is clear that equilibrium and harmony can only be restored when all the forces that were set in motion return to that same point. Here, you have proof that the consequences of a person's deeds, thoughts, and so on, must all react upon *themselves* with the same force with which they were originally set in motion.

<margin>ENQUIRER.</margin> But I see nothing of a moral character in this law. It looks to me like the simple physical law that action and reaction are equal and opposite.

<margin>THEOSOPHIST.</margin> I am not surprised to hear you say that. We Theosophists, however, say that "Good" and—

"Harmony" is the natural state, while "Evil" and "Disharmony" are synonymous. Furthermore, we maintain that all pain and suffering result from a lack of harmony, and that the single terrible cause of disturbed harmony is *selfishness* in some form or another. Therefore, Karma returns the actual consequences of a person's own actions to them, without regard to their moral character. <meta>This page continues the explanation of the Theosophical view on Karma, specifically addressing why the concept of "vicarious atonement" is rejected in favor of individual responsibility and the restoration of universal harmony.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>

concepts of Theosophy. In this faith, there is no such thing as pardon <gloss>original: "parac<gloss>e</gloss>"; likely a misspelling of 'pardon'</gloss> or the "blotting out of evil or wickedness already done," other than the adequate punishment for the wrongdoer and the restoration of the harmony in the universe that had been disturbed by their wrongful act. The evil has been their own, and while others must suffer its consequences, atonement can be made by nobody but themselves.

> "The condition contemplated . . . . in which a person has been 'awakened and converted to a certain extent from evil or wickedness,' is that in which a person has realized that their deeds are evil and deserving of punishment. In that realization, a sense of personal responsibility is inevitable, and the sense of that awful responsibility must be in exact proportion to the extent of their awakening or 'conversion.' While this feeling is strong within them, that is when they are urged to accept the doctrine of **vicarious atonement** <term>original: "vicarious atonement"; the theological doctrine that the atonement for one's sins is achieved through the suffering of another (specifically, Jesus Christ).</term>.

> "They are told that they must also repent, but nothing is easier than that. It is an understandable weakness of human nature that we are quite prone to regret the evil we have done when our attention is called to it, and we have either suffered from it ourselves or finished enjoying its fruits. Possibly, a close analysis of the feeling would show us that what we regret is actually the necessity that seemed to require the evil as a means of achieving our selfish ends, rather than the evil itself."

> "Attractive as this prospect of casting our burden of sins 'at the foot of the cross' may be to the ordinary mind, it does not appeal to the student of **Theosophy**. They do not understand why the sinner, by gaining knowledge of their evil, can thereby deserve any pardon or the erasing of their past wickedness; or why repentance and future right living entitle them to a suspension in their favor of the universal law of relation between cause and effect. The results of their evil deeds continue to exist; the suffering caused to others by their wickedness is not erased. The Theosophical student includes the result of wickedness upon the innocent in their calculations. They consider not only the guilty person but also their victims.

> "Evil is a violation of the laws of harmony governing the universe, and the penalty for it must fall upon the violator of that law themselves."

<summary>This page explains the Theosophical rejection of "vicarious atonement," arguing instead for personal responsibility and the idea that individuals must personally atone for the disharmony their actions cause.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Karma, atonement, vicarious atonement, personal responsibility, cause and effect, laws of harmony, repentance</keywords>
