<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Theosophical view on Karma, specifically addressing why the concept of "vicarious atonement" is rejected in favor of individual responsibility and the restoration of universal harmony.</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

Christ uttered the warning, "Sin no more, lest a worse thing come upon you," and St. Paul said, "Whatever a man sows, that shall he also reap." That, by the way, is a fine metaphorical translation of a sentence from the <term>Puranas</term> <note>original: "Puranas"; ancient Sanskrit texts containing Hindu philosophy, mythology, and tradition</note> which predates him by many centuries—that "every man reaps the consequences of his own acts."

"This is the principle of the law of <term>Karma</term> <note>original: "Karma"; the spiritual law of cause and effect</note> which is taught by <term>Theosophy</term> <note>original: "Theosophy"; a religious and philosophical system based on mystical insight into the nature of the divine</note>. A. P. Sinnett, in his book *Esoteric Buddhism*, defined <term>Karma</term> as 'the law of ethical causation.' 'The law of retribution,' as Madame <term>Blavatsky</term> <note>Helena Petrovna Blavatsky, a co-founder of the Theosophical Society</note> translates its meaning, is better. It is the power which:

->Just though mysterious, leads us on unerringly<-
->Through ways unmarked from guilt to punishment.<-

"But it is more. It rewards merit as unerringly and amply as it punishes wrongdoing. It is the outcome of every act—of thought, word, and deed—and by it, people shape themselves, their lives, and their circumstances. Eastern philosophy rejects the idea of a newly created soul for every baby born. It believes in a limited number of <term>monads</term> <note>original: "monads"; in this context, the eternal, spiritual spark or individual soul that survives death</note>, evolving and growing more and more perfect through their assimilation of many successive personalities. Those personalities are the product of <term>Karma</term>, and it is by <term>Karma</term> and reincarnation that the human monad in time returns to its source—absolute deity."

E. D. Walker, in his book *Re-incarnation*, offers the following explanation:

> "Briefly, the doctrine of Karma is that we have made ourselves what we are by former actions and are building our future eternity by present actions. There is no destiny but what we ourselves determine. There is no salvation or condemnation except what we ourselves bring about. . . . Because it offers no shelter for guilty actions and requires a true manliness, it is less welcome to weak natures than the easy religious doctrines of vicarious atonement, intercession, forgiveness, and deathbed conversions. . . . In the domain of eternal justice, the offense and the punishment are inseparably connected as the same event, because there is no real distinction between the action and its outcome. . . . It is Karma, or our old acts, that draws us back into..."

<summary>This page explains the concept of Karma as the "law of ethical causation" or "retribution," emphasizing that individuals are entirely responsible for their own spiritual progress through successive reincarnations. It contrasts this law of personal responsibility with the Christian doctrine of vicarious atonement, arguing that Karma is a more rigorous and just system.</summary>

<keywords>Karma, Theosophy, Reincarnation, Monads, Puranas, Blavatsky, A.P. Sinnett, E.D. Walker, Ethical Causation, Retribution</keywords>

earthly life. The spirit’s abode changes according to its <term>Karma</term> <note>original: "Karma"; the law of moral cause and effect</note>, and this Karma forbids any long continuance in one condition, because it is always changing. So long as action is governed by material and selfish motives, just so long must the effect of that action be manifested in physical rebirths. Only the perfectly selfless person can elude the gravitation of material life. Few have attained this, but it is the goal of mankind.”

And then the writer quotes from the *Secret Doctrine* <note>original: "Secret Doctrine"; H. P. Blavatsky’s foundational book on Theosophy, published in 1888</note>:

> “Those who believe in Karma have to believe in destiny, which, from birth to death, every person is weaving, thread by thread, around themselves, as a spider does its cobweb; and this destiny is guided either by the heavenly voice of the invisible prototype outside of us, or by our more intimate astral or inner man, who is but too often the evil genius of the embodied entity called man. Both these lead on the outward person, but one of them must prevail; and from the very beginning of the invisible struggle, the stern and implacable law of compensation steps in and takes its course, faithfully following the fluctuations. When the last strand is woven, and a person is seemingly wrapped in the network of their own doing, then they find themselves completely under the empire of this self-made destiny. . . . An <term>Occultist</term> <note>original: "Occultist"; a student of the hidden or "occult" spiritual laws of the universe</note> or a philosopher will not speak of the goodness or cruelty of <term>Providence</term> <note>original: "Providence"; the concept of divine intervention or God's will</note>; but, identifying it with <term>Karma-Nemesis</term> <note>original: "Karma-Nemesis"; Nemesis was the Greek goddess of divine retribution; here, the term emphasizes Karma as the inescapable law of justice</note>, they will teach that, nevertheless, it guards the good and watches over them in this as in future lives; and that it punishes the evil-doer—yes, even to their seventh rebirth—so long, in short, as the effect of their having thrown into disturbance even the smallest atom in the infinite world of harmony has not been finally readjusted. For the only decree of Karma—an eternal and immutable decree—is absolute harmony in the world of matter as it is in the world of spirit. It is not, therefore, Karma that rewards or punishes, but it is we who reward or punish ourselves according to whether we work with, through, and along with nature, abiding by the laws on which that harmony depends, or—break them. Nor would the ways of Karma be inscrutable <note>original: "inscrutable"; impossible to understand or interpret</note> were people to work in union and harmony, instead of disunion and strife. For our ignorance

<summary>This page explains that human destiny is a self-woven web of Karma, where individuals are responsible for their own rewards and punishments based on their alignment with universal harmony. It introduces the concept of "Karma-Nemesis" as an impartial law that restores balance rather than a personal deity that judges.</summary>

...of those ways—which one portion of mankind calls the ways of <term>Providence</term> <note>original: "Providence"; the concept of divine intervention or God's plan for the world</note>, dark and intricate; while another sees in them the action of blind <term>fatalism</term> <note>original: "fatalism"; the belief that all events are predetermined and inevitable</note>; and a third sees simple chance, with neither gods nor devils to guide them—these views would surely disappear if we would only attribute all of these to their correct cause. . . . We stand bewildered before the mystery of our own making and the riddles of life that we refuse to solve, and then we accuse the great Sphinx <note>original: "Sphinx"; a mythical creature that killed those who could not solve its riddle. Here, it represents the unexplained mysteries of existence</note> of devouring us. But truly, there is not an accident in our lives, not a ruined day, or a misfortune, that could not be traced back to our own actions in this life or in another. . . . The law of <term>Karma</term> <note>original: "Karma"; the spiritual principle of cause and effect where a person's actions determine their future</note> is inextricably interwoven with that of <term>reincarnation</term>. . . . It is only this doctrine that can explain to us the mysterious problem of good and evil and reconcile humanity to the terrible and apparent injustice of life. Nothing but such certainty can quiet our outraged sense of justice. For, when someone who is unfamiliar with this noble doctrine looks around and observes the inequalities of birth and fortune, of intellect and abilities; when one sees honor paid to fools and immoral people, upon whom fortune has heaped her favors by the mere privilege of birth, while their neighbor, with all his intellect and noble virtues—deserving much more in every way—is perishing from poverty and a lack of sympathy—when one sees all this and must turn away, helpless to relieve the undeserved suffering, with one’s ears ringing and heart aching from the cries of pain around them—only that blessed knowledge of <term>Karma</term> prevents them from cursing life and humanity, as well as their supposed Creator. . . . This law, whether conscious or unconscious, predestines nothing and no one. It truly exists from and within eternity, for it is eternity itself; and as such, since no single act can be equal to eternity, it cannot be said to "act," for it is action itself. It is not the wave that drowns the man, but the personal action of the person who deliberately goes and places themselves under the impersonal action of the laws that govern the ocean’s motion. <term>Karma</term> creates nothing, nor does it design. It is humanity who plants and creates causes, and <term>Karmic law</term> <note>original: "Karmic law"; the specific application of the law of cause and effect to human morality and destiny</note> adjusts the effects. This adjustment is not an "act" but universal harmony, tending always to return to its original position, like a branch which, when bent down too forcibly, rebounds with corresponding [force]...

<summary>This page argues that the law of Karma, working alongside reincarnation, provides the only logical explanation for life's inequalities and suffering, framing it as an impersonal law of universal harmony rather than divine whim or blind chance.</summary>

vigor. If it happens to dislocate the arm that tried to bend it out of its natural position, should we say it is the branch that broke our arm, or that our own foolishness has brought us to grief? Karma has never sought to destroy intellectual and individual liberty, unlike the god invented by the <term>monotheists</term> <note>original: "Monotheists"; those who believe in a single, personal, and often judgmental creator deity</note>. It has not wrapped its decrees in darkness on purpose to confuse humanity, nor does it punish those who dare to examine its mysteries. On the contrary, whoever unveils its intricate paths through study and meditation, and sheds light on those dark ways—in the turns of which so many people perish because of their ignorance of the labyrinth of life—is working for the good of their fellow humans. Karma is an absolute and eternal law in the world of manifestation; and as there can only be one Absolute, just as there is only one Eternal, ever-present Cause, believers in Karma cannot be regarded as atheists or <term>materialists</term> <note>original: "materialists"; people who believe that only physical matter exists and deny the spiritual</note>, and even less as <term>fatalists</term> <note>original: "fatalists"; those who believe that all events are predetermined and that human effort is useless</note>. Another skilled Theosophical writer says (*The Purpose of Theosophy*, by Mrs. Patience Sinnett):

> “Every individual is making Karma—either good or bad—in every action and thought of their daily routine, and is at the same time working through in this life the Karma produced by the actions and desires of the last. When we see people afflicted with congenital illnesses, it may be safely assumed that these ailments are the inevitable results of causes started by themselves in a previous birth. It may be argued that, since these afflictions are hereditary, they can have nothing to do with a past <term>incarnation</term> <note>original: "incarnation"; the soul's birth into a new physical body</note>; but it must be remembered that the <term>Ego</term> <note>original: "Ego"; in Theosophy, the permanent, spiritual individuality that reincarnates, as opposed to the temporary physical personality</note>—the real man, the true individuality—does not have its spiritual origin in the parents through whom it is <term>re-embodied</term> <note>original: "re-embodied"; born again into a physical body</note>. Instead, it is drawn by the <term>affinities</term> <note>original: "affinities"; spiritual or psychological attractions developed through past behavior</note> which its previous way of life attracted around it. It is pulled into the current that carries it, when the time comes for rebirth, to the home best suited for the development of those tendencies. . . . This doctrine of Karma, when properly understood, is well-suited to guide and assist those who realize its truth toward a higher and better way of life. For it must not be forgotten that not only our actions, but our thoughts also, are most certainly...”

followed by a crowd of circumstances that will influence our own future for good or for evil, and, what is still more important, the future of many of our fellow human beings. If sins of omission (failing to do what is right) and commission (doing what is wrong) only affected the person who committed them, then the effect on the sinner’s <term>Karma</term> <note>original: "Karma"; the spiritual law of cause and effect where one's actions determine their future destiny</note> would be a matter of minor importance. This reality makes a strict sense of justice, morality, and unselfishness absolutely necessary for future happiness or progress. A crime once committed, or an evil thought sent out from the mind, is past recall—no amount of repentance can wipe out their results in the future. Repentance, if sincere, will stop a person from repeating errors; but it cannot save them or others from the effects of those already produced. Those consequences will most unerringly catch up with the individual, either in this life or in the next rebirth.”

> “The believers in a religion based upon such a doctrine are willing for it to be compared with a religion in which a person's destiny for eternity is determined by the accidents of a single, brief earthly existence. Neither are any others redeemed by Christ, or effectively called, justified, adopted, sanctified, and saved, but the elect only.”

And now I advise you to compare our Theosophical views on <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect; the total sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences</note>—the law of retribution—and say whether they are not both more philosophical and just than this cruel and idiotic dogma which turns "God" into a senseless fiend. ENQUIRER. Yes, I see what you mean in general; but I wish you could give some concrete example of the action of Karma?

THEOSOPHIST. That I cannot do. We can only feel sure, as I said before, that our present lives and circumstances are the direct results of our own deeds and thoughts in past lives. THEOSOPHIST. Certainly: "those who know" can do so by exercising powers which are latent in all human beings.

...perceive that, if things with us ought to have been different, they would have been different; that we are what we have made ourselves, and have only what we have earned for ourselves.

**ENQ.** I am afraid such a concept would only make us bitter.

**THEO.** I believe it is exactly the opposite. It is a lack of belief in the just law of <term>retribution</term> <note>original: "retribution"; punishment inflicted as vengeance for a wrong or criminal act; in this context, the natural consequence of one's actions</note> that is more likely to stir up aggressive feelings in a person. A child, just like an adult, resents a punishment—or even a scolding—that they believe to be undeserved far more than they do a harsher punishment if they feel it is earned. Belief in <term>Karma</term> <note>The spiritual principle of cause and effect where intent and actions of an individual influence their future</note> is the greatest reason for being reconciled to one’s lot in this life, and the most powerful motivation to work toward a better future <term>re-birth</term> <note>original: "re-birth"; the process of being born again in a new body</note>. Both of these would be destroyed if we supposed that our fate was the result of anything other than strict **Law**, or that destiny was in any hands other than our own.

**ENQ.** You have just claimed that this system of reincarnation under the law of Karma appeals to reason, justice, and our sense of morality. But if that is true, doesn't it require sacrificing the gentler qualities of sympathy and pity? Wouldn't that harden the finer instincts of human nature?

**THEO.** Only on the surface, not in reality. No one can receive more or less than they deserve without a corresponding injustice or favoritism being shown to others. A law that could be bypassed through compassion would cause more misery than it saved, and lead to more frustration and bitterness than gratitude. Remember also that we do not personally enforce the law, even if we do create the causes for its effects; the law enforces itself. Furthermore, the most abundant...

Instead, many incarnations are necessary after a person has formed a conscious purpose and begun the required training. 

There may be many men and women in the very midst of our Society who began this uphill work toward illumination several incarnations ago, and who yet—due to the personal illusions of their present life—are either ignorant of that fact or are on the road to losing every chance in this existence of progressing any further. ...too opinionated, too much in love with the deceptive attractions <note>original: "allurements"</note> of mundane <note>original: "mundane"; relating to the world or worldly affairs as opposed to spiritual ones</note> life and the world’s fleeting <note>original: "ephemeral"</note> pleasures, to give them up; and so they lose their chance in their present birth. But, for ordinary men, for the practical duties of daily life, such a far-off result is inappropriate as an aim and quite ineffective as a motive.

**ENQ.** What, then, may be their objective or distinct purpose in joining the Theosophical Society?

**THEO.** Many are interested in our doctrines and feel instinctively that they are truer than those of any dogmatic religion. Others have formed a fixed resolve to attain the highest ideal of man’s duty.

### THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN FAITH AND KNOWLEDGE; OR, BLIND AND REASONED FAITH.

**ENQ.** You say that they accept and believe in the doctrines of <term>Theosophy</term> <note>A system of esoteric philosophy seeking direct knowledge of the divine and the underlying unity of the universe</note>. But, as they do not belong to those <term>Adepts</term> <note>Highly evolved individuals who have achieved spiritual mastery and gained direct knowledge of the hidden laws of nature</note> you have just mentioned, then they must accept your teachings on *blind faith*. In what does this differ from that of conventional religions?

**THEO.** Just as it differs on almost all the other points, so it differs on this one. What you call “faith”—and that which is blind faith, in reality, regarding the dogmas of the Christian religions—becomes with us “*knowledge*.” It is the logical sequence of things we know about *facts in nature*. Your doctrines are based upon interpretation, and therefore upon the second-hand testimony of <term>Seers</term> <note>original: "Seers"; individuals credited with the power to see the future or perceive spiritual truths</note>; ours are based upon the invariable and unvarying...

the testimony of seers <note>Individuals who possess spiritual insight or the ability to perceive realities invisible to the physical eyes</note>. For example, standard Christian theology maintains that a human being is a creature of God made of three component parts—body, soul, and spirit. It holds that all three are essential to his wholeness <note>original: "integrity"</note>, and that all—whether in the dense <note>original: "gross"</note> form of physical earthly existence or the refined <note>original: "etherealized"</note> form of experience after the resurrection—are needed to define him forever. In this view, each person has a permanent existence separate from other people and from the Divine. 

Theosophy, on the other hand, holds that because a human being is an emanation <note>A flowing out or proceeding from a source; in this context, a spiritual "ray" from the divine source</note> from the Unknown, yet ever-present and infinite Divine Essence, his body and everything else is impermanent and, therefore, an illusion. Spirit alone within him is the one enduring substance, and even that spirit loses its separate individuality at the moment of its complete reunion with the Universal Spirit.

**ENQ.** If we lose even our individuality, then it simply becomes annihilation.

**THEO.** I say it does not, since I am speaking of *separate* individuality, not universal individuality. The latter becomes like a part transformed into the whole; the dewdrop is not evaporated, but instead becomes the sea. Is a physical man *annihilated* when he grows from a fetus into an old man? What kind of demonic <note>original: "Satanic"</note> pride must be ours if we place our infinitesimally small consciousness and individuality higher than the universal and infinite consciousness!

**ENQ.** Does it follow, then, that in reality <note>original: "de facto"</note> there is no "man," but everything is Spirit?

**THEO.** You are mistaken. It follows instead that the union of Spirit with matter is only temporary. Or, to put it more clearly—since Spirit and matter are one, being the two opposite poles of the universal manifested substance—it means that Spirit loses its right to

Our "faith" has all the logical force of the mathematical truth that 2 plus 2 will produce 4. Your faith is like the logic of some emotional women, of whom Turgenev <note>original: "Tourgenyeff"; referring to the Russian novelist Ivan Turgenev</note> said that for them 2 plus 2 were generally 5, with a tallow candle thrown into the bargain. Yours is a faith, moreover, which clashes not only with every conceivable view of justice and logic, but which, if analyzed, leads man to his moral ruin <note>original: "perdition"</note>, stops the progress of mankind, and effectively makes "might" into "right"—transforming every other person into a Cain toward his brother Abel.

**ENQ.** What do you refer to?

# THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

### DOES GOD HAVE THE RIGHT TO FORGIVE?

**THEO.** I am referring to the Doctrine of Atonement—that dangerous dogma in which you believe. It teaches that no matter how enormous our crimes against the laws of God and man, we only have to believe in the self-sacrifice of Jesus for the salvation of humanity, and his blood will wash away every stain. I have been preaching against this for twenty years, and I can now draw your attention to a paragraph from *Isis Unveiled* <note>The author's first major work, published in 1877, which critiqued both modern science and dogmatic religion</note>, written in 1875. This is what Christianity teaches, and what we oppose:
> “God’s mercy is boundless and unfathomable. It is impossible to imagine a human sin so damnable that the price paid in advance for the sinner’s redemption would not wipe it out, even if the sin were a thousand times worse. Furthermore, it is never too late to repent. Even if the offender waits until the last minute of the last hour of the last day of his mortal life before his pale <note>original: "blanched"</note> lips utter the confession of faith, he may go to Paradise; the dying thief <note>A reference to the "Penitent Thief" crucified alongside Jesus in the Gospel of Luke</note> did it, and so may all others just as wicked <note>original: "vile"</note>. These are the claims of the Church and the Clergy—claims forced upon your countrymen by England’s favorite preachers, right in the ‘light of the 19th century <note>original: "XIXth century"</note>’ ”

—this most contradictory <note>original: "paradoxical"</note> age of all. Now, where does this lead?

**ENQ.** Doesn't it make the Christian happier than the Buddhist or Brahmin <note>term: A member of the highest, priestly caste in Hindu society</note>?

**THEO.** No—not the educated man, at any rate, since the majority of these have long since practically lost all belief in this cruel...

> “If we step outside the little circle of creed <note>A formal statement of religious belief</note> and consider the universe as a whole, balanced by the exquisite adjustment of its parts, how all sound logic—how even the faintest glimmering sense of Justice—revolts against this **Vicarious Atonement** <term>The Christian doctrine that Jesus Christ suffered and died as a substitute for sinners, satisfying divine justice on their behalf</term>! If the criminal sinned only against himself and wronged no one but himself; if by sincere repentance he could cause the erasure <note>original: "obliteration"</note> of past events, not only from the memory of man but also from that imperishable record <note>A reference to the 'Akashic Records' or the eternal memory of nature, where every action is said to be recorded</note> which no deity—not even the highest of the high <note>original: "Supremes of the Supreme"</note>—can cause to disappear, then this dogma might not be so impossible to understand. Can the results of a crime be wiped out even if the crime itself should be pardoned? The effects of a cause are never limited to the boundaries of the cause, nor can the results of a crime be confined to the offender and his victim. Every good as well as evil action has its effects, as clearly as a stone flung into calm water. The comparison is common <note>original: "trite"</note>, but it is the best ever conceived, so let us use it. The eddying circles are greater and swifter depending on whether the disturbing object is larger or smaller, but even the smallest pebble—no, the tiniest speck—makes its ripples. And this disturbance is not only visible on the surface. Below, unseen, in every direction—outward and downward—drop pushes drop until the sides and bottom are touched by the force. Furthermore, the air above the water is disturbed, and this disturbance passes, as the physicists tell us, from layer to layer <note>original: "stratum to stratum"</note> out into space forever and ever; an impulse has been given to matter, and that is never lost and can never be recalled! . . .

> “So it is with crime, and so with its opposite. The action may be instantaneous, but the effects are eternal. When, after the stone is once flung into

**THEOSOPHIST.** That is the final goal toward which everything in nature moves.

**ENQUIRER.** Do some of you not regard this association, or the "fall of spirit into matter," as evil, and rebirth as a source of sorrow?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Some do, and therefore they strive to shorten their period of testing <note>original: "probation"</note> on earth. However, it is not entirely evil, since it provides the experience through which we climb toward knowledge and wisdom. I mean the experience that teaches us that the needs of our spiritual nature can only be satisfied by spiritual happiness. As long as we are in the body, we are subject to pain, suffering, and all the disappointing events that occur during life. Therefore, to ease <note>original: "palliate"</note> this, we eventually acquire the knowledge that alone can offer us relief and the hope of a better future.

### XII.

## WHAT IS PRACTICAL THEOSOPHY?

---

->*DUTY.*<-

**ENQUIRER.** Why, then, is there a need for rebirths, since they all alike fail to achieve a permanent peace?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Because the final goal cannot be reached in any way except through life experiences, and because the majority of these consist of pain and suffering. It is only through suffering that we can learn. Joys and pleasures teach us nothing; they are fleeting <note>original: "evanescent"</note> and, in the long run, can only bring a sense of being overfilled or world-weary <note>original: "satiety"</note>. Moreover, our constant failure to find any permanent satisfaction in life that would meet the needs of our higher nature shows us plainly that those needs can be met only on their own level—namely, the spiritual.

**ENQUIRER.** Is the natural result of this a desire to end one's life by one means or another?

**THEOSOPHIST.** If you mean "suicide" by that desire, then I say most decidedly not. Such a result can never be a "natural" one; it is always due to a morbid brain disease or to very definite and strong materialistic views. It is the worst of crimes and

<summary>This page explains that rebirth and suffering are essential for spiritual growth because worldly pleasures are temporary and cannot satisfy the soul's higher needs. It also clarifies that understanding the nature of suffering should lead to spiritual awareness rather than suicide, which is characterized as a result of disease or materialism.</summary>
<keywords>rebirth, reincarnation, suffering, spiritual evolution, satiety, materialism, suicide, Practical Theosophy</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on suicide, framing it as an escape from duty, before transitioning into a definition of "Duty" within the framework of Theosophy. It argues that moral duties are universal rather than exclusively Christian.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

terrible <note>original: "dire"</note> in its results. But if by desire, you mean simply an aspiration to reach spiritual existence, rather than a wish to leave the earth, then I would call it a very natural desire indeed. Otherwise, voluntary death <note>meaning suicide</note> would be an abandonment of our current post and of the duties required of us. It would also be an attempt to avoid <term>Karmic</term> responsibilities, and would thus lead to the creation of new Karma <note>Karma: the spiritual law of cause and effect; the idea that our actions in this life determine our future circumstances.</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** But if actions on the material plane <term>the physical world</term> are unsatisfying, why should duties—which are just such actions—be mandatory?

**THEOSOPHIST.** First of all, because our philosophy teaches us that the goal of doing our duty to all people—and to ourselves last—is not to achieve personal happiness, but to ensure the happiness of others. It is the fulfillment of what is right for the sake of right, not for what it might bring us. Happiness, or rather contentment, may indeed follow the performance of duty, but it is not and must not be the reason for doing it.

**ENQUIRER.** What exactly do you mean by "duty" in Theosophy? Surely it cannot be the Christian duties preached by Jesus and his Apostles, since you recognize neither as divine authorities?

**THEOSOPHIST.** You are mistaken once again. What you call "Christian duties" were taught <note>original: "inculcated"</note> by every great moral and religious reformer ages before the Christian era. In ancient times, everything that was great, generous, and heroic was not just talked about and preached from pulpits as it is today; it was acted upon, sometimes by entire nations. The history of the Buddhist reform is full of the most noble and most heroically unselfish acts. "Be all of one mind, having compassion for one another; love as brothers, be compassionate <note>original: "pitiful," which in this context means "full of pity" or "merciful"</note>, be courteous; not ren—" <note>The text likely continues "not rendering evil for evil," quoting 1 Peter 3:8-9.</note>

<summary>The author explains that suicide is an irresponsible escape from "Karmic" duties and defines duty as acting for the benefit of others rather than for personal happiness. The text argues that the moral values often labeled as "Christian" are actually universal truths taught by reformers like the Buddha long before Christianity.</summary>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of ethical duties, arguing that many concepts considered "Christian" were actually practiced by Buddhists and other "Pagan" groups centuries earlier. It then transitions into a specific definition of "Duty" within Theosophy.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

...returning evil for evil, or insult for insult; but on the contrary, blessing" <note>original: "rendering evil for evil, or railing for railing; but contrariwise, blessing" — a reference to 1 Peter 3:9</note> was practically carried out by the followers of Buddha several centuries before the apostle Peter. The ethics of Christianity are grand, no doubt; but they are undeniably not new, and they originated as "Pagan" duties.

**ENQUIRER.** And how would you define these duties, or "duty" in general, as you understand the term?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Duty is that which *is due* to Humanity: to our fellow humans, neighbors, family, and especially that which we owe to all those who are poorer and more helpless than we are ourselves. This is a debt which, if left unpaid during life, leaves us spiritually insolvent and moral bankrupts in our next incarnation. Theosophy is the very essence <note>original: "quintessence"</note> of duty.

**ENQUIRER.** So is Christianity, when it is rightly understood and carried out.

**THEOSOPHIST.** No doubt it is; but if it were not merely a *lip-religion* <note>A religion of words and superficial profession rather than actual practice.</note> in practice, Theosophy would have little to do among Christians. Unfortunately, it is often only such *lip-ethics*. Those who practice their duty toward all, and for the sake of duty itself, are few; and fewer still are those who perform 그that duty while remaining content with the satisfaction of their own secret consciousness. It is—

> ". . . . . . the public voice
> Of praise that honors virtue and rewards it,"

which is always foremost in the minds of "world-renowned" philanthropists. Modern ethics are beautiful to read about and hear discussed; but what are words unless they are converted into actions? Finally, if you ask me how we understand Theosophical duty practically and in view of <term>Karma</term>, I may answer you that our duty is to drink without a murmur to the last...

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

...drop whatever contents the cup of life may have in store for us, in order to pluck the roses of life only for the fragrance they may shed on *others*, and to be ourselves content with only the thorns, if that fragrance cannot be enjoyed without depriving someone else of it.

**ENQUIRER.** All this is very vague. What do you do more than Christians do?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It is not what we members of the Theosophical Society do—though some of us try our best—but how much farther Theosophy leads toward good than modern Christianity does. I say—*action*, practiced action, instead of mere intention and talk. A man may be whatever he likes—the most worldly, selfish, and hard-hearted of men, even a deep-dyed rascal—and it will not prevent him from calling himself a Christian, or others from seeing him as one. But no Theosophist has the right to that name unless he is thoroughly convinced of the truth of Carlyle’s <note>Thomas Carlyle (1795–1881), a Scottish philosopher and author who emphasized the importance of work and action.</note> truism: “The end of man is an action and not a thought, though it were the noblest”—and unless he sets and models his daily life upon this truth. To profess a truth is not the same as to live it; and the more beautiful and grand a truth sounds, and the more loudly virtue or duty is talked about instead of being acted upon, the more it will always remind one of the "Dead Sea fruit" <note>A metaphor for something that appears tempting or beautiful but is actually deceptive and disappointing; based on a legendary fruit that turned to ashes in the mouth.</note>. *Cant* <term>Hypocritical or sanctimonious talk, typically regarding moral or religious matters.</term> is the most loathsome of all vices; and *cant* is the most prominent feature of the greatest Protestant country of this century—England.

**ENQUIRER.** What do you consider to be due to humanity at large?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Full recognition of equal rights and privileges for all, without distinction of race, color, social position, or birth.

<header>THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.</header>

**ENQUIRER.** When would you consider such a "due" <note>In this context, "due" refers to the moral and social obligations or rights owed to others.</note> as not being given?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Whenever there is the slightest invasion of another’s right—whether that other is a person or a nation. It happens whenever there is any failure to show someone the same justice, kindness, consideration, or mercy which we desire for ourselves. The entire current system of politics is built on the disregard of such rights and the aggressive assertion of national selfishness. The French have a saying, “Like master, like man”; they should add, “Like national policy, like citizen.”

**ENQUIRER.** Do you take any part in politics?

**THEOSOPHIST.** As a Society, we carefully avoid them for the reasons given below. Seeking to achieve political reforms before we have reformed human nature is like putting new wine into old bottles <note>A biblical metaphor (Matthew 9:17) suggesting that new ideas or systems cannot be successfully contained within old, rigid, or corrupt frameworks.</note>. If we make people feel and recognize in their innermost hearts what their true duty to all people is, then every old abuse of power and every unjust <note>original: "iniquitous"</note> law in national policy—based on human, social, or political selfishness—will disappear on its own. A gardener is foolish if he tries to weed his flowerbed of poisonous plants by cutting them off at the surface of the soil instead of pulling them out by the roots. No lasting political reform can ever be achieved with the same selfish men at the head of affairs as in the past.

### *THE RELATIONS OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY TO POLITICAL REFORMS.*

**ENQUIRER.** The Theosophical Society is not, then, a political organization?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Certainly not. It is international in the highest sense, in that its members include men and women of all races and creeds...

<summary>This page explains that true justice requires treating others and other nations as we wish to be treated, and argues that the Theosophical Society avoids politics because lasting social change only comes from reforming human nature rather than changing laws.</summary>

and ways of thinking, who work together for a single goal: the improvement of humanity. However, as a society, it takes absolutely no part in any national or party politics.

**ENQUIRER.** Why is this?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Simply for the reasons I have mentioned. Furthermore, political action must necessarily change based on the circumstances of the time and the unique characteristics <note>original: "idiosyncracies"</note> of individuals. While the members of the T.S. (Theosophical Society) agree on the principles of Theosophy—otherwise they would not belong to the society at all—it does not follow that they agree on every other subject. As a society, they can only act together on matters they all share—that is, Theosophy itself. As individuals, each person is left perfectly free to follow their own particular line of political thought and action, as long as it does not conflict with Theosophical principles or harm the Theosophical Society.

**ENQUIRER.** But surely the T.S. does not remain entirely detached <note>original: "aloof"</note> from the social questions that are now so rapidly becoming prominent?

**THEOSOPHIST.** The core principles of the T.S. prove that it does not—or rather, that most of its members do not—stay detached. If humanity can only be developed mentally and spiritually by first applying the soundest and most scientific laws of physical health <note>original: "physiological laws"</note>, then it is the mandatory duty of everyone striving for this development to do their best to ensure those laws are generally put into practice. All Theosophists are sadly aware that, especially in Western <note>original: "Occidental"</note> countries, the social conditions of large masses of people make it impossible for either their bodies or their spirits to be properly trained.

so that the development of both is thereby hindered <note>original: "arrested"</note>. Since this training and development is one of the express objects of Theosophy, the T.S. (Theosophical Society) is in thorough sympathy and harmony with all true efforts in this direction.

**ENQUIRER.** But what do you mean by “true efforts”? Every social reformer has his own cure-all <note>original: "panacea"</note>, and each believes theirs to be the one and only thing which can improve and save humanity.

**THEOSOPHIST.** That is perfectly true, and this is the real reason why so little satisfactory social work is accomplished. In most of these cure-alls, there is no real guiding principle, and there is certainly no single principle which connects them all. Valuable time and energy are thus wasted; for men, instead of cooperating, strive against one another—often, it is feared, for the sake of fame and reward rather than for the great cause which they profess to have at heart, and which should be supreme in their lives.

**ENQUIRER.** How, then, should Theosophical principles be applied so that social cooperation may be promoted and true efforts for social improvement <note>original: "amelioration"</note> be carried on?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Let me briefly remind you what these principles are—universal Unity and Causation; Human Solidarity; the Law of <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect; the idea that every action has a corresponding consequence.</note>; and <term>Reincarnation</term> <note>The rebirth of a soul in a new body.</note>. These are the four links of the golden chain which should bind humanity into one family, one universal Brotherhood.

**ENQUIRER.** How?

**THEOSOPHIST.** In the present state of society, especially in so-called civilized countries, we are continually brought face to face with the fact that large numbers of people are suffering from misery,

poverty and disease. Their physical condition is wretched, and their mental and spiritual abilities <note>original: "faculties"</note> are often almost dormant. Neither of these ways of living is a matter of mere chance. Both are the effects of the conditions that surround those who are subject to them; the neglect of social duty on one side is closely connected with the stunted and halted development on the other. 

In sociology, as in all branches of true science, the law of <term>universal causation</term> <note>The principle that every event has a cause, and every cause has a specific effect.</note> holds true. But this cause and effect necessarily implies, as its logical result, that human solidarity <note>Unity and mutual support within the human family.</note> on which Theosophy so strongly insists. If the action of one person reacts on the lives of all—and this is the true scientific idea—then it is only by all men becoming brothers and all women sisters, and by everyone practicing true brotherhood and sisterhood in their daily lives, that the real human solidarity which lies at the root of the improvement of the human race <note>original: "elevation of the race"</note> can ever be achieved. It is this action and interaction—this true brotherhood and sisterhood in which each shall live for all and all for each—which is one of the fundamental Theosophical principles that every Theosophist should be obligated, not only to teach, but to carry out in his or her individual life.

Every Theosophist, therefore, is obligated to do his or her utmost to help, by all means possible, every wise and well-considered social effort that aims to improve <note>original: "amelioration"</note> the condition of the poor. **ENQUIRER.** But why should he do this? Aren’t he and everyone else, as you teach, conditioned by their Karma <note>The law of cause and effect; the sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences.</note>, and mustn't Karma necessarily work itself out along certain lines?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It is this very law of Karma that gives weight to everything I have said. The individual cannot be separated from the human race, nor the race from the individual. The law of Karma applies equally to everyone, even though not everyone is at the same stage of development. By helping others develop, the Theosophist believes he is not only helping them fulfill their Karma, but that he is also, in the literal sense, fulfilling his own. He always keeps the development of humanity in mind—of which both he and they are essential parts. He knows that any failure on his part to respond to the highest potential within him holds back <note>original: "retards"</note> not only himself but everyone else in their shared progress. Through his actions, he can make it either harder or easier for humanity to reach the next higher level <note>original: "plane"</note> of existence.

<term>THEOSOPHIST</term>. The connection is very close. If our present lives depend upon the development of certain principles which are a growth from the seeds <note>original: "germs"; referring to the spiritual or karmic origins of past actions</note> left by a previous existence, the same law holds true regarding the future. Once you grasp the idea that universal cause and effect <note>original: "causation"</note> is not merely present, but past, present, and future, then every action on our current level of existence <note>original: "plane"</note> falls naturally and easily into its true place. It is then seen in its true relation to ourselves and to others. Every mean and selfish action sends us backward rather than forward, while every noble thought and every unselfish deed are stepping-stones to the higher and more glorious levels <note>original: "planes"</note> of being. If this life were all there is, then in many respects it would indeed be poor and insignificant; but when regarded as a preparation for the next sphere of existence, it can be used as the golden gate through which we may pass—not selfishly and alone, but in the company of our fellow human beings—to the palaces that lie beyond.

### *ON SELF-SACRIFICE.*

<term>ENQUIRER</term>. Is equal justice to all and love for every creature the highest standard of Theosophy?

<term>THEOSOPHIST</term>. No; there is one that is even higher.

<term>ENQUIRER</term>. What could it be?

<term>THEOSOPHIST</term>. Giving to others *more than to oneself—self-sacrifice.* Such was the standard and overflowing <note>original: "abounding"</note> measure which so notably characterized the greatest Teachers and Masters of Humanity—for example <note>original: "e.g."</note>, Gautama Buddha in history, and Jesus of Nazareth as portrayed in the Gospels. This trait alone was enough to

<page-num>238</page-num>

-> # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

secure for them the perpetual reverence and gratitude of the generations of people who come after them. We say, however, that self-sacrifice must be performed with discrimination <note>Discrimination here means wise judgment or the ability to distinguish what is truly helpful from what is not.</note>; such self-abandonment, if made without justice or blindly, regardless of the eventual results, may often prove to be not only in vain, but actually harmful. One of the fundamental rules of Theosophy is justice to oneself—viewed as a unit of collective humanity, not as a personal self. This means giving oneself justice, no more but no less than what is given to others; unless, indeed, by the sacrifice of the *one* self we can benefit the many.

**ENQUIRER.** Could you make your idea clearer by giving an instance?

**THEOSOPHIST.** There are many instances in history to illustrate it. Theosophy holds that self-sacrifice for a practical good—to save many, or even several people—is far higher than self-denial <note>original: "self-abnegation"</note> for a sectarian idea, such as the goal of "saving the heathen from damnation," for instance. In our opinion, Father Damien <note>Joseph de Veuster (1840–1889), a Belgian priest who devoted his life to serving people with leprosy on the island of Molokai.</note>, the young man of thirty who offered his whole life in sacrifice for the benefit and relief of the sufferings of the lepers at Molokai, and who went to live for eighteen years alone with them—only to finally catch the loathsome disease and die—has not died in vain. He has given relief and relative happiness to thousands of miserable, suffering people. He brought them mental and physical consolation. He threw a streak of light into the black and dreary night of an existence whose hopelessness is unparalleled in the records of human suffering. He was a *true Theosophist*, and his memory will live forever in our annals. In our sight, this poor Belgian priest stands immeasurably higher than—for instance—all those sincere but

...conceited <note>original: "vain-glorious"</note> fools, the missionaries who have sacrificed their lives in the South Sea Islands or China. What good have they done? In the first case, they went to those who are not yet ready for any higher truth; in the second, they went to a nation whose systems of religious philosophy are as grand as any, if only the people who follow them would live up to the standards of Confucius and their other sages. Instead, these missionaries died as victims of unaccountable <note>original: "irresponsible"</note> cannibals and savages, or of popular fanaticism and hatred. 

However, by going to the slums of Whitechapel <note>An area in East London that was notorious in the late 19th century for extreme poverty and squalor.</note> or other similar locations—places that stagnate right under the blazing sun of our civilization, full of "Christian savages" and mental leprosy—they might have done real good. They could have preserved their lives for a better and worthier cause.

**ENQUIRER.** But Christians do not think so?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Of course not, because they act on an erroneous belief. They think that by baptizing the body of an unaccountable savage, they save his soul from damnation. One church forgets her martyrs, while the other beatifies and raises statues to such men as Labre <note>original: "Labro"; referring to Saint Benedict Joseph Labre (1748–1783), a Catholic saint who lived as a beggar in extreme filth as a form of religious penance.</note>, who sacrificed his body for forty years only to benefit the vermin that it bred. Had we the means to do so, we would raise a statue to Father Damien, the true, practical saint. We would perpetuate his memory forever as a living example of Theosophical heroism and of Buddha- and Christ-like mercy and self-sacrifice.

**ENQUIRER.** Then you regard self-sacrifice as a duty?

**THEOSOPHIST.** We do; and we explain it by showing that altruism is an integral part of self-development. But we have to use discernment <note>original: "discriminate"</note>. A man has no right to starve himself to death so that another...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on self-sacrifice, specifically addressing the balance between altruism for humanity and personal responsibilities to family.</meta>

<page-num>240</page-num>

-> # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

...A person might need food, but they should not take it unless that person's life is obviously more useful to the many than their own life is. However, it is a person's duty to sacrifice their own comfort and to work for others if those others are unable to work for themselves. It is a person's duty to give away everything that belongs solely to them and can benefit no one but themselves if they selfishly keep it from others. Theosophy teaches self-denial <note>original: "self-abnegation"</note>, but it does not teach reckless and useless self-sacrifice, nor does it justify fanaticism.

**ENQUIRER.** But how are we to reach such a high standard?

**THEOSOPHIST.** By applying our principles to our daily lives with insight. We do this by using our higher reasoning, spiritual intuition, and moral sense, and by following the commands of what we call "the still small voice" of our conscience. This voice is that of our Higher Self <note>original: "Ego"; in Theosophy, this refers to the permanent spiritual individuality that reincarnates, rather than the modern psychological ego.</note>. It speaks louder within us than the earthquakes and the thunders of Jehovah, in which "the Lord is not" <note>A reference to 1 Kings 19:11–12, where God is found not in the wind, earthquake, or fire, but in a "still small voice."</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** If those are our duties to humanity in general, what do you consider to be our duties to those in our immediate surroundings?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Exactly the same, plus the duties that arise from the special obligations of family ties.

**ENQUIRER.** Then it is not true, as some say, that as soon as a man joins the Theosophical Society, he begins to be gradually separated from his wife, children, and family duties?

**THEOSOPHIST.** That is a baseless lie <note>original: "groundless calumny"</note>, like so many others. The first duty of a Theosophist is to do their duty by *all* people, and especially by those to whom they have *specific* responsibilities. These are responsibilities we have either voluntarily undertaken, such as marriage vows, or those to which fate <note>original: "destiny"</note> has connected us—meaning the duties we owe to parents or next of kin.

<summary>The author clarifies that while Theosophy values self-denial, it rejects fanatical or useless sacrifice. It emphasizes that a member's primary duty is to fulfill their responsibilities toward family and those to whom they are specifically committed.</summary>

**ENQUIRER.** And what is the duty of a Theosophist to himself?

**THEOSOPHIST.** To control and conquer the *lower self* through the *Higher* self. To purify himself inwardly and morally; to fear no one and nothing, except for the judgment <note>original: "tribunal"</note> of his own conscience. Never to do things halfway; *that is*, if he thinks it is the right thing to do, let him do it openly and boldly, and if it is wrong, never touch it at all. It is the duty of a Theosophist to lighten his burden by thinking of the wise aphorism <note>A concise statement of a truth or principle.</note> of Epictetus <note>A Greek Stoic philosopher (c. 50–135 AD) who taught that peace of mind comes from focusing only on what is within our own power to control.</note>, who says: “*Do not be distracted from your duty by any idle thoughts the foolish world may have about you, for their criticisms are not within your power to control, and consequently should not be any part of your concern.*”

**ENQUIRER.** But suppose a member of your Society should plead an inability to practice altruism <note>The principle or practice of selfless concern for the well-being of others.</note> toward other people on the grounds that “charity begins at home.” Suppose he argues that he is too busy, or too poor, to benefit humanity or even any of its individuals—what are your rules in such a case?

**THEOSOPHIST.** No man has a right to say that he can do nothing for others, on any pretext whatever. “By doing the proper duty in the proper place, a man may make the world his debtor,” says an English writer. A cup of cold water given in time to a thirsty traveler <note>original: "wayfarer"</note> is a nobler duty and worth more than a dozen dinners given away at the wrong time <note>original: "out of season"</note> to men who can afford to pay for them. No man who does not have it in him will ever become a *Theosophist*; but he may remain a member of our Society all the same. We have no rules by which we could force any man to become a practical Theosophist if he does not desire to be one.

**ENQUIRER.** Then why does he enter the Society at all?

**THEOSOPHIST.** That is best known to the person who does so. For, here again,

-> # THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY. <-

We have no right to judge someone in advance, even if the entire community speaks against them, and I can tell you why. In our time, the "voice of the people" <note>original: *vox populi*</note> (at least regarding the voice of the educated) is no longer the "voice of God" <note>original: *vox dei*</note>. Instead, it is usually the voice of prejudice, of selfish motives, and often simply a reaction to someone's unpopularity. Our duty is to scatter seeds widely for the future and ensure they are good. We should not stop to ask *why* we should do so, or how or why we are "wasting" our time, since we ourselves will never be the ones to reap the harvest in the days to come.

### ON CHARITY.

**ENQUIRER.** How do you Theosophists view the Christian duty of charity?

**THEOSOPHIST.** What kind of charity do you mean? Charity of the mind, or practical charity on the physical level <note>original: "physical plane"</note>?

**ENQUIRER.** I mean practical charity, as your concept of Universal Brotherhood would naturally include charity of the mind.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Then you are thinking of the practical application of the commandments given by Jesus in the Sermon on the Mount? <note>A collection of sayings and teachings of Jesus found in the Gospel of Matthew (chapters 5, 6, and 7) which emphasize moral and ethical behavior.</note>

**ENQUIRER.** Exactly.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Then why call them "Christian"? Because, although your Savior preached and practiced them, the very last thing modern Christians think of is actually carrying them out in their lives.

<summary>This page argues against following biased public opinion and introduces a discussion on charity, where the Theosophist critiques modern Christians for failing to practice the core ethical teachings of Jesus.</summary>

**ENQUIRER.** And yet, aren't there many people who spend their lives giving to charity?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Yes, they do so out of the excess of their massive fortunes. But show me a single Christian, even among the most philanthropic, who would give their cloak to a shivering and starving thief who just stole their coat? Or who would offer their right cheek to someone who hit them on the left, without even thinking of fighting back?

**ENQUIRER.** Ah, but you must remember that these teachings <note>original: "precepts"</note> are not meant to be taken literally. Times and circumstances have changed since the time of Christ. Besides, he spoke in parables <note>Simple stories used to illustrate a moral or spiritual lesson.</note>.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Then why don't your churches teach that the doctrine of eternal damnation and hellfire is to be understood as a *parable* as well? Why do some of your most popular preachers—while essentially allowing these ethical "parables" to be interpreted however you like—insist on the literal meaning of the fires of Hell and the physical torture of an "asbestos-like" soul? <note>A soul that, like the mineral asbestos, is imagined to be indestructible and able to endure fire without being consumed.</note> If one is a "parable," then the other is too. If hellfire is a literal truth, then Christ's commandments in the Sermon on the Mount <note>The collection of ethical teachings found in the Gospel of Matthew, chapters 5–7.</note> must be obeyed to the very letter. And I tell you that many people who do not believe in the divinity of Christ—such as Count Leo Tolstoy <note>The famous Russian novelist (1828–1910) who later in life became a Christian anarchist and pacifist, strictly following the Sermon on the Mount.</note> and many Theosophists—actually *do* practice these noble and universal teachings literally. Many more good men and women would do so as well, if they weren't absolutely certain that living such a life would likely land them in a mental asylum—that is how "Christian" your laws really are!

**ENQUIRER.** But surely everyone knows that millions upon millions are spent every year on private and public charities?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Oh, yes—and half of that money stays stuck to the hands of the people it passes through <note>This implies that administrative costs or corruption often drain the funds meant for the poor.</note>.

**ENQUIRER.** But how can this be applied practically?

**THEOSOPHIST.** The Theosophical ideas of charity mean *personal effort* <note>original: "exertion"</note> for others; *personal mercy and kindness*; *personal interest* in the welfare of those who suffer; *personal sympathy*, forethought, and assistance in their troubles or needs. We Theosophists do not believe in giving money (note <note>original: "N.B." (Latin: *nota bene*)</note>, if we even had it) through other people’s hands or organizations. We believe in giving to the money a thousandfold greater power and effectiveness by our personal contact and sympathy with those who need it. We believe in relieving the starvation of the soul as much as, if not more than, the emptiness of the stomach; for gratitude

<summary>The Theosophist argues against institutionalized charity, claiming it often helps the wrong people and inflates costs for the poor, advocating instead for personal, direct involvement and Buddhist-inspired individual mercy.</summary>

Do your helpless old men and women thank you for the workhouses? Or do your poor thank you for the poisonously unhealthy dwellings where they are allowed to breed new generations of diseased, malnourished <note>original: "scrofulous and rickety" — medical terms for diseases caused by poor sanitation and Vitamin D deficiency.</note>, and sickly children, only to put money into the pockets of the insatiable landlords <note>original: "Shylocks" — a reference to the greedy moneylender in Shakespeare's *The Merchant of Venice*.</note> who own the houses? Therefore, every pound <note>original: "sovereign"</note> of all those "millions" contributed by well-meaning and supposedly charitable people falls like a burning curse instead of a blessing on the poor whom it should relieve. We call this generating national *Karma*, and terrible will be its results on the day of reckoning.

### THEOSOPHY FOR THE MASSES.

**ENQUIRER.** And you think that Theosophy would, by intervening, help to remove these evils under the practical and difficult conditions of our modern life?

**THEOSOPHIST.** If we had more money, and if most Theosophists did not have to work for their daily bread, I firmly believe we could.

**ENQUIRER.** How? Do you expect that your doctrines could ever take hold...

Your mind is so full of intellectual subtleties and preconceived notions that your natural intuition and your ability to perceive the truth cannot function. One does not need a degree in metaphysics or a formal education to understand the broad truths of <term>Karma</term> and <term>Reincarnation</term>. Look at the millions of poor and uneducated <term>Buddhists</term> and <term>Hindus</term> <note>original: "Hindoos"</note>, to whom <term>Karma</term> and <term>reincarnation</term> are solid realities, simply because their minds have never been cramped and distorted by being forced into an unnatural way of thinking. Their innate human sense of justice has never been corrupted by being told to believe that their sins would be forgiven because another man was put to death for their sake <note>This is a reference to the Christian doctrine of substitutionary atonement, which the author argues undermines personal moral responsibility.</note>. 

And take note: the <term>Buddhists</term> live according to their beliefs without complaining about <term>Karma</term> or what they regard as a just punishment. In contrast, the Christian population neither lives up to its moral ideals nor accepts its lot in life contentedly. This leads to grumbling, dissatisfaction, and the intense struggle for existence seen in Western lands.

**Enquirer:** But this contentment, which you praise so much, would do away with all motivation for hard work and would bring progress to a standstill.

**Theosophist:** And we, <term>Theosophists</term>, say that your boasted progress and civilization are no better than a host of will-o'-the-wisps <note>A "will-o'-the-wisp" is a flickering light seen at night over bogs or marshes; metaphorically, it refers to a deceptive or misleading goal.</note> flickering over a marsh that breathes out a poisonous and deadly miasma <note>original: "miasma" — an archaic medical term for "bad air" or vapors once thought to cause disease; here used metaphorically for a corrupt atmosphere.</note>. We say this because we see selfishness, crime, and immorality...

It is only through philosophy that an intelligent and educated person can avoid the "intellectual suicide" of relying on blind faith. And it is only by absorbing the strict continuity and logical coherence of Eastern (if not <term>esoteric</term> <note>Esoteric refers to knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with a specialized interest.</note>) doctrines that one can realize their truth. Conviction breeds enthusiasm, and as Bulwer-Lytton <note>Edward Bulwer-Lytton (1803–1873) was a famous English novelist and politician.</note> says, "Enthusiasm is the genius of sincerity, and truth achieves no victories without it"; while Emerson <note>Ralph Waldo Emerson (1803–1882), the American philosopher and leader of the Transcendentalist movement.</note> most truly remarks that "every great and commanding movement in the annals of the world is the triumph of enthusiasm." And what is more likely to produce such a feeling than a philosophy so grand, so consistent, so logical, and so all-embracing as our Eastern Doctrines?

**Enquirer:** And yet its enemies are very numerous, and every day <term>Theosophy</term> gains new opponents.

The main goal is to uproot the most fertile source of all crime and immorality—the belief that it is possible for people to escape the consequences of their own actions. Once you teach them that greatest of all laws, **Karma** <note>The law of cause and effect, where a person's actions determine their future state.</note> and **Reincarnation**, they will not only feel the true dignity of human nature within themselves, but they will also turn from evil and avoid <note>original: "eschew"</note> it as they would a physical danger.

### HOW MEMBERS CAN HELP THE SOCIETY.

**Enquirer:** How do you expect the Fellows <note>Members of the Theosophical Society were formally called "Fellows."</note> of your Society to help in the work?

**Theosophist:** First, by studying and understanding the theosophical doctrines, so that they may teach others, especially young people. Secondly, by taking every opportunity to talk to others and explain to them what Theosophy is and what it is not; by removing misconceptions and spreading interest in the subject. Thirdly, by assisting in circulating our literature: by buying books when they have the means, by...

<page-num>249</page-num>

->**THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.**<-

...lending and giving books away, and by encouraging their friends to do the same. Fourthly, by defending the Society from unfair criticisms <note>original: "aspersions"</note> using every legitimate means <note>original: "device"</note> at their disposal. Fifth, and most important of all, by the example of their own lives.

**ENQUIRER:** But all this literature—which you emphasize spreading so much—does not seem to me to be of much practical use in helping mankind. This is not practical charity.

**THEOSOPHIST:** We think otherwise. We hold that a good book which gives people food for thought, which strengthens and clears their minds, and enables them to grasp truths which they have dimly felt but could not formulate—we hold that such a book does a real, substantial good. As for what you call practical acts of charity to help people’s physical needs, we do the little we can; but, as I have already told you, most of us are poor, and the Society itself does not even have the money to pay a staff of workers. All of us who work for it give our labor for free <note>original: "gratis"</note>, and in most cases, we give money as well. The few who have the means to perform what are usually considered charitable acts follow Buddhist teachings <note>original: "precepts"</note> and do the work personally, rather than through others <note>original: "by proxy"</note> or by donating publicly to charity funds. Above all, a Theosophist must learn to set aside their own ego <note>original: "forget his personality"</note>.

**ENQUIRER:** Do you have any laws or rules that forbid certain actions for Theosophists in your Society?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Many, but, alas! None of them are enforced. They express the ideal of our organization, but we are compelled to leave the practical application of such things to the discretion of the members <note>original: "Fellows"</note> themselves. Unfortunately, the mindset of people in the present century is such that, unless we allow these clauses to remain effectively inactive <note>original: "obsolete"</note>, no man or woman would dare to risk joining the Theosophical Society. This is precisely why I feel forced to emphasize the difference between true Theosophy and its hard-struggling and well-intentioned—but still imperfect <note>original: "unworthy"</note>—vehicle, the Theosophical Society.

**ENQUIRER:** May I be told what these perilous reefs are in the open sea of Theosophy?

**THEOSOPHIST:** You may well call them reefs, as more than one otherwise sincere and well-meaning <term>Fellow of the Theosophical Society (F.T.S.)</term> has had his Theosophical canoe shattered into splinters on them! And yet, avoiding certain things seems like the easiest thing in the world to do. For instance, here is a series of such "don'ts" <note>original: "negatives"</note>, which shield positive Theosophical duties:

> No Theosophist should remain silent when he hears evil reports or slanders spread about the Society, or innocent persons, whether they are his colleagues or outsiders.

**ENQUIRER:** But suppose what one hears is the truth, or might be true without one knowing it?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Then you must demand strong proof of the assertion and hear both sides impartially before you permit the accusation to go uncontradicted. You have no right to believe in evil,

**ENQUIRER:** And what should you do then?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Pity and forbearance, charity and patience <note>original: "long-suffering"</note>, ought to always be there to prompt us to excuse our sinning brothers <note>original: "brethren"</note> and to pass the gentlest sentence possible upon those who err. A Theosophist ought never to forget what is due to the shortcomings and weaknesses of human nature.

**ENQUIRER:** Should he forgive entirely in such cases?

**THEOSOPHIST:** In every case—especially the person who has been wronged.

**ENQUIRER:** But what if, by doing so, he risks causing injury or allowing others to be injured? What should he do then?

**THEOSOPHIST:** His duty; whatever his conscience and higher nature suggests to him, but only after mature deliberation. Justice consists of doing no injury to any living being; but justice also commands us never to allow injury to be done to the many—or even to one innocent person—by allowing a guilty person to go unchecked.

**ENQUIRER:** What are the other negative clauses <note>These are the "don'ts" or prohibitions mentioned on the previous page.</note>?

**THEOSOPHIST:** No Theosophist should be satisfied with an idle or frivolous life, doing no real good for himself and even less for others. He should work for the benefit of the few who need his help if he is unable to labor for all of humanity, and in this way work for the advancement of the Theosophical cause.

**ENQUIRER:** This requires an exceptional nature and would be quite difficult for some people.

**THEOSOPHIST:** Then they would be better off remaining outside the <term>T.S.</term> <note>The Theosophical Society</note> instead of—

**ENQUIRER:** What comes next?

**THEOSOPHIST:** No active member should value their personal progress or proficiency in Theosophical studies too highly; instead, they must be prepared to do as much selfless <note>original: "altruistic"</note> work as is within their power. They should not leave the entire heavy burden and responsibility of the Theosophical movement on the shoulders of only a few devoted workers. Each member ought to feel it is their duty to take whatever share they can in the collective work and help it by every means at their disposal.

**ENQUIRER:** That is only fair. What comes next?

**THEOSOPHIST:** No Theosophist should place their personal vanity or feelings above those of the Society as a whole. Anyone who sacrifices the interests of the Society—or other people’s reputations—on the altar of their personal vanity, worldly benefit, or pride, should not be allowed to remain a member. One diseased <note>original: "cancerous"</note> limb makes the whole body sick.

**ENQUIRER:** Is it the duty of every member to teach others and preach Theosophy?

**THEOSOPHIST:** It certainly is. No member <note>original: "fellow"</note> has a right to remain idle on the excuse that they know too little to teach. For they may always be sure that they will find others who know even less than themselves. Furthermore, it is not until a person begins trying to teach others that they discover their own ignorance and try to remove it. But this is a secondary point <note>original: "minor clause"</note>.

**ENQUIRER:** What do you consider, then, to be the most important of these negative <note>In this context, "negative duties" refers to things a member must *not* do, or prohibitions.</note> Theosophical duties?

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**THEOSOPHIST:** To be always prepared to recognize and admit your own faults. To prefer to make a mistake through too much praise rather than through too little appreciation of a neighbor's efforts. Never to talk behind someone's back <note>original: "backbite"</note> or slander another person. Always to speak openly and directly to someone's face if you have a grievance against them. Never to repeat rumors <note>original: "make yourself the echo of anything you may hear"</note> against another, nor hold onto a desire for revenge against those who happen to hurt you.

**ENQUIRER:** But it is often dangerous to tell people the truth to their faces, don’t you think? I know of one of your members who was deeply offended, left the Society, and became its greatest enemy, simply because he was told some unpleasant truths to his face and was criticized for them.

**THEOSOPHIST:** We have had many such cases. No member, whether prominent or insignificant, has ever left us without becoming our bitter enemy.

**ENQUIRER:** How do you explain that?

**THEOSOPHIST:** It is simply this: in most cases, having been intensely devoted to the Society at first and having heaped the most exaggerated praise upon it, the only excuse such a former member <note>original: "backslider"</note> can make for their later behavior and past lack of foresight is to pretend to be an innocent and deceived victim. In this way, they shift the blame from their own shoulders onto the Society in general, and its leaders especially. Such people remind one of the old fable about the man with a deformed face who broke his mirror <note>original: "looking-glass"</note> because it showed that his face <note>original: "countenance"</note> was crooked.

**ENQUIRER:** But what makes these people turn against the Society?

<summary>The Theosophist outlines the importance of self-criticism and honesty, explaining that former members often become bitter enemies of the Society to hide their own past errors or bruised egos.</summary>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on why members leave the Theosophical Society and turn against its leaders, attributing their hostility to wounded vanity and failed personal ambitions.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

**THEOSOPHIST:** Wounded vanity in one form or another is behind almost every case.

Generally, it is because their dictates <note>original: "dicta"</note> and advice are not accepted as final and authoritative; or else, because they are the type of people who would rather reign in Hell than serve in Heaven. In short, it is because they cannot bear to be second to anyone in anything. So, for example, one member—a self-important "Sir Oracle" <note>A reference to Shakespeare's *The Merchant of Venice*, describing someone who thinks their word is absolute law.</note>—criticized and nearly defamed every member in the Theosophical Society to outsiders as much as to fellow Theosophists. He did this under the pretense that they were all *untheosophical*, blaming them for the very things he was doing himself the entire time. Finally, he left the Society, claiming his reason was a deep conviction that we were all (the Founders especially)—FRAUDS! 

Another person, after scheming in every possible way to be placed at the head of a large section of the Society, found that the members would not have him. He then turned against the Founders of the Theosophical Society and became their bitterest enemy, denouncing one of them whenever he could, simply because the leader could not and would not force the members to accept him. This was simply a case of extreme wounded vanity. 

Still another wanted to, and essentially did, practice *black magic*—that is <note>original: "i.e."</note>, exerting improper psychological influence over certain members <note>original: "Fellows"</note>—while pretending to be devoted and to possess every Theosophical virtue. When this was stopped, the member broke ties with Theosophy. Now, this person slanders and lies about the same unfortunate <note>original: "hapless"</note> leaders in the most poisonous <note>original: "virulent"</note> manner, attempting to destroy the society by blackening the reputations of those whom that so-called "Member" <note>original: "Fellow"</note> was unable to deceive.

**ENQUIRER:** What would you do with characters like these?
