-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

...and refute once and for all the many charges made against Madame Blavatsky and the Society?

**THEOSOPHIST.** What charges?

**ENQUIRER.** The charge that the "Masters" do not exist and that she has invented them. That they are merely puppets—"Mahatmas made of muslin and bladders." <note>This refers to contemporary accusations that Blavatsky used crude props, like fabric and inflated bladders, to fake the appearance of spirits or Masters during séances.</note> Doesn't all of this damage her reputation?

**THEOSOPHIST.** In what way can such an accusation truly harm her? Did she ever make money from their supposed existence, or gain any personal benefit or fame from it? I would argue that she has gained only insults, abuse, and slanders <note>original: "calumnies"</note>—which would have been very painful if she hadn't learned long ago to be completely indifferent to such false charges. 

After all, what does it really mean? It amounts to an *implied compliment*. If her accusers weren't blinded by their own hatred, they would have thought twice before saying it. To say that she invented the Masters means this: she must have invented every single piece of philosophy ever published in Theosophical literature. She would have to be the actual author of the letters upon which *Esoteric Buddhism* <note>A book by A.P. Sinnett (1883) that first popularized Theosophical concepts in the West.</note> was based. She would be the sole creator of every principle found in *The Secret Doctrine*—a work that, if the world were fair, would be recognized as providing many of the "missing links" in science, as will likely be discovered a hundred years from now. 

By making these claims, they are also giving her credit for being far more clever than the hundreds of people—including many very intelligent and even scientific men—who believe what she says. If she invented it all, she must have fooled every one of them! If they are telling the truth, then she must be several Mahatmas rolled into one, like a set of <term>Chinese boxes</term> <note>original: "nest of Chinese boxes"; a set of boxes of graduated sizes, each fitting inside the next larger one, similar to Russian nesting dolls.</note>;

<summary>The author argues that if Madame Blavatsky had fabricated the "Masters," she would have to be a genius of unprecedented proportions to have authored the entire complex system of Theosophical philosophy herself.</summary>

-># THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

...since among the so-called “Mahatma letters,” there are many written in completely different and distinct styles. Her accusers claim that she wrote all of them herself.

**ENQUIRER.** That is exactly what they say. But isn't it very painful for her to be publicly condemned as “the most accomplished impostor of the age, whose name deserves to pass to future generations,” as was done in the report by the “Society for Psychical Research”? <note>The Society for Psychical Research (SPR) published a famous report in 1885, known as the Hodgson Report, which labeled Blavatsky a fraud and a Russian spy.</note>

**THEOSOPHIST.** It might be painful if it were true, or if it came from people who weren't so aggressively materialistic and biased. As it stands, she personally treats the whole matter with contempt, while the Mahatmas simply laugh at it. In truth, it is the greatest compliment that could be paid to her—I will say it again.

**ENQUIRER.** But her enemies claim to have proven their case.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Yes, it is easy enough to make such a claim when you have appointed yourself as judge, jury, and prosecutor all at once, as they did. But who believes it, except for their own followers and our enemies?

**ENQUIRER.** But they sent a representative to India to investigate the matter, didn’t they?

**THEOSOPHIST.** They did, and their final conclusion rests entirely on the unchecked statements and unverified claims of this young gentleman <note>Richard Hodgson (1855–1905), the Australian researcher sent by the SPR to India to investigate the "theosophical phenomena."</note>. A lawyer who read through his report told a friend of mine that in all his experience, he had never seen “such a ridiculous and self-contradictory <note>original: "self-condemnatory"</note> document.” It was found to be full of guesses and “working hypotheses” that canceled each other out. Is this really a serious charge?

**ENQUIRER.** Still, it has done the Society great harm. Why, then, did she not defend her own character in a court of law?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Firstly, because as a Theosophist, it is her duty to ignore all personal insults. Secondly, because neither the Society nor Madame Blavatsky had any money to waste on such a lawsuit. And lastly, because it would have been ridiculous for both to betray their principles just because of an attack made on them by a flock of stupid old British <term>wethers</term> <note>original: "wethers"; a wether is a castrated ram, used here metaphorically to describe the SPR members as gullible followers</note>, who had been led to butt at them by an overly frolicsome "lambkin" from Australia <note>A dismissive reference to Richard Hodgson, the Australian-born researcher who wrote the negative report for the SPR</note>.

**THEOSOPHIST.** Perhaps. But do you believe that any English jury or judge would have ever admitted the reality of psychic phenomena, even if they were entirely unprejudiced beforehand? And when you remember that they would have already been turned against us by the "Russian Spy" scare, the charges of ***Atheism*** and ***infidelity***, and all the other slanders that have been circulated against us, you cannot fail to see that such an attempt to obtain justice in a court of law would have been worse than useless! All of this the Psychic Researchers knew well, and they took a low and mean advantage of their position to elevate themselves while saving their own reputations at our expense.

**ENQUIRER.** The SPR now completely denies the existence of the Mahatmas. They say that from beginning to end, the Mahatmas were a fictional story that Madame Blavatsky wove from her own brain?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Well, she could have done many things less clever than that. At any rate, we do not have the slightest objection to this theory. As she always says now, she almost prefers that...

...stence of the Masters. Madame Blavatsky says she is actually glad that people do not believe in the Masters. Initially, she used to protest indignantly against any doubts regarding their existence. Now, she never goes out of her way to prove or disprove it. Let people think what they like.

**THEOSOPHIST.** We maintain that *they do*. Nevertheless, this does not help much. Many people, even some Theosophists and former Theosophists, say that they have never had any proof of their existence. Very well; Madame Blavatsky responds with this alternative: if she invented them, then she also invented their philosophy and the practical knowledge which a few people have acquired. If that is the case, what does it matter whether they exist or not, since she herself is here and her own existence, at any rate, can hardly be denied? If the knowledge supposedly shared by them is intrinsically good, and is accepted as such by many people of more than average intelligence, why should there be such an uproar made over that question? The claim that she is an impostor has never been proven and will always remain under judicial consideration <note>original: "*sub judice*"; a Latin legal term for a matter currently under trial or still being considered by a judge</note>. Thus the slanderers, while moved by the lowest...

**THEOSOPHIST.** The Masters do *not* guide the Society, nor even the Founders; and no one has ever asserted that they did. They only watch over and protect it. This is amply proven by the fact that no mistakes have been able to cripple it, and no scandals from within—nor the most damaging attacks from without—have been able to overthrow it. The Masters look at the future, not at the present, and every mistake provides more accumulated wisdom for days to come. That other "Master" <note>A reference to Jesus’ Parable of the Talents in the Bible (Matthew 25:14–30)</note> who sent the man with the five talents did not tell him how to double them, nor did he prevent the foolish servant from burying his one talent in the earth. Each person must acquire wisdom by his own experience and merits. The Christian Churches, who claim a far higher "Master"—the very Holy Ghost itself—have always been and are still guilty not only of "mistakes," but of a series of bloody crimes throughout the ages. Yet, I suppose no Christian would deny his belief in that "Master" because of those crimes? Even though his existence is far more...

### THE ABUSE OF SACRED NAMES AND TERMS.

**ENQUIRER.** Then, is what I have heard untrue—namely, that many of your Theosophical writers claim to have been inspired by these Masters, or to have seen and spoken with them?

**THEOSOPHIST.** It may or it may not be true. How can I tell? The burden of proof rests with those making the claims. A few of them—very few, indeed—have clearly either lied or suffered from hallucinations when boasting of such inspiration. Others, however, were truly inspired by great <term>Adepts</term> <note>original: "Adepts"; individuals who have attained mastery over their spiritual and physical nature</note>. A tree is known by its fruit; and just as all Theosophists must be judged by their deeds and not by what they write or say, so all Theosophical books must be accepted on their own merits, and not according to any claim of authority they might put forward.

**ENQUIRER.** But would Madame Blavatsky apply this rule to her own works—*The Secret Doctrine*, for instance?

**THEOSOPHIST.** Certainly. She states explicitly in her preface that she is sharing the doctrines she learned from the Masters, but she claims no personal inspiration for what she has recently written. As for our most respected Theosophists, they would also much prefer it if the names of the Masters had never been mixed up with our books in any way. With few exceptions, most of these works are not only imperfect, but contains clear errors and...

It highlights that Madame Blavatsky herself claimed no personal inspiration for her writing, merely that she was passing on teachings learned from her "Masters."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the "Masters" (highly evolved spiritual teachers). The Theosophist discusses how the public disclosure of these teachers' names has led to exploitation by fraudulent organizations and mediums.</meta>

...misleading. The names of two of the Masters have been greatly desecrated. There is hardly a medium who has not claimed to have seen them. Every fraudulent, swindling society organized for profit now claims to be guided and directed by "Masters," who are often supposedly much higher in rank than ours! Those who make these claims have committed many serious sins, driven either by a desire for profit <note>original: "lucre"</note>, vanity, or irresponsible mediumship. Many people have been robbed of their money by such societies, which offer to sell the secrets of power, knowledge, and spiritual truth for worthless gold. Worst of all, the sacred names of <term>Occultism</term> <note>original: "Occultism"; the study of hidden spiritual laws and the powers of the human soul</note> and their holy guardians have been dragged through the mud, polluted by being associated with greedy motives and immoral practices. Meanwhile, thousands of people have been kept away from the path of truth and light because of the discredit and bad reputation that such shams, swindles, and frauds have brought upon the entire subject. I say again, every sincere <term>Theosophist</term> <note>original: "Theosophist"; a member of the Theosophical Society or a student of its philosophy</note> deeply regrets today that these sacred names and matters were ever mentioned to the public, and strongly wishes they had been kept secret within a small circle of trusted and devoted friends.

**ENQUIRER.** These names certainly appear very frequently nowadays, and I do not remember ever hearing of people called "Masters" until quite recently.

**THEOSOPHIST.** That is true; and if we had acted on the wise principle of silence instead of rushing into the public eye and publishing everything we knew and heard, such desecration would never have happened. Look—only fourteen years ago, before the <term>Theosophical Society</term> <note>original: "Theosophical Society"; an organization founded in 1875 to promote universal brotherhood and study ancient wisdom</note>...

...was founded, all the talk was of "Spirits." They were everywhere, in everyone’s mouth; and no one by any chance even dreamed of talking about living "Adepts," "Mahatmas," or "Masters." One hardly even heard the name of the **Rosicrucians**, while the existence of such a thing as <term>Occultism</term> <note>original: "Occultism"; the study of hidden spiritual laws and the powers of the human soul</note> was suspected by only a very few. 

Now all that has changed. We <term>Theosophists</term> <note>original: "Theosophists"; members of the Theosophical Society who study universal truths</note> were, unfortunately, the first to talk of these things, to make known the fact that "Adepts," "Masters," and Occult knowledge exist in the East; and now these names have become common property. It is on us, now, that the <term>Karma</term> <note>original: "Karma"; the law of cause and effect, where actions result in inevitable consequences</note>—the consequences of the resulting desecration of holy names and things—has fallen. All that you now find about such matters in current literature—and there is quite a lot of it—can all be traced back to the impulse given in this direction by the Theosophical Society and its Founders. 

Our enemies profit to this day by our mistake. The most recent book directed against our teachings is alleged to have been written by *an Adept of twenty years’ standing*. In reality, it is a *palpable lie*. We know the <term>amanuensis</term> <note>original: "amanuensis"; a literary assistant or secretary who writes down what another dictates</note> and his inspirers (as he is himself too ignorant to have written anything of the sort). These "inspirers" are living persons, as revengeful and unscrupulous as they are intellectually powerful; and these bogus Adepts are not one, but several. The cycle of using "Adepts" as sledgehammers to break the Theosophical heads began twelve years ago, with Mrs. Emma Hardinge Britten’s "Louis" of *Art Magic* and *Ghost-Land*, and now ends with the "Adept" and "Author" of *The Light of Egypt*, a work written by <term>Spiritualists</term> <note>original: "Spiritualists"; those who believe that the spirits of the dead communicate with the living, usually through a medium</note> against Theosophy and...

**ENQUIRER:** Do you reject "Louis" as an <term>Adept</term> <note>original: "Adept"; a highly advanced being who has mastered secret spiritual and natural laws</note>?

**THEOSOPHIST:** We denounce no one, leaving this "noble" task to our enemies. The spiritualist author of *Art Magic*, etc., may or may not have been acquainted with such an Adept—and by saying this, I say far less than what that lady has said and written about us and Theosophy for the last several years—that is her own business. However, when in a solemn scene of mystical vision, a supposed "Adept" sees "spirits" presumably at Greenwich, England, through Lord Rosse's telescope—which was built in and never moved from Parsonstown, Ireland*—I may well be permitted to wonder at the ignorance of that "Adept" in matters of science. This surpasses all the mistakes and blunders committed at times by the <term>chelas</term> <note>original: "chelas"; disciples or students of a spiritual Master</note> of our Teachers! And it is this "Adept" that is used now to undermine the teachings of our Masters!

**ENQUIRER:** I quite understand your feeling in this matter, and think it only natural. And now, in view of all that you have said and explained to me, there is one subject on which I should like to ask you a few questions.

**THEOSOPHIST:** If I can answer them I will. What is that?

# CONCLUSION.

## *THE FUTURE OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.*

**ENQUIRER:** Tell me, what do you expect for Theosophy in the future?

**THEOSOPHIST:** If you are speaking of THEOSOPHY itself, I answer that just as it has existed eternally throughout the endless cycles of the past, so it will always exist throughout the infinite reaches of the future. This is because Theosophy is synonymous with EVERLASTING TRUTH.

**ENQUIRER:** Pardon me; I meant to ask instead about the prospects of the Theosophical Society.

**THEOSOPHIST:** Its future will depend almost entirely on the degree of selflessness, earnestness, and devotion of its members. Last, but not least, it will depend on the amount of knowledge and wisdom possessed by those members who will be responsible for carrying on the work and directing the Society after the death of the Founders <note>Founders: Primarily Helena Blavatsky and Henry Steel Olcott, who established the Society in 1875</note>.

**ENQUIRER:** I clearly see the importance of them being selfless and devoted, but I do not quite understand how their knowledge can be as vital a factor as those other qualities. Surely the literature that already exists—and to which new additions are still being made—should be sufficient?

**THEOSOPHIST:** I am not referring to technical knowledge of the <term>esoteric doctrine</term> <note>original: "esoteric doctrine"; the secret or inner teachings intended only for a small group of initiates</note>, although that is very important. I was speaking instead of the great...

<summary>The author distinguishes between the eternal nature of Theosophical truth and the organizational future of the Theosophical Society, which depends on the wisdom and character of future leaders.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophy, Theosophical Society, Founders, esoteric doctrine, selflessness, wisdom, future prospects</keywords>

->THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.<-

...need our successors in the leadership of the Society will have for unbiased and clear judgment. Every previous attempt like the Theosophical Society has ended in failure because, sooner or later, it has devolved into a sect, established rigid <term>dogmas</term> <note>original: "hard-and-fast dogmas"; fixed, unquestionable beliefs mandated by an authority</note> of its own, and so lost—by imperceptible degrees—the vitality that only living truth can provide. You must remember that all our members have been born and raised in some specific creed or religion, and that everyone is, to some extent, a product of their generation both physically and mentally. Consequently, their judgment is very likely to be warped and unconsciously biased by some or all of these influences. If they cannot be freed from such inherent bias—or at least taught to recognize it instantly and so avoid being led astray by it—the result can only be that the Society will drift onto some sandbank of thought or another, and there remain a stranded carcass to molder and die.

**ENQUIRER:** But if this danger is avoided?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Then the Society will live on into and through the twentieth century. It will gradually influence and permeate the vast majority of thinking and intelligent people with its broad-minded and noble ideas of religion, duty, and philanthropy. Slowly but surely, it will shatter the iron chains of creeds and dogmas, of social and caste prejudices; it will break down racial and national hostilities and barriers, and will open the way to the practical realization of the Brotherhood of all men. Through its teaching, and through the philosophy which it has made accessible and intelligible to the modern mind, the West will learn to understand and appreciate the East at its...

...true value. Furthermore, the development of psychic powers and abilities—the early signs of which are already visible in America—will progress in a healthy and normal way. Humanity will be saved from the terrible mental and physical dangers that are unavoidable when such an awakening occurs in an environment of selfishness and negative passions, as is currently the threat. Human growth, both mental and psychic, will progress in harmony with moral improvement. Meanwhile, material surroundings will reflect the peace and brotherly goodwill reigning in the mind, rather than the discord and conflict visible everywhere today.

**ENQUIRER:** A truly delightful picture! But tell me, do you really expect all this to be accomplished in just one short century?

**THEOSOPHIST:** Hardly. But I must tell you that during the last quarter of every century, an attempt is made by those "Masters" <note>In Theosophy, "Masters" or Mahatmas are believed to be highly evolved human beings who oversee the spiritual development of the race.</note> I mentioned to assist the spiritual progress of humanity in a significant and specific way. Toward the end of every hundred years, you will consistently find that a surge or awakening of spirituality—or call it <term>mysticism</term> <note>original: "mysticism"; the pursuit of achieving communion with or conscious awareness of an ultimate reality or spiritual truth</note> if you prefer—has occurred. One or more individuals have appeared in the world as their agents, and varying amounts of <term>occult</term> <note>original: "occult knowledge"; here referring to "hidden" or esoteric spiritual teachings that are not yet common knowledge</note> knowledge and teachings have been shared. If you wish, you can trace these movements back through the centuries as far as our detailed historical records go.

**ENQUIRER:** But how does this relate to the future of the Theosophical Society?

**THEOSOPHIST:** If the current effort, in the form of our Society, succeeds better than previous attempts, then it will exist as an organized, living, and healthy body when the time...

### THE KEY TO THEOSOPHY.

...comes for the work of the 20th century. The general condition of people’s minds and hearts will have been improved and purified by the spread of its teachings, and, as I have said, their prejudices and dogmatic illusions will have been, to some extent at least, removed. Not only that, but in addition to a large and accessible collection of literature readily available to everyone, the next spiritual impulse will find a numerous and united body of people ready to welcome the new messenger <note>original: "torch-bearer"</note> of Truth. He will find people's minds prepared for his message, a language already developed for him to express the new truths he brings, and an organization awaiting his arrival that will remove the purely practical and physical obstacles and difficulties from his path. 

Think how much someone who is given such an opportunity could accomplish. Measure it by comparison with what the Theosophical Society has actually *achieved* in the last fourteen years, without *any* of these advantages and surrounded by a multitude of hindrances that would not hamper the new leader. Consider all of this, and then tell me whether I am too optimistic <note>original: "sanguine"</note> when I say that if the Theosophical Society survives and remains true to its mission and its original purpose through the next hundred years—tell me, I say, if I go too far in asserting that earth will be a heaven in the 21st century compared to what it is now!

<meta>This page serves as an appendix to the main text, providing organizational details and the founding principles of the Theosophical Society as of 1889.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

## THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

### INFORMATION FOR ENQUIRERS.

The Theosophical Society was formed in New York on November 17, 1875. Its founders believed that the best interests of religion and science would be promoted by the revival of Sanskrit, Pali, <term>Zend</term> <note>Zend refers to the Avestan language used in the sacred texts of Zoroastrianism.</note>, and other ancient literature. In these works, sages and initiates <note>original: "Initiates." This refers to those who have been admitted into secret spiritual knowledge.</note> had preserved truths of the highest value regarding humanity and nature for the use of all people. A society of an absolutely non-sectarian character, whose work would be conducted in a friendly manner by the learned people of all races—in a spirit of unselfish devotion to the search for truth and with the purpose of sharing it impartially—seemed likely to do much to counter materialism and strengthen the fading religious spirit. The simplest expression of the Society's objects is as follows:

*First.*—To form the foundation <note>original: "nucleus"</note> of a Universal Brotherhood of Humanity, without distinction of race, creed, sex, caste, or color.

*Second.*—To promote the study of <note>original: "Aryan." In the 19th century, this term was used scientifically to refer to the Indo-Iranian and Indo-European language families and their ancient cultures.</note> Aryan and other Eastern literatures, religions, and sciences.

*Third.*—A third object—pursued by only a portion of the Society's members—is to investigate unexplained laws of nature and the psychic powers of humanity.

No person’s religious opinions are asked when they join, nor is interference with those opinions permitted. However, everyone is required, before admission, to promise to show their fellow members the same tolerance in this respect as they claim for themselves.

The headquarters, offices, and managing staff are at Adyar, a suburb of Madras <note>Now known as Chennai, India.</note>, where the Society owns a property of twenty-seven acres and extensive buildings. These include a building for the Oriental Library and a spacious hall where the General Council meets annually in convention on December 27.

The Society does not yet have a permanent endowment, but there is the start of a fund; the income from its investment goes toward paying current expenses. These have previously been met by entrance fees, donations, and a small annual subscription from each member. However, according to the Revised Rules of 1889, the Society has been placed on a basis of voluntary contributions. It is therefore entirely dependent for its maintenance on the generosity of its members <note>original: "Fellows"</note> and others, as entrance fees and annual dues have been abolished. No salaries are paid; all work is done by volunteers, who receive simple food and necessary clothing when their private circumstances require such support.

The official trustee for all Society property is the current President. Legacies and bequests *should always be made in his name*, using the legal language of the country where the person making the will <note>original: "testator"</note> signs <note>original: "executes"</note> it. If left to the Society by name, the bequest becomes legally invalid. The President's full address is Henry Steel Olcott, Adyar, Madras, India.

The Society, as a body, avoids politics and all subjects outside its declared sphere of work. The rules strictly forbid members from compromising its strict neutrality in these matters.

Many branches of the Society have been formed in various parts of the world, and new ones are constantly being organized. Each branch creates its own bylaws and manages its own local business without interference from headquarters, provided that the fundamental rules of the Society are not violated. Branches located within certain borders (for example, America, the British Islands, Ceylon, etc.) have been grouped into territorial sections for administrative purposes. For details, see the Revised Rules of 1889, where all necessary information regarding joining the Society and other matters can also be found.

Up to the present date (1889), 173 branches of the Society have been founded. For details, see the Rules, etc., of the Theosophical Society, which can be obtained by applying to the Recording Secretary of the Theosophical Society, Adyar, Madras, or to the General Secretaries of the sections:

In England: Dr. A. Keightley, 7, Duke Street, Adelphi, London.
In America: William Q. Judge, P. O. Box 2659, New York.

<summary>This appendix outlines the history, three primary objectives, and organizational structure of the Theosophical Society as of 1889, emphasizing its non-sectarian nature and reliance on voluntary support.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophical Society, Universal Brotherhood, Adyar, Henry Steel Olcott, William Q. Judge, 1889 Rules, Eastern literature, psychic powers, non-sectarianism</keywords>

<page-num>309</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>
<sig>X</sig>

# THE LEGAL STATUS OF THE THEOSOPHICAL SOCIETY.

*The following official report, which granted a decree of incorporation to the St. Louis Theosophical Society, is an important document. It records the legal view of the Theosophical Society—based on a careful examination of witnesses under oath—taken by an American court of law.*

First—The applicant <note>original: "petitioner"</note> is not a religious body. I am reporting this negative finding because the word “Theosophical” in the applicant's name implies a possible religious connection. I assume the legal phrase “society formed for religious purposes” applies only to an organization formed, at least in part, for worship—worship being an individual act involving adoration and perhaps emotional power—or to an organization formed to spread a religious faith. Simply teaching a religion as one might teach algebra is not, in my view, a religious work as the word “religious” is used in the Law <note>original: "Statute"</note> and the Constitution. A person may hold a university position <note>original: "collegiate chair"</note> as a Professor of Religions and teach the principles of many faiths. Since these various religions are different and often conflicting <note>original: "variant and antagonistic"</note>, the professor could not possibly worship according to all of them. In fact, he might even be irreligious. Therefore, merely teaching religions is not a religious work in the legal sense. It should be noted that in Article 2 of this society’s constitution, the word "religion" is used in the plural. To teach religions is educational, not religious. “To promote the study of religions” is, in part, to promote the study of human history. I include the additional finding that the society has no religious creed and practices no worship. 

Second—The applicant proposes to promote the study of literature and the sciences. These goals fall explicitly within the terms of the law. 

Third—Related <note>original: "Cognate"</note> to the previous goal is the investigation of “unexplained laws of nature and the psychic <note>original: "psychical"</note> powers latent in humanity.” These two phrases, when taken at face value, are unobjectionable. However, there is reason to believe they carry a meaning other than the obvious one. The court will take note of the commonly accepted meaning of the word “Theosophy.” Although I am not personally familiar with Theosophy, it is generally understood to include, among other things, physical and psychic manifestations and phenomena that violate the laws currently known by physicists and philosophers <note>original: "metaphysicians"</note>, and perhaps are not even claimed to be explained or understood by Theosophists themselves. This group of phenomena may include Spiritualism <note>The belief that the spirits of the dead can communicate with the living.</note>, mesmerism <note>An early form of hypnosis named after Franz Mesmer.</note>, clairvoyance, mind-healing, mind-reading, and similar practices. I took testimony on this matter and found that while a belief in any of these types of manifestations is not required, and while each member of the society is free to hold their own opinion, these subjects are topics of inquiry and discussion. As a whole, the members are likely individual believers in abnormal phenomena and in powers that

<summary>This page contains a legal report regarding the incorporation of the St. Louis Theosophical Society, concluding that the organization is educational rather than religious because it studies religions objectively without practicing worship.</summary>
<keywords>Theosophical Society, St. Louis, legal status, incorporation, religious vs. educational, Spiritualism, mesmerism, psychic powers</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal report from August W. Alexander, acting as a "friend of the court," regarding the incorporation of the St. Louis Theosophical Society. The text concludes his argument that the society's interest in "occultism" is a respectable intellectual pursuit rather than a fraudulent enterprise.</meta>

<page-num>310</page-num>
<header>APPENDIX.</header>

...are superhuman as far as science currently understands. It is certainly the right of any citizen to hold whatever opinions they wish on these subjects, and to try as they please to investigate what is unexplained and reveal what is hidden <note>original: "display the latent"</note>. 

But the question here is: Should the Court grant a charter <note>original: "franchise"</note> to support such an effort? "Voodooism" is a term used for the practices of deceptive men who take advantage of the ignorant and superstitious. No Court would grant a legal status to promote such practices. Therefore, the Court must examine the practices and perhaps the reputation of the organization seeking judicial help. 

I do not mean to compare voodooism with this group of phenomena which, for convenience (though I do not know if it is strictly accurate), I will call "occultism." I only use voodooism as an extreme example to show why the Court ought to investigate. If we examine occultism, we find it has occasionally been used for fraud, according to reports. But this does not define its true character. Bad people will always use anything for selfish purposes. 

The goal of this society, whether it can be achieved or not, is clearly praiseworthy—assuming that there are unexplained physical and psychological <note>original: "psychical"</note> phenomena and that Theosophy seeks to explain them. Assuming there are human powers still hidden <note>original: "latent"</note>, it seeks to discover them. It may be that some absurdities and frauds are naturally part of the early stages of its development. 

Regarding a field like occultism—which claims powers usually thought of as superhuman and phenomena usually thought of as supernatural—it seemed to me that the Court should not try to legally decide if these things are true. Instead, before granting occultism a legal charter, the Court should ask whether the movement has gained a respectable reputation, or if its followers are merely people of low intelligence and excessive gullibility <note>original: "omnivorous credulity"</note>. 

I therefore took testimony on that point and found that a number of scientists in different European countries and in the United States believe in occultism. Sir Edward Bulwer Lytton <note>a famous 19th-century novelist and politician</note>, a writer of vast learning and strong intellect, is claimed to have been an occultist—a claim supported by at least two of his books. The late President Wayland of Brown University, writing about unusual mental functions like clairvoyance <note>the supposed power of seeing things beyond normal sensory contact</note>, says: "The subject seems to me well worth a thorough and honest examination. It does not deserve ridicule, but requires the most serious philosophical inquiry." 

Sir William Hamilton—likely the most sharp-minded and certainly the most learned English philosopher <note>original: "metaphysician"</note> who ever lived—said at least thirty years ago: "However astonishing it may be, it is now proven beyond all reasonable doubt that in certain unusual states of the nervous system, perception is possible through channels other than the ordinary senses." 

Because of this testimony, Theosophy is at least established as a respectable pursuit. Whether it can turn partial truths into complete truths through further work, or whether it can remove exaggerations and cleanse itself of any flaws, are likely questions the Court does not need to decide. I see no other part of the applicants' constitution that is legally objectionable. Therefore, I have the honor to report that I see no reason why the applicants' request should not be granted.

->AUGUST W. ALEXANDER,<-
->Friend of the Court <note>original: "Amicus Curiæ"</note>.<-

---

->ALLEN, SCOTT, AND CO., PRINTERS, 30, BOUVERIE STREET, LONDON, E.C.<-

The author distinguishes the society's goals from fraudulent practices like "voodooism," noting that even if the phenomena are currently unexplained, the pursuit of knowledge is legally valid.</summary>

# THE SECRET DOCTRINE,

->BY<-
### H. P. BLAVATSKY,
->AUTHOR OF "ISIS UNVEILED."<-

Volume I contains a general outline of the <term>Esoteric Doctrine</term> <note>The "inner" or hidden teachings of a spiritual or philosophical system, as opposed to "exoteric" or public teachings.</note> regarding the formation of the Solar System. This is preceded by a statement of the fundamental philosophical concepts that form the basis of the entire system.

The mysteries of religious and <term>Occult Symbology</term> <note>The study of hidden symbols and their meanings within mystical traditions.</note> are treated at great length in a section of the work dedicated to that subject. Additionally, the relationship between modern science and the "Secret Doctrine" is discussed with great care.

Volume II, which deals with the Esoteric Doctrine regarding the evolution of humanity on this earth, is now ready.

This volume contains an account of the various origins of humankind. It provides an outline of human evolutionary history down to the end of the race that immediately preceded our own. The history concludes with an ancient <note>original: "archaic"</note> version of the events described in the Biblical account of the Exodus of the Israelites.

# THE BUDDHIST.

## THE ENGLISH PUBLICATION OF THE SOUTHERN BRANCH OF BUDDHISM
<note>original: "Organ of the Southern Church"; "Southern Church" refers to the Theravada tradition of Buddhism practiced primarily in Sri Lanka, Myanmar, and Thailand.</note>

->Edited by C. W. LEADBEATER.<-
<note>Charles Webster Leadbeater (1854–1934) was a prominent member of the Theosophical Society and a prolific writer on occult and esoteric topics.</note>

"THE BUDDHIST" is a weekly paper, published in English at Colombo, Ceylon <note>modern-day Sri Lanka</note>, and devoted to the interests of the Buddhist religion. Among its contributors are the High Priest Sumangala <note>Hikkaduwe Sri Sumangala Thera (1827–1911) was a world-renowned scholar and a key figure in the 19th-century Buddhist revival in Sri Lanka.</note> (one of the greatest living scholars in Pali and Sanskrit, and the most prominent leader of the Southern branch), Sir Edwin Arnold, Mr. A. P. Sinnett, Madame H. P. Blavatsky, Colonel H. S. Olcott, Dr. Franz Hartmann, and other great writers on these specialized subjects.

->The Subscription is 3 Rupees per year <note>original: "per annum"</note> in Ceylon or India, and 10 shillings per year or its equivalent for other countries.<-
